Polymorphic Records for Dynamic Languages DOI: 10.1145/3720497 Journal: PACMPL Volume: 9 OOPSLA1 132 4 CCS: Theory of computation Type theory CCS: Software and its engineering Polymorphism
Giuseppe Castagna, Loïc Peyrot

Abstract
. We study row polymorphism for records types in systems with set-theoretic types, specifically, union, intersection, and negation types. We consider record types that embed row variables and define a subtyping relation by interpreting record types into sets of record values, and row variables into sets of rows , that is, “chunks” of record values where some record keys are left out: subtyping is then containment of the interpretations. We define a λ-calculus equipped with operations for field extension, selection, and deletion, its operational semantics, and a type system that we prove to be sound. We provide algorithms for deciding the typing and subtyping relations, and to decide whether two types can be instantiated to make one subtype of the other. This research is motivated by the current trend of defining static type systems for dynamic languages and, in our case, by an ongoing effort of endowing the Elixir programming language with a gradual type system.



†
†
cc-license:
by

1.
Introduction

The goal of this work is to define and study
row polymorphism
for a type system with
set-theoretic types, in particular, union, intersection, and negation types.
Row polymorphism was originally introduced by
wand87
;
wand91
;
remy89
;
remy94
and
further studied and developed in several works
(e.g.,
(
gaster96
;
leijen05
;
morris19
;
tang23
)
). However, as we argue
in this work, the theories in the current literature—which use
algebraic approaches—cannot
be reused for systems with set-theoretic types
and, hence, an original theory must be developed.
More broadly, the primary conceptual contribution of our work is
demonstrating that row polymorphism is not limited to syntactic or
algebraic approaches. Instead, it can be effectively integrated into
the semantic subtyping framework using the same three conceptual steps
employed to incorporate parametric polymorphism into the
framework
(
castagna14
;
castagna15
)
.

The interest of developing this new theory is grounded in
practice. There is a growing effort to develop type systems for
dynamic languages: TypeScript
(
typescript
)
and Flow
(
facebookflow
)
are two major examples of such
an effort and, as most of the existing attempts to add type systems to
dynamic languages, they include union and
intersection types to accommodate the flexible programming patterns
prevalent in such languages.
In particular, some widely used dynamic programming languages—such as
Luau
(
luau
;
luausemsub
)
, and
elixir
—have embraced set-theoretic
types as defined in the
semantic subtyping
framework by
frisch08
.
There,
(i)
types are interpreted as sets of
values,
(ii)
unions, intersections, and negation types are
interpreted as the corresponding set-theoretic interpretations, and

(iii)
the subtyping between two types is defined as set-containment
of their interpretations. Since semantic subtyping is the
framework that we use here, our work can be considered
as a study on adding row polymorphism to Luau and
Elixir. In particular, the rest of this section uses Elixir’s syntax to showcase
practical motivations.

For space reasons, several examples, definitions, and all proofs are omitted. They can be found in the appendixes available in the full version of this work and in the Auxiliary Material section of the ACM Digital Library entry for this work.

1.2.
The Need for Row Polymorphism

Before delving into the necessity of a new theory of row polymorphism for records, we might first question whether row polymorphism is needed at all. The system we intend to extend already incorporates first-order (
a.k.a.
prenex) polymorphism alongside set-theoretic types. This combination suffices to encode a limited form of bounded polymorphism
à la

Fun

(
CardelliWegner85
)
, which was employed to type polymorphic records.
Additionally,
castagna16
demonstrated that semantic subtyping with polymorphic types is enough to capture polymorphic variant types, which are usually typed by row polymorphism since, in these specifics cases, they are dual of polymorphic record types.

In terms of records, the combination of prenex polymorphism and set-theoretic
types can be used to type the following function without losing the static
information of the fields of the argument:

⬇

def

bump_counter
(
x
),

do
:

%{
x

|

counter
:

x
.
counter
+1}

This function increments the

field of its argument and
can be typed by bounded polymorphism by the type
$\forall(\alpha
≤
{..., counter

=

integer()}).
α
→
α
.Thetypevariable
α
{{{{{capturesthewholetypeoftheargument,thusalsoitsextrafields.Thankstothatthetypesystemdeducesfor\hbox{\pagecolor{snow}\hskip-2.36247pt\parbox[b][4.72495pt][b]{0.0pt}{\verb{}{(bump_counter(!\%!{counter: 1, file:
"foo.txt"})).file}{}{(bump_counter(!\%!{counter: 1, file:
"foo.txt"})).file}\hskip-2.36247pt} the type string.

instantiated by a record type that contains all the fields of the input and of
the output, in particular the field for \elix:domain}.If,asabove,\hbox{\pagecolor{snow}\hskip-2.36247pt\parbox[b][4.72495pt][b]{0.0pt}{\verb{}{:domain}{}{:domain}\hskip-2.36247pt} has different types in the
input and in the output, then one of the two intersections results
empty (making the whole record type containing it to be empty) which, as explained above, is unsound.
For the typing to work, we need the type
variable to instantiate all the fields of the input \emphexceptthefieldfor\hbox{\pagecolor{snow}\hskip-2.36247pt\parbox[b][4.72495pt][b]{0.0pt}{\verb{}{:domain}{}{:domain}\hskip-2.36247pt}}. This is exactly the role of row variables,
which instantiate ``all the other fields'' of the record type. The
function \elixput_{d}omain}canbegivenanyofthetwofollowingtypes(oneforeachline):{\@@listings@block{8}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@frame\small\@lst@startline{}!\textbackslash{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{textcolor}}}\textbraceleft{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{cadmiumgreen}}}\textbraceright\textbraceleft\textbackslash{\@listingGroup{}{$}!!{\color[rgb]{0,0.42,0.24}\ $}}\textbraceright!{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{}}\%\textbraceleft{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{f}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{}}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{domain}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{}}=\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{term}}}()\textbraceright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{}}-\textgreater{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{}}\%\textbraceleft{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{f}}},{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{domain}}}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{list}}}({\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{atom}}}())\textbraceright{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{when}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{f}}}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{fields}}}(){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{}}!\textbackslash{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{phantomsection}}}\textbackslash{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{label}}}\textbraceleft{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{twenty}}}\textbraceright!\@lst@endline}}{}}}Thetwotypesaboveareessentiallytwodifferentsyntaxesforthesametype:thetypeinline\penalty\ \ref{nineteen}issyntacticsugarfortheoneinline\penalty\ \ref{twenty},which,despitebeingmoreverbose,explicitlystatesinitsinputpart,thatthefield\hbox{\pagecolor{snow}\hskip-2.36247pt\parbox[b][4.72495pt][b]{0.0pt}{\verb{}{:domain}{}{:domain}\hskip-2.36247pt} is either undefined or contains a value of any type. The type in line\penalty\ \ref{twenty}, thus, explicitly shows that the row variable \hbox{\pagecolor{snow}\hskip-2.36247pt\parbox[b][4.72495pt][b]{0.0pt}{\verb{}{f}{}{f}\hskip-2.36247pt} captures all fields except \hbox{\pagecolor{snow}\hskip-2.36247pt\parbox[b][4.72495pt][b]{0.0pt}{\verb{}{:domain}{}{:domain}\hskip-2.36247pt}.
\par Likewise, we need row variables for typing a
function that deletes, say, the \elix:domain} field since, once
again, the type of the deleted field will be different in the input
and in the output:
\beginminted}elixir}!{\color[rgb]{0,0.42,0.24}\$}!defdel_{d}omain(x),do:Map.delete(x,:domain)\vskip-1.0ptThecodomainofthetypeinline\penalty\ \ref{twentyone}statesthatthefieldfor\hbox{\pagecolor{snow}\hskip-2.36247pt\parbox[b][4.72495pt][b]{0.0pt}{\verb{}{:domain}{}{:domain}\hskip-2.36247pt} must be absent (i.e., if present, it
must contain a value of the empty type \elixnone()},ofwhichthereisnone).Asbefore,thetypeabovecanbemoreconvenientlywrittenas:\hbox{\pagecolor{snow}\hskip-2.36247pt\parbox[b][4.72495pt][b]{0.0pt}{\verb{}{!\%!{f} -> !\%!{f, :domain => none()} when f: fields()}{}{!\%!{f} -> !\%!{f, :domain => none()} when f: fields()}\hskip-2.36247pt}, and again, this works because \elixf}canonlybeinstantiatedtorowsthatdonotcontainafieldfor\hbox{\pagecolor{snow}\hskip-2.36247pt\parbox[b][4.72495pt][b]{0.0pt}{\verb{}{:domain}{}{:domain}\hskip-2.36247pt}.
\par Since we have established that we need row polymorphism, the next question is
why not use the existing theory? A first reason for that is that to integrate row
polymorphism in a semantic subtyping
setting, we need to define subtyping for polymorphic records, and this
requires to interpret the row-polymorphic record types as sets of values which,
to our knowledge, was never done before.
% A second peculiarity of our setting are optional fields that, as far as we know,
% are not dealt with by the current approaches

thediscussiononpresencepolymorphismin\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde \par Even if the problem above can be solved by particular typing techniques,
the presence of negation types in our theory
invalidate such techniques in general (see example in
\crefsec:app-atomic}).Therefore,weneedtodevelopanoriginaltechniquetoreplaceunification,sothatittakesintoaccountsubtypingandenablessubstitutionstoexpandrowvariablesintoBooleancombinationsofrows.\par Inconclusion,toembedrowpolymorphisminatypesystemfeaturingsemanticallydefinedunion,intersection,andnegationtypes,weneednewtheoreticaldevelopmentstocopewiththesemanticinterpretationoftypesandtheinferenceofsubstitutionsfortypevariablesinthepresenceofsubtypingandset-theoretictypes.\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Overview}
\par In \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:types} we define the syntax of types and the subtyping relation. We abandon Elixir's syntax for maps/records and introduce in\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:typesyntax} a more
theoretically-oriented one: we denote records types as finite lists
of field-type specifications of the form $\ell\mathrel{\texttt{=}}\tau$, followed by
a \emph{tail} $\varsigma$ specifying an infinite row of fields as in
$\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell_{1}\mathrel{\texttt{=}}\tau_{1},...,\ell_{n}\mathrel{\texttt{=}}\tau_{n}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$. In this type,
each $\ell_{i}$ denotes a label (or key) of a field, and labels are pairwise distinct; $\tau_{i}$ is either a type
$t$ or the union $t\vee\bot$, meaning that the field is optional with
type $t$ (i.e., Elixir's \hbox{\pagecolor{snow}\hskip-2.36247pt\parbox[b][4.72495pt][b]{0.0pt}{\verb{}{:key => t()}{}{:key => t()}\hskip-2.36247pt} field) and absent if $t$
is the empty type (i.e., \elix:key => none()}); $\varsigma$ is either a row
variable $\rho$, or ``$\textbf{..}$'' (meaning that the record type is
open), or ``$\epsilon$'' (meaning that the record is closed: its values
contain all and only the fields specified in the type).
\par To define the subtyping relation on types, we give
in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:models} an interpretation
of types as sets of elements of a domain $\mathcal{D}$---whose elements,
intuitively, represent the values of the language---and then define
subtyping as containment of the
interpretations. Following\penalty\ \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{frisch04}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}, records values are
interpreted as quasi-constant functions, that is, functions that map
all labels into $\bot$ (meaning that the field for that label is
undefined) except for a finite set of labels that are mapped into
values. Therefore, (ground) record types are interpreted as
sets of quasi-constant functions. More subtle is the interpretation
for row variables which, as we saw, define the type of \emph{all the
labels except a few ones}; as a consequence our interpretation will map
them into \emph{partial} quasi-constant functions (as in \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{remy94}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}),
requiring a careful handling of their domains.
In \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:subtyping} we define an algorithm to decide the
subtyping relation just defined. We do so by extending the subtyping
algorithm of the monomorphic record types of CDuce\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{benzaken03}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}---on which we base our theory--- to our polymorphic records.
The resulting (backtrack-free) algorithm has the same order of complexity as the
one for monomorphic record types, which is currently used in Elixir.
Finally, we give a formal definition for type substitutions in
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:rowsub}, in particular for the case of row variables which
are expanded into Boolean combinations of rows of fields, and prove
that its application preserves subtyping.
\par In \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:language} we define a language with operations on records.
For those, several equivalent choices are
possible\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{cardelli91}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}. We build records
starting from the empty record value, noted $\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}${\lx@text@rbrace}, and adding new
fields to it by the expression $\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}e\;\texttt{with}\;\ell\;\texttt{=}\;e^{\prime}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$ which extends the record
(resulting from the evaluation of) $e$ with the field $\ell\mathrel{\texttt{=}}e^{\prime}$, provided
that a field for $\ell$ is not already present in $e$.
The other operations on records are field selection, noted
$e.\ell$, which returns the content of the field $\ell$ in $e$, and field
deletion, noted $e\backslash\ell$ which removes from $e$ the field labeled
$\ell$, if any. We define an operational semantics and a declarative type
system, and we show that the latter is sound in the sense of\penalty\ \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{wright94}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}, by proving
that every well-typed expression either diverges or returns a value of the
expression's type (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:syntax}). Next, we define an algorithmic type system
and prove it to be sound and complete with respect to the declarative
one. The system is derived from the declarative one in a standard way:
subsumption is embedded in the elimination rules, intersection
introduction is essentially embedded in the typing of
$\lambda$-abstractions, and the rule for applications performs
instantiation and expansion by looking for a set of substitutions that
make the type of the argument be a subtype of the domain of the
function (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:typealgo}).
\par\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:tallying} studies the tallying problem, which plays the same role as
the unification problem in type inference, but for a subtyping---rather than an
equality---relation on types. The algorithmic system in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:typealgo} is
effective, provided that we produce an algorithm to deduce the type
substitutions to apply to the types of the function and the argument when typing
an application. Following\penalty\ \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} this can be done by solving the
tallying problem for our types, namely, the problem of deciding whether given two types, there
exists a type substitution that makes one type subtype of the other.
\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} prove that the problem is decidable for a system with
function and product type constructors and set-theoretic types, and give a sound
and complete algorithm. However, defining a tallying algorithm for types with row
variables is far more difficult. This is because substitutions
replace row variables by Boolean combinations of rows of fields. We tackle this
problem in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:tallying}, where we define a tallying algorithm for row polymorphic types. We prove that the algorithm is sound but not complete, and we conjecture completeness for the case in which row variables are substituted by a single row of fields.
\par We conclude by discussing related work (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:related}) and further research
directions (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:conclusion}).
\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Contributions and Limitations}
The overall contribution of this work is threefold, since it provides $(i)$ a
theory for a first-order polymorphic type system with row polymorphism and set-theoretic
types, $(ii)$ the practical motivations for such a system, as well as
$(iii)$ the relevant algorithms to apply it in practice. In particular, all the
examples we presented in \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:example} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{sec:need} are typed by our system.
\par The technical contributions can be summarized as follows:
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@We describe a first-order polymorphic type theory with union,
intersection and negation type connectives, and function and record
type constructors, where record types can be either closed, open, or specify
a row variable, and their fields can be declared optional
(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:typesyntax}). We define a subtyping relation for these types by
providing an interpretation
where types are interpreted as set of values and subtyping as set containment
(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:models}).
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@We prove that the subtyping relation is decidable and provide a
backtrack-free algorithm to decide it (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:subtyping}) with
the same order of complexity as the one implemented for Elixir\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{elixirtypes}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@We define type substitutions that map row variables into Boolean
combinations of rows, and prove that the application of type substitutions
preserves subtyping (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:rowsub}).
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@We define a declarative type system
for a record calculus with record extension, selection, and deletion
and prove its soundness (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:syntax}).
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@We define an algorithmic system that we prove sound and complete with
respect to the declarative one (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:typealgo}).
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@We define an algorithm for the \emph{tallying problem} for our system, that is,
the problem of deciding whether given two types there exists a type
substitution that make one subtype of the other; we prove soundness of the
algorithm (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:tallying}).
\end{enumerate}The system defined here presents some limitations. Some are
expected and characteristic of the kind of systems we consider here: the typing
relation is not decidable (this is typical of systems with intersection
types) and the type system has no principal types (which is already
the case both for systems with polymorphic set-theoretic
types\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna14,castagna15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} and for expressive record type
systems\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{cardelli91}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}). Other limitations are instead new, in
particular that the tallying algorithm is sound but not
complete (an example is given in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{ex:incomplete}; a complete algorithm exists when record types are kept out
of the equation: see\penalty\ \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}). We prove that one of the
reasons for incompleteness is that we interpret row variables into
Boolean combinations of rows rather than into single rows, and we
conjecture that completeness can be recovered in the latter case, but at
the expenses of the type system which can type fewer expressions (cf.\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{ex:boolrows}).
Note however that all the examples given in the introduction
fall outside the incompleteness area: as \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{ex:incomplete} shows, building an example for
incompleteness requires the application of higher-order functions
whose types map unions of record types into similar unions of record
types.
\par From a practical point of view, the main limitation of this
system is that it does not feature first class record labels, that is, the
operations for field selection, extension, and deletion must specify
nominal labels which, thus, cannot be obtained as the result of a
computation. This important omission might hinder the
application of our theory to dynamic languages where such a feature is
widely used. This omission, however, is deliberate since we wanted to
focus on the problem of row polymorphism, and having
first-class labels is mostly orthogonal to it. We believe that
it will not be hard
to extend our work on the lines of\penalty\ \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna23a}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} to add first
class labels to our system, and we leave it for future work.
Finally, we expect that implementing our system in a language such as
Elixir will not affect the performance of the type checker: the complexity of
our subtyping algorithm is the same as the one currently implemented in Elixir,
while the complexity of the tallying algorithm and, thus, of type inference, is
the same as the one implemented in CDuce\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} and planned for
Elixir\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna24a}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, up to a low multiplicative factor (cf.\ Footnote\penalty\ \ref{fn:multiplicative}).
Upcoming work on implementing the type system (in CDuce)
shall provide empirical evidence.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Types}
We introduce the syntax of types (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:typesyntax}) and their set-theoretic
interpretation from which we derive the subtyping relation
(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:models}).
We define the algorithm to decide the subtyping relation (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:subtyping})
and prove that subtyping is preserved by type and row substitutions (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:rowsub}).
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Syntax of Types}
\par\begin{definition}Let $\mathcal{L}$ be a countable set of \emph{labels} ranged over by $\ell$.
The set $\mathcal{T}$ of \emph{types} (ranged over by $t$) contains all terms
\emph{coinductively} generated by the corresponding grammar below, and that $(1)$ have a
finite number of different sub-terms (\emph{regularity}) and $(2)$ in which every
infinite branch contains an infinite number of occurrences of the record or
arrow type constructors (\emph{contractivity}).
The set $\mathcal{R}$ of \emph{rows} (ranged over by $r$) as well as the set of
\emph{field-types} (ranged over by $\tau$) contain all terms
\emph{inductively} generated by the corresponding grammars below.
\par$$\begin{array}[]{lrclr}\textbf{Kinds}&\kappa&\Coloneqq&\star\penalty\ |\penalty\ \star_{\bot}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathrm{Row}(L)\\
\textbf{Types}&t&\Coloneqq&\alpha\penalty\ |\penalty\ b\penalty\ |\penalty\ t\to t\penalty\ |\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\tau,\dots,\ell=\tau\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\penalty\ |\penalty\ t\lor t\penalty\ |\penalty\ \neg t\penalty\ |\penalty\ \mathbb{0}\\
\textbf{Field-types}&\tau&\Coloneqq&\theta\penalty\ |\penalty\ t\penalty\ |\penalty\ \bot\penalty\ |\penalty\ \tau\lor\tau\penalty\ |\penalty\ \neg\tau\\
\textbf{Tails}&\varsigma&\Coloneqq&\rho\penalty\ |\penalty\ \epsilon\penalty\ |\penalty\ \textbf{..}\\
\textbf{Rows}&r&\Coloneqq&\boldsymbol{\langle}\ell=\tau,\dots,\ell=\tau\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L}\penalty\ |\penalty\ r\lor r\penalty\ |\penalty\ \neg r\end{array}$$where, $\alpha$, $\theta$, $\rho$ range, respectively, on type variables, field-type variables, and row variables; $\bot$ is a distinguished symbol different from all types; and (from now on) $L\in\mathcal{P}_{\!\textup{fin}}(\mathcal{L})$ is a finite set of labels.
\end{definition}\noindent Following a mathematical logic terminology, basic types, arrows, and records are
called \emph{type constructors} and yield \emph{type atoms}, while unions, intersections, and
negations are \emph{type connectives}.
Our system use kinds. Types are of kind $\star$, field-types of kind $\star_{\bot}$, and we have an
infinite set of kinds for rows, parametrized by a finite set $L$: a
row indexed by the set $L$ is of kind $\mathrm{Row}(L)$.
We use $T$ to range over types, field-types, and rows
(i.e., $T$ stands for either $t$, $\tau$, or $r$), and
define $T_{1}\land T_{2}\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}\neg(\neg T_{1}\lor\neg T_{2})$
and $T_{1}{\smallsetminus}T_{2}\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}T_{1}\land\neg T_{2}$.
For every kind, besides the full set of type connectives, there are a bottom
and a top element (forming a lattice w.r.t.\ subtyping).
In $\star$, the top type is noted $\mathbb{1}\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}\neg\mathbb{0}$; in $\star_{\bot}$,
the top field-type is $\mathbb{1}\lor\bot$ (notice that $\mathbb{1}$ contains all types, but not $\bot$ which is not a type); in each kind
$\mathrm{Row}(L)$, the top row element is $\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L}$
We use the generic notation $\mathbb{0}\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}\neg\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L}$ for the
bottom element in $\mathrm{Row}(L)$, with $L$ being, thus, implicitly given by the context.
\par Besides the aforementioned connectives, the types $t$ of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:types} are
made of variables (from a countable set $\mathcal{V}_{t}$ and ranged over by
$\alpha$), a finite set $\mathcal{B}$ of basic types (e.g., \text{{Int}}, \text{{Bool}}; ranged
over by $b$), function types, and record types.
Coinduction accounts for recursive types and comes with the usual
restrictions of regularity ---necessary for the decidability of the
subtyping relation--- and
contractivity ---which rules out meaningless types such as an infinite tower of
negations, while providing a well-founded order for inductive proofs (see\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna24}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} for details).
Notice that since both field-types and rows are built inductively, they are always \emph{finite} unions or negations of, respectively, field-types or rows ---this aligns with the contractivity requirement of types---, which are formed of (possibly infinite) types.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Records}
Our records are based on the theory for records defined by
\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{frisch04}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} (see\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna23a}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} for a description in English)
and first used in CDuce. Our work extends the (monomorphic) record theory of\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{frisch04}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} with row and field-type variables.
In Frisch's theory, a record \emph{value} is a total function on $\mathcal{L}$ that maps
a finite set of labels into values, and all the remaining labels to a distinguished symbol
$\bot$ representing the undefined (such a function is dubbed
\emph{quasi-constant} by Frisch: cf.\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:qcf}).
\par Record type atoms (ranged over by ${\texttt{R}}$) are types of the form
$\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell_{1}=\tau_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}=\tau_{n}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$, that is, an
unordered list describing the mapping of a finite set of pairwise
distinct labels into field-types, which is followed by a \emph{tail} $\varsigma$
that covers the
infinitely many remaining labels.
We often use the more compact notation
$\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$, where $L\in\mathcal{P}_{\!\textup{fin}}(\mathcal{L})$.
For ${\texttt{R}}=\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$, we define
$\fin({\texttt{R}})\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}L$ and $\tail({\texttt{R}})\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}\varsigma$.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Fields}
In\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{frisch04}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, field-types $\tau$ are either:
(a) \emph{mandatory} when $\tau=t$ with type $t$;
or (b) \emph{optional} when $\tau=t\lor\bot$ with type $t$,
and in particular always undefined if $\tau=\bot$---i.e., $\tau=\mathbb{0}\lor\bot$ (notice that
undefined fields cannot be typed just by $\mathbb{0}$:
records are modeled as (indexed) products, in which a single empty
field makes the whole record empty; also notice that since $\bot$ is distinct from all types, then $t\lor\bot\neq t$ and $\bot\wedge t=\emptyset$ for any type\penalty\ $t$).
Our definition adds field-type variables (\emph{field variables} for short),
ranged over by $\theta$ and drawn from a countable set $\mathcal{V}_{f}$.
In what follows, they are used in two ways:
(1) to solve the tallying problem (see \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:tallying})
and (2) to implement \emph{presence polymorphism} without additional effort (see
the discussion in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:related}).
The introduction of field variables forces us to loosen the form of
field-types by allowing arbitrary Boolean combinations.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Rows}
The tail $\varsigma$ of a record type atom ${\texttt{R}}=\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$
can be of three sorts.
If $\varsigma=\epsilon$, then ${\texttt{R}}$ is \emph{closed}, and only includes
records that
assign $\bot$ to every label outside $L$.
If $\varsigma=\textbf{..}$, then ${\texttt{R}}$ is \emph{open}, and imposes no restriction on the
values of the fields outside $L$.
That is, those labels are given field-type $\mathbb{1}\lor\bot$.
Giving $\mathbb{1}$ would be unsound, as it would imply that the
selection of any remaining field of a record of this type would always be
well-typed, even for fields not containing a value (such fields always exist
as records are built inductively).
New in our work with respect to \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{frisch04}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} is that $\varsigma$ may also be a row variable (taken from a countable set $\mathcal{V}_{r}$ ranged
over by $\rho$) in which case ${\texttt{R}}$ is \emph{polymorphic}.
\par In ${\texttt{R}}$, the row variable $\rho=\varsigma$ defines the fields for the cofinite set of
labels $\rdef(\rho)$ that we call the \emph{definition space} of $\rho$.
Now, since record types are {total} functions on $\mathcal{L}$, we cannot use
them to interpret row variables.
For this, we use the new syntactic category of \emph{rows} from \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:types},
that denote {partial} functions on $\mathcal{L}$ defined on co-finite sets of labels.
\par Rows are of the form $\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L^{\prime}}$.
The relevant part is the set $L^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{\!\textup{fin}}(\mathcal{L})$ at the index, which denotes the
finite set of labels on which the row is \emph{not} defined. In
other terms, the row above is a total function from
$\mathcal{L}\smallsetminus L^{\prime}$ into field-types: the $\tau_{\ell}$'s define the
fields for the labels in $L$ and the tail $\varsigma$ the fields for the
labels in $\mathcal{L}\smallsetminus(L\cup L^{\prime})$.
\par Of course, not every row or record type is well-formed, since we must ensure
that the various $L$, $L^{\prime}$, and $\rdef(\rho)$ form a partition of $\mathcal{L}$.
They have to verify the following three properties (enforced statically by a kinding
system whose straightforward definition is given in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{app:types}, \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:kinding}):
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@In a type $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ we must have
$\mathcal{L}\smallsetminus L=\rdef(\rho)$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@In a row $\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L^{\prime}}$ we must have
$L\cap L^{\prime}=\emptyset$ and, if $\varsigma=\rho$, then
$\mathcal{L}\smallsetminus(L\cup L^{\prime})=\rdef(\rho)$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Unions (and, thus, intersections) are only on rows defined on the
same set of labels.
\end{enumerate}Finally, for all $\ell\in\mathcal{L}$, we define ${\texttt{R}}(\ell)$
to yield the field-type associated to $\ell$ by ${\texttt{R}}$:
\par${\texttt{R}}(\ell)\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\tau_{\ell}&\text{if }\ell\in L\text{; otherwise:}\\
\bot&\text{if }\varsigma=\epsilon\\
\mathbb{1}\lor\bot&\text{if }\varsigma=\textbf{..}\text{ or }\varsigma\in\mathcal{V}_{r}\end{array}\right.$
\par\noindent This operator, as well as others, is trivially transferred from record types to rows.
Notice that in the case of open and closed records (and rows), the choice of $L$
for a record type is not canonical, as for instance
$\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell_{1}=\text{{Int}}\lor\bot,\ell_{2}=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\epsilon\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ and $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell_{1}=\text{{Int}}\lor\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\epsilon\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ are semantically equivalent.
Since row variables have a constant definition space however, a record type like
$\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ has a single (top-level) representation with
$L=\{\ell\}=\mathcal{L}\smallsetminus\rdef(\rho)$.
\par\begin{example}$t=\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell_{1}=\text{{Int}},\ell_{2}=\text{{Bool}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ is an open record type, whose values
are records with at least an integer in $\ell_{1}$ and a boolean in
$\ell_{2}$---e.g., $\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\ell_{1}=3,\ell_{2}=\text{{True}},\ell_{3}=\text{{\textquoteleft a\textquoteright}}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$.
Its closed counterpart, $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell_{1}=\text{{Int}},\ell_{2}=\text{{Bool}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\epsilon\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$, types records in which both and only $\ell_{1}$ and $\ell_{2}$ have some value. The type
$t$ is equivalent to (i.e., it is both a subtype and a supertype of)
both $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell_{1}=\text{{Int}},\ell_{2}=\text{{Bool}},\ell_{4}=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$
and $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell_{1}=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell_{2}=\text{{Bool}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
By De Morgan's laws, $\neg t$ is equivalent to
$\neg\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell_{1}=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\vee\neg\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell_{2}=\text{{Bool}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ and thus to $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell_{1}=\neg\text{{Int}}\lor\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\vee\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell_{2}=\neg\text{{Bool}}\lor\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
The type $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell_{1}=\mathbb{0}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ is equivalent to $\mathbb{0}$, and so are both
$\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell_{2}=\mathbb{0}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\epsilon\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ and $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell_{1}=\mathbb{0}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.\hfill\qed\end{example}\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Subtyping Relation}
The subtyping relation characterizes the type system:
union and intersection types are the least upper bounds and
greatest lower bounds of this relation, and the typing relation
relies on subtyping to compare types.
Our goal is to extend the monomorphic type theory of records of\penalty\ \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{frisch04}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}
to a polymorphic one, obtained by adding row variables.
For this, we stick to the technique employed to extend the theory of semantic
subtyping on monomorphic types to type-polymorphic ones
\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{frisch08,castagna11,gesbert15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}.
This technique can be distilled into three conceptual steps:
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@\emph{Define the subtyping relation on monomorphic types.} This is
done in \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{frisch08}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} by the definition of a \emph{set-theoretic model}.
This definition describes how to interpret types as subsets of some domain
whose elements represent the values of the language. Given a specific
model (i.e., a specific domain $\mathcal{D}$ and an interpretation
function $\llbracket\,\rrbracket$ from types in $\mathcal{T}$ to sets in $\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{D})$), this induces a subtyping
relation defined as the containment of
the interpretations (i.e., $t\leq t^{\prime}\hbox{\;\;$\Leftrightarrow$\hskip-10.243pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 5.12149pt}\llbracket t\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket t^{\prime}\rrbracket$).
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@\emph{Extend the definition of the \emph{model} to polymorphic
types}. This is done by making
the interpretation of types parametric in the interpretation $\eta$
of \emph{type variables} as sets of values. Subtyping can then be defined
as containment for every type variable interpretation $\eta$ (i.e., $t\leq t^{\prime}\!\hbox{\;\;$\Leftrightarrow$\hskip-10.243pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 5.12149pt}\!\forall\eta.(\llbracket t\rrbracket\eta\subseteq\llbracket t^{\prime}\rrbracket\eta)$), but only for models that satisfy a
so-called \textquotedblleft convexity\textquotedblright condition\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna11}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} (see after
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:interpretation}).
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@\emph{Exibit a specific convex model for polymorphic types and deduce
the subtyping relation}. \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{gesbert15}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} show that a
convex model for polymorphic types can be obtained by taking a specific model for
monomorphic types and indexing all its elements by finite sets of
type variables.
\end{enumerate}Henceforth, we apply the same approach to record types:
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@[(r1)]The subtyping relation for monomorphic record types is defined
by interpreting them as sets of \emph{record values}
(i.e., {total} functions from labels to values).
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@[(r2)]We extend this interpretation to polymorphic record types, by
making it parametric in the interpretation $\eta$ of \emph{row
variables} into sets of \emph{rows} (i.e., {partial}
functions from labels to values).
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@[(r3)]We define a convex model of polymorphic record types, by taking
a specific model for monomorphic record types and indexing all its
elements by finite sets of \emph{row variables}.
\end{enumerate}The sole distinction between the two approaches, thus, lies in the
interpretation of \emph{type variables} and of \emph{row
variables}. While \emph{type} variables are mapped into sets of
values, \emph{row} variables are mapped into sets of \emph{rows}. Rows
are not values themselves but rather \textquotedblleft chunks\textquotedblright of values: both (record)
values and rows are quasi-constant functions; however, (record) values
are total functions, whereas rows are partial ones.
We thus achieve the same conceptual simplicity as the polymorphic
extension of semantic subtyping, though the technical development is, in
our case, far more involved.
\par For space reasons, we present here only the final result of this
process, that is, the specific convex model of step (r3), given by the
domain of\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:interpretation} and the interpretation
of\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:indint}. We then derive from
it the definition of the subtyping relation (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:subtyping}) and
a decision procedure that we prove sound, complete, and terminating
(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:correct_subalg} and \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:term_subalg}). The development of the first
two steps is necessary only to prove that the domain and the interpretation
given below satisfy the properties of model and of convexity. This
development and the corresponding proofs are given
in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:app-models}. Although a detailed explanation of these properties
is outside the scope of this presentation, we want to stress that
without the model property the definition of subtyping
would not be well-founded and without the convexity property the decision
procedure would not be sound (see\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{frisch08,castagna11}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} for details).
\par To interpret record values we follow\penalty\ \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{frisch04}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}, and represent each record
value by a quasi-constant function that either maps labels into values
(i.e., the elements of $\mathcal{D}$) or into $\bot$.
Let us write $\mathcal{D}_{\bot}$ for $\mathcal{D}\cup\{\bot\}$ where $\bot$ is a
distinguished element not in $\mathcal{D}$.
Quasi-constant functions are total functions that map all but a finite set of elements of their
domain into the same default value.
Formally, we have the following definition.
\begin{definition}A function $r:\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{D}_{\bot}$ is \emph{quasi-constant} if the set
$\{\ell\in\mathcal{L}\mid r(\ell)\not=\bot\}$ is finite.
We use $\mathcal{L}\rightarrowtriangle\mathcal{D}_{\bot}$ to denote
the set of quasi-constant functions from $\mathcal{L}$ to $\mathcal{D}_{\bot}$ and
$\mathopen{\{\hskip-2.65pt[}\ell_{1}=\delta_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}=\delta_{n},\,\rule[-1.0pt]{6.0pt}{0.7pt}=\bot\mathopen{]\hskip-2.65pt\}}$ to denote the
quasi-constant function $r:\mathcal{L}\rightarrowtriangle\mathcal{D}_{\bot}$ defined
by $r(\ell_{i})=\delta_{i}$ for $i=1..n$ and $r(\ell)=\bot$ for
$\ell\in\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}\{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{n}\}$.
\end{definition}We also have to interpret \emph{rows}, which are defined only on a cofinite \emph{subset} of $\mathcal{L}$.
In other terms, rows are \emph{partial} quasi-constant functions from
$\mathcal{L}$ to $\mathcal{D}_{\bot}$, that we note $\mathcal{L}\mathrlap{\;\;\raisebox{0.8pt}{\tiny/}}\rightarrowtriangle\mathcal{D}_{\bot}$.
Since a total function is also a partial one, then we need just
the latter in our domain to interpret record types and rows. This
yields the following definition of domain.
\begin{definition}The \emph{interpretation domain} $\mathcal{D}$ for types, is the set of finite terms $d$
inductively produced by the following grammar, where $c$ ranges over the set
$\mathcal{C}$ of constants, $\ell$ over the set $\mathcal{L}$ of labels, and $V$ over sets of variables contained
in $\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}_{t}\cup\mathcal{V}_{f}\cup\mathcal{V}_{r}$.
The interpretation domain $\mathcal{D}_{\bot}$ for fields (resp. $\mathcal{D}_{\operatorname{row}}$ for
rows) is the set of terms $\delta$ (resp. ${\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}$).
\@@amsalign
We use $D$ for an element that is either $d$, ${\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}$ or $\delta$.
We define $\rdef(\langle\!|(\ell=\delta_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{1}},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle_{L_{2}}^{V})=\mathcal{L}\smallsetminus L_{2}$. We use $\Tag(D)$ to denote the set of
variables indexing $D$, that is, $\Tag(c^{V})=\Tag(\{(d,\partial),\dots,(d,\partial)\}^{V})=\Tag(\mathrm{Rec}({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud})^{V})=\Tag(\langle\!|\ell_{1}=\delta,\dots,\ell_{n}=\delta,\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle_{L}^{V})=\Tag(\bot^{V})=V$.
\end{definition}The elements of the domain are constants $\mathcal{C}$ to interpret basic types,
sets of finite binary relations $\mathcal{P}_{\!\textup{fin}}(\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D}_{\Omega})$ to
interpret function types, and \emph{partial} quasi constant functions
$\mathcal{L}\mathrlap{\;\;\raisebox{0.8pt}{\tiny/}}\rightarrowtriangle\mathcal{D}_{\bot}$ to interpret rows (and record types
by the total ones). The fact
that functions are finite binary relations is a standard technique of semantic
subtyping, and corresponds to interpreting function spaces
into the infinite set of their finite approximations; that these binary relations can yield a distinguished
element $\Omega$ (which, intuitively, represents a type error) is also
a standard technique of semantic subtyping used to avoid $\mathbb{1}\to\mathbb{1}$ to be a supertype of
all function types: since both aspects do not play any specific role in our
work we will not further
comment on them (see\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{frisch08}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} for a detailed explanation
or\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna23a}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Section 3.2)} for a shorter one).
\par If we look more closely at the definition of the row elements
in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:interpretation}, we see
that they are \emph{partial} quasi-constant functions in
$\mathcal{L}\mathrlap{\;\;\raisebox{0.8pt}{\tiny/}}\rightarrowtriangle\mathcal{D}_{\bot}$ with default value $\bot$. More
precisely, the row element $\langle\!|\ell_{1}=\delta_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}=\delta_{n},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle_{L}$
is the quasi-constant function $\mathopen{\{\hskip-2.65pt[}\ell_{1}=\delta_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}=\delta_{n},\rule[-1.0pt]{6.0pt}{0.7pt}=\bot\mathopen{]\hskip-2.65pt\}}$ in $(\mathcal{L}\smallsetminus L)\rightarrowtriangle\mathcal{D}_{\bot}$. When a row is total on $\mathcal{L}$, then it can
be wrapped in a $\mathrm{Rec}()$ constructor yielding (the interpretation
of) a record value (the inverse operation is given in the \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:row}
below).
\par All these elements are indexed by
a finite set of variables ranged over by $V$. This technique was introduced
by\penalty\ \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{gesbert15}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} to interpret type variables (cf.\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:indint}), while ensuring that the model
we obtain is \emph{convex} in the sense of\penalty\ \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna11}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}. Convexity is
a property that prevents the definition of meaningless subtyping relations, by
imposing that the interpretation of types changes uniformly for any possible
change of the interpretation of the type variables. A sufficient condition
to satisfy convexity is that the interpretation maps every type into an infinite
set. Indexing each element of the domain with a finite set of variables is an
easy way to guarantee this since, for instance, even the interpretation of the
singleton type $c$ is the infinite set $\{c^{V}\penalty\ |\penalty\ V\in\mathcal{P}_{\!\textup{fin}}(\mathcal{V})\}$.
In summary, the domain of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:interpretation} is the
one by\penalty\ \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{gesbert15}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}, but where pairs (inhabiting product types) are replaced by
record values of the form $\mathrm{Rec}({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud})$ and rows.
\par\begin{definition}Let ${\texttt{R}}=\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
We define $\operatorname{row}({\texttt{R}})=\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset}$.
We extend this definition homomorphically to Boolean combinations of
record type atoms.
\end{definition}\par We have now all ingredients needed to define our set-theoretic interpretation
for the types:
\par\begin{definition}Let $\mathcal{D}$ be the domain of\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:interpretation} and $\mathcal{T}$ the
types of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:types}. We define a binary predicate $(D:T)$
(\textquotedblleft the element $D$ belongs to $T$\textquotedblright) on $\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{D}_{\bot}\times\mathcal{T}_{\bot}\cup\mathcal{D}_{\operatorname{row}}\times\mathcal{R}$
by induction on the pair $(D,T)$ ordered lexicographically.
The predicate is only defined if $D$ is coherent with the kind of
$T$:
$D=d$ if $T=t$, $D=\delta$ if $T=\tau$, and
$D={\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}$ if $T=r$ and $\dom(D)=\rdef(r)$.
\par\begin{description}\description@item@[\framebox[14mm]{\rm Types:}]\hskip 18.88913pt\!\!\!$\begin{array}[t]{c}(d:\alpha)=\alpha\in\Tag(d)\hskip 18.49988pt(c^{V}:b)=c\in\mathbb{B}(b)\hskip 18.49988pt(\mathrm{Rec}({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud})^{V}:{\texttt{R}})=({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}:\operatorname{row}({\texttt{R}}))\\[0.56905pt]
(\{(d_{1},\partial_{1}),\dots,(d_{n},\partial_{n})\}^{V}:t_{1}\to t_{2})=\forall i\in[1..n].\>\mathsf{if}\>(d_{i}:t_{1})\mathrel{\mathsf{then}}(\partial_{i}:t_{2})\end{array}$
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\description@item@[\framebox[14mm]{\rm Fields:}]\hskip 18.88913pt$(\delta:\theta)=\theta\in\Tag(\delta)\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt(\bot^{V}:\bot)=\mathsf{true}$\\
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\description@item@[\framebox[14mm]{\rm Rows:}]\mbox{}\\
\begin{equation}\begin{split}\;\;(\langle\!|(\ell=\delta_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{1}},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle_{L_{2}}^{V}:r)&=(\forall\ell\in L_{1}.(\delta_{\ell}:r(\ell)))\\[-5.12149pt]
&\phantom{=}\ \mathrel{\mathsf{and}}(\forall\ell\in\rdef(r)\smallsetminus L_{1}.(\bot^{\emptyset}:r(\ell)))\\[-5.12149pt]
&\phantom{=}\ \mathrel{\mathsf{and}}\tail(r)=\rho\implies\rho\in V\end{split}\vskip-5.69054pt\end{equation}\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\description@item@[\framebox[14mm]{\rm All:}]\hskip 18.88913pt\!\!\!$\begin{array}[t]{rll}(D:T_{1}\lor T_{2})&=\hskip 9.24994pt(D:T_{1})\mathrel{\mathsf{or}}(D:T_{2})&\text{if $T_{1}$, $T_{2}$ of the same kind}\\
(D:\lnot T)&=\hskip 9.24994pt\mathsf{not}\>(D:T)&\text{if the kinds of $D$ and $T$ correspond}\\
(D:T)&=\hskip 9.24994pt\mathsf{false}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}$
\end{description}We define the interpretation $\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket:\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{D})$
as $\llbracket t\rrbracket=\{d\in\mathcal{D}\mid(d:t)\}$.
\end{definition}\noindent We cannot define $\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket$ by induction on types, since
their coinductive definition would make the definition
ill-founded. Thus, \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:indint} uses the predicate
$(D:T)$ for which an inductive definition is possible thanks to the inductive definition of $\mathcal{D}$.
The interpretation of types in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:indint} is mostly the same as in\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{gesbert15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}: a type variable $\alpha$ is interpreted as the set of all elements tagged by $\alpha$; a type $t_{1}\to t_{2}$ is the set all the finite approximations of functions that map inputs of type $t_{1}$ into results of type $t_{2}$; and union, intersection, and negation types are mapped into the corresponding set-theoretic counterparts. The main difference with \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{gesbert15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} is
the interpretation of rows and, thus, of record types. Equation\penalty\ \eqref{eq:rowint}
defines when a row element is in the
interpretation of a row $r$: it requires that all the components of
the row element are in the interpretations of the types specified by
$r$ (first two lines), and if the tail of $r$ is a row variable,
then it must index the row element (last line).
\par\begin{definition}Let $\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket:\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{D})$ be the interpretation from \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:indint}. It induces the
following subtype relation in $\mathcal{T}{\times}\mathcal{T}$:\\
\centerline{\hbox{
$t_{1}\leq t_{2}\hbox{\;\;$\iff$\hskip-16.90094pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 11.38109pt}\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket t_{2}\rrbracket$}}\\
The interpretation also induces the subfield relation in
$\mathcal{T}_{\bot}{\times}\mathcal{T}_{\bot}$ and subrow relation in
$\mathcal{R}{\times}\mathcal{R}$ defined as\\
\centerline{\hbox{
$\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}\hbox{\;\;$\iff$\hskip-16.90094pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 11.38109pt}\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{fld}}\subseteq\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{fld}}\hskip 18.49988ptr_{1}\leq r_{2}\hbox{\;\;$\iff$\hskip-16.90094pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 11.38109pt}\llbracket r_{1}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{row}}\subseteq\llbracket r_{2}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{row}}$}}\\
where, the interpretation $\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\operatorname{fld}}:\mathcal{T}_{\bot}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{D}_{\bot})$ is defined as
$\llbracket\tau\rrbracket^{\operatorname{fld}}=\{\delta\in\mathcal{D}_{\bot}\mid(\delta:\tau)\}$, and the interpretation $\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\operatorname{row}}:\mathcal{R}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{D}_{\operatorname{row}})$ is defined as $\llbracket r\rrbracket^{\operatorname{row}}=\{{\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}\in\mathcal{D}_{\operatorname{row}}\mid({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}:r)\}$.
\end{definition}\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Deciding Subtyping}
\par Now that subtyping is defined, we need an
effective decision procedure sound and complete
with respect to this definition.
Deciding subtyping amounts to deciding the emptiness of a type, since $t_{1}\leq t_{2}$ is equivalent to $t_{1}\wedge\neg t_{2}\leq\mathbb{0}$.
From \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{frisch08}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, we know that any type can be equivalently rewritten into a
disjunctive normal form (DNF) of the form $\bigvee_{i\in I}(\bigwedge_{a\in P_{i}}a\wedge\bigwedge_{a\in N_{i}}\neg a\wedge\bigwedge_{\alpha\in V^{p}_{i}}\alpha\wedge\bigwedge_{\alpha\in V^{n}_{i}}\neg\alpha)$ where each intersection
contains only atoms $a$'s with the same type constructors: they are
all basic types, or all arrows, or all records.
Thus, checking emptiness of a type amounts to checking emptiness of all
these intersections.
\par We suppose the sets $V_{i}^{p}$ and $V_{i}^{n}$ to be disjoint, as otherwise the
$i$-th intersection is trivially empty and can be discarded.
Then, emptiness of the intersections cannot depend on the type variables
whose intersection is not empty.
Thus, we just need decision procedures for the emptiness of the
$\bigwedge_{a\in P_{i}}a\wedge\bigwedge_{a\in N_{i}}\neg a$ parts.
Those are already known for every intersection of atoms, except for polymorphic records.
What is still missing is a formula that characterizes the emptiness of an intersection of the form
$\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P}{\texttt{R}}\wedge\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in N}\neg{\texttt{R}}$, that is,
that decides whether $\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P}{\texttt{R}}\leq\bigvee_{{\texttt{R}}\in N}{\texttt{R}}$ holds.
\par To rephrase, given any type $t$, the subtyping procedure recursively apply
these two steps:
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@Reduce $t$ to a DNF $\bigvee_{i\in I}t_{i}$ with $t_{i}=\bigwedge_{a\in P_{i}}a\wedge\bigwedge_{a\in N_{i}}\neg a\wedge\bigwedge_{\alpha\in V^{p}_{i}}\alpha\wedge\bigwedge_{\alpha\in V^{n}_{i}}\neg\alpha$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Check the emptiness of each $t_{i}$ by checking if
$\bigwedge_{a\in P_{i}}a\leq\bigvee_{a\in N_{i}}a$ is empty:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@[-]If the atoms are not records, we use the existing corresponding
functions given in \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{frisch04}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@[-]If they are (polymorphic) records, we use the new algorithm
that we describe below.
\end{itemize}\end{enumerate}\par\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Subtyping algorithm}
\par Let $t=\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P}{\texttt{R}}\wedge\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in N}\neg{\texttt{R}}$.
Deciding emptiness of this type is done in two main steps.
First, preprocess $t$ by normalizing the positive
side of the type to \emph{isolate} row variables.
For this, we use the equivalence between
$\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ and
$\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot)_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
Note that a type $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot)_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ will be
abbreviated as $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
Second, apply the function $\Phi$, the core of our algorithm that we describe
below.
\par Let $L=\bigcup_{r\in P\cup N}\fin(r)$ be the set of labels appearing
explicitely in every (top-level) record atom of $t$.
Our starting type $t$ is equivalent to the following intermediate one:\vskip-3.69885pt\begin{equation}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell={\textstyle\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P}{\texttt{R}}(\ell)})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\fin({\texttt{R}})\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\tail({\texttt{R}})\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in N}\neg{\texttt{R}}\vskip-5.97508pt\end{equation}This type was obtained by transforming the positive $\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P}{\texttt{R}}$ part of $t$: we merged the fields over $L$ into a single atomic record type,
and grouped the tails of positive records in a separate intersection.
Next, we are going to rewrite the type in \eqref{eq:subtyping_two} by
splitting the middle intersection $\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\fin({\texttt{R}})\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\tail({\texttt{R}})\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ in two: an intersection with all the record
atoms whose tail is a row variable, and all the others that we will merge
with the leftmost record in \eqref{eq:subtyping_two}. For that, let us define
$\varsigma_{\circ}$ to represent the intersection of the tails of
the records in $P$ whenever this tail is either $\epsilon$ or $\textbf{..}$, that
is, $\varsigma_{\circ}=\epsilon$ if there is ${\texttt{R}}\in P$ such that $\tail({\texttt{R}})=\epsilon$, and
$\varsigma_{\circ}=\textbf{..}$ otherwise.
If we take all the records in $\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\fin({\texttt{R}})\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\tail({\texttt{R}})\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ whose tail is not a row variable and intersect
them with the leftmost record in \eqref{eq:subtyping_two}, then
we obtain the record type ${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}=\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell={\textstyle\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P}{\texttt{R}}(\ell)})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma_{\circ}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
Notice that ${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}$ is a monomorphic record type.
For the remaining records in the middle intersection, let us denote by $V_{p}=\{\rho\mid\exists{\texttt{R}}\in P.\tail({\texttt{R}})=\rho\}$ the set of all
top-level type variables occurring in $P$.
The intersection of atoms in \eqref{eq:subtyping_two} is then equivalent to the following type, which is the one
for which we have to decide emptiness:\vskip-3.69885pt\begin{equation}{\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\wedge\bigwedge_{\rho\in V_{p}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}\rdef(\rho)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in N}\neg{\texttt{R}}\vskip-5.69054pt\end{equation}The second step of our algorithm is realized by the function
$\Phi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},N)$, where ${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\not\leq\mathbb{0}$:\\
$\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Phi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},\emptyset)&\coloneq&\textsf{false}\\
\Phi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},N\cup\{{\texttt{R}}\})&\coloneq&\textsf{if }(\tail({\texttt{R}})=\textbf{..}\ \textsf{ or}\ \tail({\texttt{R}})=\tail({\texttt{R}}_{\circ})\ \textsf{ or}\ \tail({\texttt{R}})\in V_{p})\textsf{ then}\\
&&\hskip 9.24994pt\forall\ell{\in}\fin({\texttt{R}}_{\circ}).\ ({\texttt{R}}_{\circ}(\ell)\leq{\texttt{R}}(\ell)\textsf{ or }\Phi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\wedge\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell:\neg{\texttt{R}}(\ell)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}},V_{p},N))\\
&&\textsf{else }\Phi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},N)\end{array}$\\
The function must decide whether ${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\wedge\bigwedge_{\rho\in V_{p}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}\rdef(\rho)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\leq\bigvee_{{\texttt{R}}\in N}{\texttt{R}}$, so it picks an ${\texttt{R}}\in N$ and
generates the conditions to test the containment. The first clause of
the definition states that if we already examined all ${\texttt{R}}\in N$, then subtyping does not hold, since ${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\nleq\mathbb{0}$ and
so its intersection with some row variables is also non-empty. If ${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}$ is
open and ${\texttt{R}}$ is closed, or if ${\texttt{R}}$ is polymorphic, but its row
variable is not one in $V_{p}$, then the
containment cannot come from this particular ${\texttt{R}}$, and it is
discarded: this corresponds to the {else} branch of second
clause. Otherwise, we are in the case in which either ${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}$ is
closed, or we are comparing two records types with a common row
variable, or ${\texttt{R}}$ is open. In these cases we compare ${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}$ and ${\texttt{R}}$
component-wise, and for each $\ell$ we check that either
${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}(\ell)\leq{\texttt{R}}(\ell)$ or that the part that is in excess in
${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}(\ell)$ is contained in the records remaining in
$N$.\par\begin{example}Consider the verification of the following subtyping relation:\\
\centerline{\hbox{$\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\text{{a}}=\text{{True}},\text{{b}}=\text{{Int}}{\lor}\text{{Bool}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\text{{b}}=\text{{Int}}{\lor}\text{{String}},\text{{c}}=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\text{{a}}=\text{{Bool}},\text{{b}}=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\vee\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\text{{a}}=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$}}\\
The first step of the subtyping algorithm isolates $\rho$, reducing the relation by equivalence into:
\\
\centerline{\hbox{$\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\text{{a}}=\text{{True}},\text{{b}}=\text{{Int}},\text{{c}}=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\text{{a}},\text{{b}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\neg\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\text{{a}}=\text{{Bool}},\text{{b}}=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\neg\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\text{{a}}=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\leq\mathbb{0}$}}\\
Following the notation used in equation\penalty\ \eqref{eq:subtyping_three}, we have
${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}=\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\text{{a}}=\text{{True}},\text{{b}}=\text{{Int}},\text{{c}}=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$,
$V_{p}=\{\rho\}$ and
$N=\{\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\text{{a}}=\text{{Bool}},\text{{b}}=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}},\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\text{{a}}=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\}$.
The function $\Phi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},N)$ verifies subtyping in three steps.
In the first step, it selects $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\text{{a}}=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ from $N$,
which is discarded (i.e., in the second clause for $\Phi$, the {else} branch is taken), since $\rho^{\prime}\notin V_{p}$.
Second, it selects $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\text{{a}}=\text{{Bool}},\text{{b}}=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ from $N$.
Since $\rho\in V_{p}$, it finishes by checking every label $\text{{a}},\text{{b}}$ and $\text{{c}}$ w.r.t. ${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}$ (i.e., the {then} branch in $\Phi$).
Here, it successfully concludes for each of them, by directly checking the
subtyping.\hfill\qed\end{example}\par The function presented generalizes the version for monomorphic records
given in \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna23a}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} and currently used in Elixir.
Interestingly, the sole difference is the addition of the test ($\tail({\texttt{R}})\in V_{p}$).
Since $V_{p}$ is constant in the function, then the complexity of
this function and of its monomorphic version are the same.
\par\begin{lemma}Let ${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}$ be a monomorphic record type, $V_{p}\subset\mathcal{V}_{r}$
finite and $N$ a finite set of (polymorphic) atomic record types. Then,
$${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\wedge\bigwedge_{\rho\in V_{p}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}\rdef(\rho)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\leq\bigvee_{{\texttt{R}}\in N}{\texttt{R}}\iff{\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\leq\mathbb{0}\mathrel{\mathsf{or}}\Phi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},N).$$\vskip-8.53581pt\end{lemma}\par\begin{proposition}The subtyping algorithm terminates. As a corollary, subtyping is decidable.
\end{proposition}\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Substitutions}
The upcoming descriptions of the type system and of the inference algorithm
rely on type, row, and field substitutions.
\begin{definition}Substitutions, ranged over by $\sigma$, are total mappings from
variables of kind $\kappa$ to terms of kind $\kappa$ (i.e., type
variables to types, field variables to field-types, and row variables of
definition space $L$ to rows of definition space $L$) that are the identity everywhere except on
a finite set of variables. This set is called the \emph{domain}
of the substitution $\sigma$ and is defined as
$\dom(\sigma)=\{\alpha\mid\sigma(\alpha)\neq\alpha\}\cup\{\theta\mid\sigma(\theta)\neq\theta\}\cup\{\rho\mid\sigma(\rho)\neq\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}{\rdef(\rho)}}\}$.
\end{definition}\par\par The application of a substitution $\sigma$ to a term $T$ is denoted by
$T\sigma$.
Notice that the application is defined both on field-types and on rows, the
latter being useful only for tallying.
The application of a substitution must satisfy the following equalities.
\begin{equation*}\begin{array}[]{c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c@{\qquad}c}\alpha\sigma=\sigma(\alpha)\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&b\sigma=b\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&\mathbb{0}\sigma=\mathbb{0}\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&(t_{1}\to t_{2})\sigma=t_{1}\sigma\to t_{2}\sigma\\
\theta\sigma=\sigma(\theta)\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&\bot\sigma=\bot\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&(\neg T)\sigma=\neg(T\sigma)\hfil\hskip 18.49988pt&(T_{1}\lor T_{2})\sigma=T_{1}\sigma\lor T_{2}\sigma\end{array}\vskip-4.2679pt\end{equation*}\begin{equation}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\sigma=\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\tau_{\ell}\sigma)_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\sigma(\rho)\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}},&\text{ if }\varsigma=\rho\\
\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\tau_{\ell}\sigma)_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}},&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}\end{equation}\vskip-4.2679ptWhere $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ and:
\begin{equation*}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L,(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\hskip 9.24994pt\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r_{1}\vee r_{2}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r_{1}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\vee\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r_{2}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\hskip 9.24994pt\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\neg r\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}\neg\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\end{equation*}The equalities above are standard, apart from the one
in \eqref{eq:subrec} which needs a definition for the notation
$\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\tau_{\ell}\sigma)_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\sigma(\rho)\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$,
since $\sigma(\rho)$ is a row rather than a tail. The definition is
given right after \eqref{eq:subrec}
and simply states that $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\tau_{\ell}\sigma)_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\sigma(\rho)\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ stands for the record type obtained by recursively decomposing
the Boolean combinations of the rows in $\sigma(\rho)$, until we arrive at single rows that are
expanded in the record type (recall that rows are inductively defined).
Substitution for rows is defined in the same way as for records (it
suffices to
change the delimiting brackets).
\par As expected, if $\dom(\sigma)=\emptyset$, then $T\sigma=T$.
If $\sigma(\rho)\leq\mathbb{0}$ and $\tail({\texttt{R}})=\rho$,
then ${\texttt{R}}\sigma\leq\mathbb{0}$.
Thanks to the parametric interpretation of types, substitution
preserves subtyping:
\par\begin{proposition}If $t_{1}\leq t_{2}$, then $t_{1}\sigma\leq t_{2}\sigma$ for any row substitution
$\sigma$.
\end{proposition}\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Language}
We define the syntax, static and dynamic semantics of
a record calculus that we prove to be type sound
(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:syntax}) and define a sound and complete typing algorithm for it (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:typealgo}).
\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Syntax and Semantics}
$$\begin{array}[]{lrclr}\textbf{Expressions}&e&\Coloneqq&c\penalty\ |\penalty\ x\penalty\ |\penalty\ ee\penalty\ |\penalty\ \lambda^{\wedge_{i\in I}(t_{i}\to t_{i}^{\prime})}x.e\penalty\ |\penalty\ \texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}e\;\texttt{with}\;\ell\;\texttt{=}\;e\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\penalty\ |\penalty\ e.\ell\penalty\ |\penalty\ e\backslash\ell\\
\textbf{Values}&v&\Coloneqq&c\penalty\ |\penalty\ \lambda^{\wedge_{i\in I}(t_{i}\to t_{i}^{\prime})}x.e\penalty\ |\penalty\ \texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}v\;\texttt{with}\;\ell\;\texttt{=}\;v\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\\
\textbf{Evaluation contexts}&E&\Coloneqq&[]\penalty\ |\penalty\ Ee\penalty\ |\penalty\ vE\penalty\ |\penalty\ \texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}e\;\texttt{with}\;\ell\;\texttt{=}\;E\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\penalty\ |\penalty\ \texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}E\;\texttt{with}\;\ell\;\texttt{=}\;v\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\penalty\ |\penalty\ E.\ell\penalty\ |\penalty\ E\backslash\ell\end{array}$$The syntax above describes a functional language with constants, functions, and
records with field selection ($e.\ell$), addition ($\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}e\;\texttt{with}\;\ell\;\texttt{=}\;e\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$), and deletion ($e\backslash\ell$). As customary in
semantic subtyping, $\lambda$-abstractions are annotated by their type,
which is an
intersection of arrow types. We use
$\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\ell_{1}=e_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}=e_{n}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$ as syntactic sugar for
$\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\dots\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\;\texttt{with}\;\ell_{1}\;\texttt{=}\;e_{1}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\dots\;\texttt{with}\;\ell_{n}\;\texttt{=}\;e_{n}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$.
\par The semantics of the language is given by the call-by-value weak reduction defined
below:
$$\begin{array}[]{rr@{\;}c@{\;}ll}\mathsf{[R}_{\mathsf{app}}\mathsf{]}&(\lambda^{t}x.e)v&\leadsto&e[v/x]\\
\mathsf{[R}_{\mathsf{sel}}^{=}\mathsf{]}&\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}v\;\texttt{with}\;\ell\;\texttt{=}\;v^{\prime}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}.\ell&\leadsto&v^{\prime}\\
\mathsf{[R}_{\mathsf{sel}}^{\neq}\mathsf{]}&\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}v\;\texttt{with}\;\ell^{\prime}\;\texttt{=}\;v^{\prime}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}.\ell&\leadsto&v.\ell&\text{ if }\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}\\
\mathsf{[R}_{\mathsf{del}}^{=}\mathsf{]}&\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}v\;\texttt{with}\;\ell\;\texttt{=}\;v^{\prime}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\backslash\ell&\leadsto&v\backslash\ell\\
\mathsf{[R}_{\mathsf{del}}^{\neq}\mathsf{]}&\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}v\;\texttt{with}\;\ell^{\prime}\;\texttt{=}\;v^{\prime}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\backslash\ell&\leadsto&\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}v\backslash\ell\;\texttt{with}\;\ell^{\prime}\;\texttt{=}\;v^{\prime}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}&\text{ if }\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}\\
\mathsf{[R}_{\mathsf{emp}}\mathsf{]}&\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\backslash\ell&\leadsto&\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\\
\mathsf{[R}_{\mathsf{ctx}}\mathsf{]}&E[e]&\leadsto&E[e^{\prime}]&\text{ if }e\leadsto e^{\prime}\end{array}$$where $e[v/x]$ is the term obtained by standard capture-avoiding substitution of
$v$ for $x$ in $e$, defined modulo
$\alpha$-equivalence.
Notice that the deletion of a label $\ell$ is defined for the empty
record $\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$ but selection is not: selection requires
the presence of the field $\ell$ while deletion does not.
\par\begin{figure}\vskip-1.42262pt\begin{equation*}\frac{}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}c:\text{b}(c)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left=({Const})}\frac{}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}x:\Gamma(x)}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left=({Var}),right=$x\in\dom(\Gamma)$}\vskip-3.98337pt\\
