\frac{(\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}\mid\Gamma,x:t_{i}\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in I}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}\lambda^{\wedge_{i\in I}(t_{i}\to t_{i}^{\prime})}x.e:\textstyle\bigwedge_{i\in I}(t_{i}\to t_{i}^{\prime})}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left=({Abs}),right=$\Delta^{\prime}{=}\vars(\textstyle\bigwedge_{i\in I}t_{i}\to t_{i}^{\prime})$}\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e_{1}:t_{1}\to t_{2}\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e_{2}:t_{1}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e_{1}e_{2}:t_{2}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left=({App})}\frac{}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}:\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\epsilon\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left=({Emp})}\vskip-3.98337pt\\
\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e^{\prime}:t^{\prime}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}e\;\texttt{with}\;\ell\;\texttt{=}\;e^{\prime}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}:\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}t\backslash\ell\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left=({Ext})}\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e\backslash\ell:\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}t\backslash\ell\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left=({Del})}\vskip-3.98337pt\\
\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e.\ell:t}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left=({Sel})}\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t_{1}\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t_{2}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t_{1}\wedge t_{2}}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left=({Inter})}\vskip-3.98337pt\\
\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t^{\prime}\leq t}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left=({Sub})}\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t\sigma}\hskip 9.24994pt\textsc{left=({Inst}),
right={$\dom(\sigma)\cap\Delta=\emptyset$}}\end{equation*}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{1}}{Declarative type system}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 1}}{\small Declarative type system}}}
\end{figure}\par The terms of the language are typed by the declarative type system
in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:decrules}, whose judgments have the form
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t$, where $\Delta\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{\!\textup{fin}}(\mathcal{V}_{t}{\cup}\mathcal{V}_{f}{\cup}\mathcal{V}_{r})$ is a set of
monomorphic variables (i.e., variables that cannot be instantiated)
and $\Gamma$ a type environment from expression variables to types.
\par The rules for the functional part are inspired from those
by\penalty\ \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna14}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}.\ Constants are typed by a given function
$\text{b}$ which maps each constant to its basic type
({const}).
Rule\penalty\ ({Abs}) checks that a function has all the types declared in its
annotation: for each $t_{i}{\to}t_{i}^{\prime}$, it checks that the body $e$ is
of type $t_{i}^{\prime}$ under the environment in
which $x$ is given type $t_{i}$ and the set $\Delta^{\prime}$ of
\emph{all} the variables in the annotation is added to the set of monomorphic
variables
($\vars(t)$ returns the set of type, field, and row variables in
$t$: cf.\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:vars}).
The rules for intersection introduction ({Inter}) and subsumption
({Sub}) are the usual ones: if an expression $e$ has two
types, then it also has their intersection; if an expression $e$ has
type $t^{\prime}$, then it also has any supertype of $t´$ (we use the notation
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t^{\prime}\leq t$ in the premises of a rule, to
indicate that the rule has premise $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t$ and
side condition $t^{\prime}\leq t$). The instantiation rule\penalty\ ({Inst}),
can instantiate any type variable unless it is in the set of monomorphic variables $\Delta$, as this
would be unsound.
\par The new rules of this system are those for record expressions and their
operations. The empty record value has the closed empty record type:
({Emp}). Rule\penalty\ ({Sel}) states that selection is typable only
if the selected field is present, in which case its type is given to the select
expression. Rule\penalty\ ({Ext}) types a strict extension of an expression $e$ of
type $t$ by the expression $e^{\prime}$ on label $\ell$, only if the field $\ell$ is
undefined in $e$, that is, the type of $e$ is a subtype of $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$. Rule\penalty\ ({Del}) states that we can delete a field $\ell$ from
an expression $e$ provided that it is record (i.e., its
type is a subtype of $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$).
\par The types of the expressions typed by\penalty\ ({Ext}) and\penalty\ ({Del}) are
both obtained in similar ways.
First, we compute the operator $t\backslash\ell$ ---whose formal definition we
give below--- which returns $\operatorname{row}(t)$ truncated by the field of label $\ell$.
Then, we put back the field of label $\ell$ with the desired field-type ($t^{\prime}$ or
$\bot$) using the operation $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ we defined in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:rowsub} for substitutions (cf.\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:subrec}).
\par We define the operator $t\backslash\ell$, on DNFs:
let $t=\bigvee_{i\in I}\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P_{i}}{\texttt{R}}\wedge\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in N_{i}}\neg{\texttt{R}}\wedge\bigwedge_{\alpha\in V_{i}}\alpha\wedge\bigwedge_{\alpha\in V_{n}}\neg\alpha$.
Then, $t\backslash\ell\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}\bigvee_{i\in I}\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P_{i}}{\texttt{R}}\backslash\ell\wedge\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in N_{i}}(\neg{\texttt{R}})\backslash\ell$ where for the
literals ${\texttt{R}}$ and $\neg{\texttt{R}}$ the definition is: \\
\centerline{\hbox{$\begin{array}[]{r{c}ll}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}({\ell^{\prime}\!}=\tau_{\ell^{\prime}\!})_{{\ell^{\prime}\!}\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\backslash\ell&\!{=}&\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{\langle}({\ell^{\prime}\!}=\tau_{\ell^{\prime}\!})_{{\ell^{\prime}\!}\in L\smallsetminus\{\ell\}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\{\ell\}}&\text{if }\ell\in L\text{ or }\varsigma\notin\mathcal{V}_{r}\\
\boldsymbol{\langle}({\ell^{\prime}\!}=\tau_{\ell^{\prime}\!})_{{\ell^{\prime}\!}\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\{\ell\}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\\
(\neg\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}({\ell^{\prime}\!}=\tau_{\ell^{\prime}\!})_{{\ell^{\prime}\!}\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}})\backslash\ell&\!{=}&\begin{cases}\neg\boldsymbol{\langle}({\ell^{\prime}\!}=\tau_{\ell^{\prime}\!})_{{\ell^{\prime}\!}\in L\smallsetminus\{\ell\}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\{\ell\}}&\hskip-5.0875pt\text{if }(\ell{\in}L\mathrel{\mathsf{and}}\tau_{\ell}=\mathbb{1}{\lor}\bot)\mathrel{\mathsf{or}}(\ell{\notin}L\mathrel{\mathsf{and}}\varsigma=\textbf{..})\\
\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\{\ell\}}&\hskip-5.0875pt\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\end{array}$}}\\
\noindent First, notice that the type variables in the DNF are simply erased.
This is not restrictive in practice, as intersections with top-level type
variables are used only to implement bounded polymorphism which, as argued in
the introduction, cannot be used for extensions and deletions, these requiring
instead the use of row variables. The definition for the positive
literal ${\texttt{R}}$ consists of two cases. The first case is the intuitive one,
with no interference from a negation or a row variable: the row is
undefined on $\ell$, so we remove the field for $\ell$ (if any) and
index the row by $\{\ell\}$.
In the second case, ${\texttt{R}}$ is polymorphic and $\ell$ is in the definition space of
its tail; therefore, similarly to what we do for type variables, we subsume ${\texttt{R}}$ to an open record
before deleting $\ell$.
For instance, if $x:\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}a=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$,
then the type deduced for $x\backslash b$ is $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}a=\text{{Int}},b=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$:
the information bound to $\rho$ must be forgotten since $b$ is in definition
space of $\rho$.
\par\par Next consider the case of a negative atom $\neg{\texttt{R}}$.
First, suppose ${\texttt{R}}(\ell)\neq\mathbb{1}\lor\bot$ (i.e., either $\ell\in L$ and
$\tau_{\ell}\neq\mathbb{1}\lor\bot$ or $\ell\notin L$ and $\varsigma=\epsilon$).
Then, the type $\neg{\texttt{R}}$ contains, among others, all row elements such that the
field $\ell$ is not of type ${\texttt{R}}(\ell)$ (since ${\texttt{R}}(\ell)\neq\mathbb{1}\lor\bot$,
then there exists at least one such element), and every other field is of
arbitrary value.
Hence, the set obtained from removing the field $\ell$ from these values gives
all quasi-constant functions on $\mathcal{L}\smallsetminus\{\ell\}$, without
restriction: the set entailed by $\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\{\ell\}}$.
Now, when ${\texttt{R}}(\ell)=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot$, there are two possibilities.
The first is $\varsigma=\rho$ and $\ell\in L$.
There is a clash, and we subsume $\neg{\texttt{R}}$ to the
top record type $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ before deleting $\ell$, as we did in the positive case.
Note that $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ is the smallest type to which we can subsume $\neg{\texttt{R}}$.
The second possibility is given by the third case of the definition
(i.e., the first case for the negated literal).
There, it is impossible to have a value of a type different from
${\texttt{R}}(\ell)=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot$.
Hence, the constraints on the type must be on the other fields: we
simply remove $\ell$ from the definition definition space of the row.
\par\par\par The language satisfies the property of soundness.
Its proof is routine (subject reduction and progress, using inversion and generation
lemmas) and can be found in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{app:language}.
\begin{theorem}Let $e$ be a well-typed closed expression, that is,
$\emptyset\mid\emptyset\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t$ for some $t$.
Then either $e$ diverges or it reduces to a value of type $t$.
\end{theorem}
\par\begin{figure}\begin{equation*}\vskip-8.53581pt\frac{\left(\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}\mid\Gamma,x:t_{i}\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e:t_{i}^{\prime\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}}t_{i}^{\prime}\right)_{i\in I}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}\lambda^{\wedge_{i\in I}(t_{i}\to t_{i}^{\prime})}x.e:\textstyle\bigwedge_{i\in I}(t_{i}\to t_{i}^{\prime})}\quad\textsc{left=({Abs}),right=$\Delta^{\prime}{=}\vars(\textstyle\bigwedge_{i\in I}t_{i}\to t_{i}^{\prime})$}\\
\vskip-8.53581pt\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e_{1}:t_{1}\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e_{2}:t_{2}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e_{1}e_{2}:t}\quad\textsc{left=({App}),right=$t\in t_{1}\bullet_{\Delta}t_{2}$}\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e:t}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e\backslash\ell:\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}}\quad\textsc{left=({Del}),
right=$r\in t\odot_{\Delta}\ell$}\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e:t}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e.\ell:t_{\ell}}\quad\textsc{left=({Sel}),
right=$t_{\ell}\in\amalg_{\Delta}^{\ell}(t)$}\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e:t\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e^{\prime}:t^{\prime}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}e\;\texttt{with}\;\ell\;\texttt{=}\;e^{\prime}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}:\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}}\quad\textsc{left=({Ext}),
right=$r\in t\odot_{\Delta}^{\bot}\ell$}\end{equation*}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{2}}{Algorithmic type
system (plus ({Const}), ({Var}), ({Emp}),
which are as in\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:decrules})}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 2}}{\small Algorithmic type
system (plus ({Const}), ({Var}), ({Emp}),
which are as in\penalty\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:decrules})}}}\end{figure}
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Algorithmic Type System} The system in
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:decrules} is not algorithmic: it is not syntax-directed and some of
its rules are not analytic.
In \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:algrules}, we give an algorithmic system that is sound
and complete with respect to the system in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:decrules} (we
omitted three rules that are the same as in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:decrules}).
The new system includes the algorithmic counterparts of the typing rules for record
operations which, apart from ({Emp}), must be changed to
account for the fact that there is no ({Inst}) rule in the
algorithmic system and, thus, instantiation must be performed by the
algorithmic elimination rules.
For instance, in the declarative system, if $x:\alpha$ and $\alpha\not\in\Delta$, then
$x.\ell:\text{{Int}}$ can be deduced by instantiating by ({Inst}) $\alpha$ to $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
In the algorithmic system, in the absence of ({Inst}), this instantiation must be done within
the algorithmic
rule\penalty\ ({Sel}).
For all record operations, the algorithmic system needs to perform a possible
instantiation of the type of the record. This is done in the side
conditions of the rules for record operations by the following
operators (that we explain after the \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:c1} for $\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}$), which instantiate
the type of the record to match the conditions in the declarative system:
\@@amsalign
These side conditions are primarily of theoretical interest to satisfy
completeness. They address the case of record expressions that
return (parametric) polymorphic values (e.g., of type $\alpha$), which
are never encountered in practice. Consequently, the sets in
these side conditions are never computed in practice. In a practical setting, the rules ({Ext}) and
({Del}) of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:decrules} should be used instead, while the rule
to use in practice for selection has premises $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e:t$, side-condition $t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\mathbb{1}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$, and conclusion $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e.\ell:t.\ell$. Keeping the rules of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:decrules}, we loose completeness, but only in theory, and we gain in
efficiency (\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Appendix B.3)} discusses this point in detail for type polymorphism).
\par In any case, selection uses a new type operator $t.\ell$ (theoretically, in its
side condition to compute $t^{\prime}$; in practical setting, in its conclusion).
Since the algorithmic type system does not
include a subsumption rule, we cannot assume that the type $t$ deduced for the
expression $e$ in ({Sel}) will be a record type atom of the form required
by the declarative system (i.e., $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$): in general, $t$ will be a
union of intersections of such atoms, type variables, and their negations. Thus
the rule checks that $t$ is a record type in which the field $\ell$ is surely
defined, and delegates to the operator $t.\ell$ (defined below) the computation
of the type of the result.
\begin{definition}Let $t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\mathbb{1}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ be a DNF.
We define the selection of the field $\ell$ of $t$ as
$(\bigvee_{i\in I}t_{i}).\ell\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}\bigvee_{i\in I}t_{i}.\ell$
and\vskip-5.69054pt$$(\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P}{\texttt{R}}\wedge\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in N}\neg{\texttt{R}}\wedge\bigwedge_{\alpha\in V_{p}}\alpha\wedge\bigwedge_{\alpha\in V_{n}}\neg\alpha).\ell\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}\bigvee_{N^{\prime}\subseteq N}\Bigg(\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P}{\texttt{R}}(\ell)\wedge\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in N^{\prime}}\neg{\texttt{R}}(\ell)\Bigg)$$\end{definition}
The condition $t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\mathbb{1}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ assures that $t.\ell\leq\mathbb{1}$,
so that selection always returns a type (and not a generic field-type).
Indeed, $t.\ell$ is equivalent to $\min\{t^{\prime}\mid t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\}$ (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{app:typealgo}).
Once more, the presence of top-level
intersections with type variables does not play any role in selection.
\par To finish explaining the algorithmic system, we need to introduce
the notations $\Vdash t\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t^{\prime}$ and $t\bullet_{\Delta}t^{\prime}$,
whose definitions are taken verbatim from\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}:
\begin{definition}Let $t$ and $t^{\prime}$ be two types and $\Delta$ a set of variables.
\@@amsalign
\end{definition}
\par\begin{definition}Let $t_{1}$ and $t_{2}$ be two types with $t\leq\mathbb{0}\to\mathbb{1}$, and $\Delta$ a set of variables.
We define $t_{1}\bullet_{\Delta}t_{2}$ as the set of types for which there exist two
sets of type substitutions (for variables not in $\Delta$) that make $t_{2}$
compatible with the domain of $t_{1}$ (defined below):
$$t_{1}\bullet_{\Delta}t_{2}\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}\left\{\penalty\ t\penalty\ \penalty\ \begin{array}[]{|l}{[\sigma_{j}]_{j\in J}}\Vdash t_{1}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}\mathbb{0}\to\mathbb{1}\\
{[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}}\Vdash t_{2}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}\mathbf{dom}(\bigwedge_{j\in J}t_{1}\sigma_{j})\\
t=\textstyle\bigwedge_{j\in J}t_{1}\sigma_{j}\cdot\bigwedge_{i\in I}t_{2}\sigma_{i}\end{array}\right\}$$Where $t_{1}\cdot t_{2}\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}\min\{t\mid t\leq t_{1}\to t_{2}\}$.
For an arrow type $t^{\prime}\leq\mathbb{0}\to\mathbb{1}$,
we have
$t^{\prime}\simeq\bigvee_{i\in I}(\bigwedge_{p\in P_{i}}(t_{p}\to t^{\prime}_{p})\wedge\bigwedge_{n\in N_{i}}\neg(t_{n}\to t^{\prime}_{n})\wedge\bigwedge_{\alpha\in V^{p}_{i}}\alpha\wedge\bigwedge_{\alpha\in V^{n}_{i}}\neg\alpha)$, and define
$\mathbf{dom}(t^{\prime})=\bigwedge_{i\in I}\bigvee_{p\in P_{i}}t_{p}$.
\end{definition}
These two definitions are used in the rule ({App}), the key rule for the
algorithmic system (which again is taken verbatim from \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} where
more details can be found).
Essentially, ({App}) merges together intersection elimination (in this
case the standard terminology is \emph{expansion}), instantiation, and subsumption.
For the application $e_{1}e_{2}$ to be well typed, the type of the function must be
a functional type (i.e., a subtype of $\mathbb{0}\to\mathbb{1}$, the type of all
functions) whose domain is a supertype of the type of the argument.
Therefore, the rule looks for two finite sets of type substitutions for the
variables not in $\Delta$, that make the type of the function subtype of
$\mathbb{0}\to\mathbb{1}$ and the type of the argument subtype of the function's domain.
This search is collapsed in the definition of $t_{1}\bullet_{\Delta}t_{2}$.
Concretely, this operation finds two sets of substitutions $[\sigma_{j}]_{j\in J}$ and
$[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}$ such that (1) $\bigwedge_{j\in J}t_{1}\sigma_{j}\leq\mathbb{0}\to\mathbb{1}$ (this corresponds to the notation $t_{1}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}\mathbb{0}\to\mathbb{1}$ of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:c1}) and (2)\penalty\ $\bigwedge_{i\in I}t_{2}\sigma_{i}$ is a subtype of the domain of $\bigwedge_{j\in J}t_{1}\sigma_{j}$.
It then returns all the types of the result of the application of such two types.
\par The operator $\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}$ is also used to type
record operations, since it is used by the side conditions of the algorithmic
rules ({Del}), ({Sel}), and ({Ext}): when applying
an operation on a record expression $e$ of type $t$, these side
conditions check whether there exists a set of substitutions making $t$
a subtype of, depending on the rule, $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\mathbb{1}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$, $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$, or $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ and, if so, they apply the corresponding operation on the
instantiation of $t$. For instance, $\amalg_{\Delta}^{\ell}(t)$ looks for some
substitutions $[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}$ such that $\bigwedge_{i\in I}t\sigma_{i}\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\mathbb{1}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ (i.e., a solution for $t\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\mathbb{1}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$) and returns its projection on $\ell$.
\par Finally, notice that, even if the typing rules\penalty\ ({Abs}) and\penalty\ ({App}) themselves are not new,
the process behind $t_{1}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t_{2}$ and $t_{1}\bullet_{\Delta}t_{2}$ are.
The novelty is that these operators now infer type substitutions that range not only over types,
but also over rows and field-types.
In the next section, we describe how to adapt the existing algorithms
to our framework.
\par As expected, the algorithmic type system is sound and complete with respect to
the declarative one, as stated by the following theorems (proofs are given in
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{app:typealgo}):\vskip-2.84526pt
\begin{theorem}If $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e:t$, then $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t$.\vskip-5.69054pt\end{theorem}\vskip-1.42262pt
\begin{theorem}If $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t$, then there is $t^{\prime}$ such that
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e:t^{\prime}$ and $t^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t$.\vskip-1.42262pt\end{theorem}
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Tallying}
\par The algorithmic type system we have defined is parametric in
the decision procedure $s\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t$,
which looks for an appropriate set of substitutions $[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}$ such that
$\bigwedge_{i\in I}s\sigma_{i}\leq t$.
While this has been tackled for type variables by \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}},
here we need to extend the procedure to row and field variables
along the lines we outline below (for a full description, see the Appendix\penalty\ \ref{app:tallying}).
\par Deciding $t_{1}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t_{2}$ is done by testing the cardinality of the
set of substitutions $[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}$ we are looking for, by incremental
steps.
For $t_{1}\bullet_{\Delta}t_{2}$, two sets of substitutions are sought for, and we can
follow a dove-tail order (more details are in
\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, \lx@sectionsign 3.2.2-3.2.3)}).
\par For each cardinality, we apply an instance of the \emph{tallying} algorithm.
Tallying is a unification problem acting on inequalities.
Given an initial set of subtyping constraints, tallying looks for a substitution
that satisfies these constraints.
For instance, deciding $t_{1}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t_{2}$ is done first by trying to solve the
tallying problem for the constraint $(t_{1},\leq,t_{2})$, looking for a singleton
substitution set.
In case of a (non-fatal) failure, the next step is to look for a set of two
substitutions such that $[\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}]\Vdash t_{1}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t_{2}$, which is
equivalent to solving the tallying problem for the constraint
$(t_{1}^{1}\wedge t_{1}^{2},\leq,t_{2})$, where each $t_{1}^{i}$ is obtained from $t_{1}$ by
replacing all variables not in $\Delta$ by fresh ones.
\par Tallying returns a set of constraint-sets.
Indeed, the presence of subtyping, and in particular of the empty type,
implies that to solve a single set of constraints $C$ we may need to generate
several sets of constraints, yielding different solutions.
For instance, solving
$\{(\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell_{1}=t_{\ell_{1}},\ell_{2}=t_{\ell_{2}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}},\leq,\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell_{1}=t^{\prime}_{\ell_{1}},\ell_{2}=t^{\prime}_{\ell_{2}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}})\}$
generates three independent subproblems:
$\{(t_{\ell_{1}},\leq,\mathbb{0})\}$, $\{(t_{\ell_{2}},\leq,\mathbb{0})\}$ and
$\{(t_{\ell_{1}},\leq,t^{\prime}_{\ell_{1}}),(t_{\ell_{2}},\leq,t^{\prime}_{\ell_{2}})\}$.
\par We define the solving procedure for the type tallying of a
constraint-set as an extension of the existing one for type variables.
The procedure follows the steps that are given by\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} (plus
an additional one), namely:
$(1)$ normalization, $(2)$ merging and saturation, $(3)$ harmonization (which is new and specific to row variables), $(4)$ transformation of constraints into equations, and $(5)$\penalty\ creation of the substitution solutions. For space reasons, in what follows we only outline these steps.
For formal definitions, refer to the Appendixes\penalty\ \ref{sec:normalization}
to\penalty\ \ref{sec:solution}.
\par A constraint is defined as a triple $(T_{1},c,T_{2})$ ($c\in\{\leq,\geq\}$) such that $T_{1}$ and $T_{2}$ are two terms of
the same kind (type, field-type, or rows of the same domain).
An arbitrary order on variables is given as an input to the algorithm in order
to guarantee well-formedness of the solution.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Normalization}
The first step of tallying decomposes the initial constraints on types
into a set $\mathcal{S}$ of \emph{normalized} constraint-sets. These
are constraint-sets where all constraints are of the shape
$(X,c,T)$, where $T$ stands for a type, field-type or row and
$X$ for a variable of the kind of $T$.
\par Normalization works by decomposing types into elementary constraints.
For records and rows, it might be necessary to decompose the row variables across
different fields.
For instance, the record $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{0}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ might need to be decomposed
over, say, labels
$\ell_{1}$ and $\ell_{2}$.
In that case, we will spread the row variable $\rho_{0}$ into
$\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell_{1}=\rho_{0}.\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=\rho_{0}.\ell_{2}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{0}\backslash{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$, introducing $\rho.\ell_{1}$, $\rho.\ell_{2}$ and
$\rho_{0}\backslash{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}}$ as field and row variables with special names.
Then, the tallying algorithm may give constraints over $\rho_{0}.\ell_{1}$,
$\rho_{0}.\ell_{2}$ and $\rho_{0}\backslash{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}}$,
and we expect a solution of the shape $\sigma(\rho_{0})=\boldsymbol{\langle}\ell_{1}=\tau_{1},\ell_{2}=\tau_{2}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset}$, where each $\tau_{i}$ has been obtained
from the constraints on $\rho_{0}.\ell_{i}$, and $r$ from the ones on
$\rho_{0}\backslash{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}}$.
\par In the literature where rows are all atomic,
unification of a row variable with another row is performed component-wise.
Considering each field independently, while it works well with atomic rows, is
unsound when working with Boolean combination of rows, as shown by this example:
\begin{example}Let $\text{{result}}=\boldsymbol{\langle}\text{{log}}=\text{{String}},\text{{succ}}=\text{{True}},\text{{val}}=\mathbb{1}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\epsilon\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset}\vee\boldsymbol{\langle}\text{{log}}=\text{{String}},\text{{succ}}=\text{{False}},\text{{val}}=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\epsilon\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset}$.
For the constraint
$(\boldsymbol{\langle}\text{{val}}=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset},\leq,\text{{result}})$,
independent component-wise tallying gives
$\sigma(\rho)=\boldsymbol{\langle}\text{{log}}=\text{{String}},\text{{succ}}=\text{{Bool}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\epsilon\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\{\text{{val}}\}}$ as a solution, which does not verify the
constraint (the type obtained as ``solution'' contains record values in
which \text{{succ}} is \text{{True}}{} and \text{{val}} is undefined, which are
not included in \text{{result}}).
Indeed, such a procedure effectively solves tallying for
$(\boldsymbol{\langle}\text{{val}}=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset},\leq,\boldsymbol{\langle}\text{{log}}=\text{{String}}\lor\text{{String}},\text{{succ}}=\text{{True}}\lor\text{{False}},\text{{val}}=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\epsilon\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset})$ instead.\hfill\qed\end{example}
\par To obtain a sound decomposition of rows, we can adapt the formula underlying our
subtyping algorithm (this formula is found in the statement of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:subtyping}).
Given a constraint $C$ on DNFs of rows, we still consider the set $L$ of all top-level
labels in the DNFs (in the last example, $L=\{\text{{log}},\text{{succ}},\text{{val}}\}$), for which we will end up with independent constraints and
constraints over the cofinine domain of the rows deducted from $L$.
Doing so, we find no solution for the constraint in
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{ex:componentwise_unsound}, which is correct, since indeed the constraint cannot be satisfied.
\par Although this method yields a correct set of solutions, this set is
far from being complete: the more fields we decompose over, the more solutions
we might loose.
Yet, the previous method decomposes records and rows over the whole set of
top-level labels.
Our solution is to decompose rows over a set of labels as small as possible.
This technique is based on a general decomposition formula given in the
statement of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:decomposition}.
Now, while normalization is sound,
the algorithm is still not complete, due to the potentially necessary
decomposition of some row variables.
\begin{example}Consider the constraint-set
$\{(\boldsymbol{\langle}\text{{log}}=\text{{String}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset}\to\boldsymbol{\langle}\text{{log}}=\text{{String}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset},\geq,\text{{result}}\to\text{{result}}),(\boldsymbol{\langle}\text{{log}}=\text{{String}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{2}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset}\to\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset},\geq,\boldsymbol{\langle}\text{{succ}}=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot,\text{{val}}=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset}\to\boldsymbol{\langle}\text{{succ}}=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot,\text{{val}}=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{1}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset})\}$.
Let $\rho_{1}$ be of smaller order than $\rho_{2}$.
By a decomposition over $L=\{\text{{log}}\}$ in the first constraint
and $L=\{\text{{succ}},\text{{val}}\}$ in the second constraint,
we derive the constraint-set (omitting trivial constraints)
$\{(\text{{String}},\leq,\rho_{1}.\text{{log}}),(\rho_{2}\backslash{\{\text{{succ}},\text{{val}}\}},\leq,\rho_{1}\backslash{\{\text{{log}}\}}),(\rho_{2},\leq,\text{{result}}),(\rho_{2},\geq,\text{{result}})\}$.
\par As we describe below (cf.\ \emph{Harmonization}), a further step of the tallying algorithm harmonizes
the decomposition of the row variables across all constraints.
In particular, it decomposes $\rho_{2}$ over $\{\text{{succ}},\text{{val}}\}$ in
the constraints $(\rho_{2},\leq,\text{{result}})$ and
$(\rho_{2},\geq,\text{{result}})$.
Since when decomposing $\rho_{2}$ in this way, no solution might apply
to both of these constraints ($\rho_{2}$ needs to be instantiated exactly to the
union type $\text{{result}}$), tallying fails.
The solution mapping $\rho_{2}$ to $\text{{result}}$ is not found.\hfill\qed\end{example}
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Constraint merging and saturation}
After normalization, a constraint set may have for the same variable $X$
different constraints of the form $T_{1}\leq X,\dots,T_{n}\leq X$ and $X\leq T^{\prime}_{1},\dots X\leq T^{\prime}_{m}$:
we replace them by two constraints $\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq n}T_{i}\leq X$ and
$X\leq\bigwedge_{1\leq j\leq m}T_{j}^{\prime}$, then we
add $\bigvee_{1\leq i\leq n}T_{i}\leq\bigwedge_{1\leq j\leq m}T_{j}^{\prime}$ to (i.e., saturate) the constraint set, and we normalize again.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Harmonization}
Take a constraint-set $C$ with $(\rho\backslash{L_{1}},c,r)\in C$,
$L_{1}\subsetneq L$ and $L$ the set of labels appearing in the names of variables
$\rho^{\prime}.\ell$ and $\rho^{\prime}\backslash{L_{2}}$ in $C$.
As mentioned in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{ex:incomplete}, harmonization of constraint-set rewrites
the constraint $(\rho\backslash{L_{1}},c,r)$ into $(\rho\backslash{L},c,r)$ and feeds this constraint to normalization again.
Harmonization ends with a constraint-set of homogeneous domain, where for each
initial row variable $\rho$ all occurrences of $\rho\backslash{L}$ are defined on
(i.e., harmonized to) the same $L$.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Equations generation}
At this point, for each type and field variable $X$ there are two unique
constraints of the form $T\leq X$ and $X\leq T^{\prime}$, that we transform into the equation
$X=(T\vee X^{\prime})\wedge T^{\prime}$ with $X^{\prime}$ fresh.
For a row variable $\rho$, there is a set of labels $L$ and constraints of the
form $\tau_{1}\leq\rho.\ell\leq\tau_{2}$ for each $\ell\in L$, and a constraint
$r_{1}\leq\rho\backslash{L}\leq r_{2}$.
We define the terms
$\tau_{\ell}=(\tau_{1}\vee\theta_{\rho}^{\ell})\wedge\tau_{2}$ and
$r=(r_{1}\vee\rho^{\prime})\wedge r_{2}$, with fresh variables $\theta_{\rho}^{\ell}$
and $\rho^{\prime}$.
We finally create an equation $\rho=\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}\rdef(\rho)}$.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Solution}
We solve the equations following the given order on the variables on the
left-hand side, by collecting the equations $X=T$ and replacing in
all other equations $X$ by $\mu X^{\prime}.(T\{X^{\prime}/X\})$
with $X^{\prime}$ fresh.
Thanks to the order, the type $\mu X^{\prime}.(T\{X^{\prime}/X\})$
is contractive and, thus, well-formed.
\par A solution to a constraint-set $C$ is a substitution $\sigma$ such that
$\forall(T_{1},\leq,T_{2}){\in}C\,.\,T_{1}\sigma\leq T_{2}\sigma$ and
$\forall(T_{1},\geq,T_{2}){\in}C\,.\,T_{1}\sigma\geq T_{2}\sigma$ hold.
If $\sigma$ is a solution to $C$, we write $\sigma\Vdash C$.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Properties of the algorithm}
\par We call $\mathbf{\mathrm{Sol}}_{\Delta}(C)$ the solving procedure for the type tallying of $C$.
We write $\mathbf{\mathrm{Sol}}_{\Delta}(C)\rightsquigarrow\Theta$ if $\mathbf{\mathrm{Sol}}_{\Delta}(C)$ terminates yielding the set of
substitutions $\Theta$, called the \emph{solution} of the type tallying problem for $C$.
\vskip-2.27621pt
\begin{theorem}Let $C$ be a constraint-set.
If $\mathbf{\mathrm{Sol}}_{\Delta}(C)\rightsquigarrow\Theta$, then for all $\sigma\in\Theta$,
$\sigma\Vdash C$.
\end{theorem}
\vskip-7.39772pt
\begin{theorem}Let $C$ be a constraint-set.
Then $\mathbf{\mathrm{Sol}}_{\Delta}(C)$ terminates.
\end{theorem}
\vskip-7.39772pt
\begin{proposition}Let $C$ be a constraint-set and $\mathbf{\mathrm{Sol}}_{\Delta}(C)\rightsquigarrow\Theta$. Then
(1) $\Theta$ is finite and
(2) for all $\sigma\in\Theta$ and for all $X\in\dom(\sigma)$,
the types in $\sigma(X)$ are contractive.
\end{proposition}
\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Related Work}
\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Row polymorphism}
Our formalization of records as quasi-constant total functions and the inclusion
of row polymorphism are directly inspired from the formalism of \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{remy89,remy94}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}.
Remy's work contains neither set-theoretic types nor subtyping, and
therefore commutation of fields is obtained by structural equations.
Unlike our case, the types in \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{remy94}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} are not recursive.
An extension of this system to recursive types is given by \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{remy98}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} when
describing a type system for objects.
\par Before Rémy, \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{wand87}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} introduced row polymorphism to type object
inheritance.
His type inference algorithm, corrected in \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{wand91}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, considers free
record extension (i.e., right priority record concatenation) but lacks principal solutions.
Instead, it deduces a finite set of solutions of which all types of the term
under consideration are instances, as we also do.
However, unlike us, Wand's type grammar lacks intersection types, so
it is not possible to merge the multiple
solutions into a single type, as we instead do.
In an earlier attempt of our work we considered free record extension, as it is present both in CDuce and in its generalization by\penalty\ \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna23a}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}, but this made the theory much
more involved, in particular tallying.
It would be worthwhile to study this possibility again, now that the theory
with strict extension is precisely laid down. However, we anticipate this study to be quite complex: typing the concatenation of two polymorphic records presents significant challenges. It will likely require enriching the type algebra to explicitly represent row concatenations (row variables included), as the type operators used in CDuce for typing record concatenation are insufficient for this purpose.
\par(Syntactic) subtyping is present in the work of \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{cardelli91}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}.
Operations on records (deletion, selection, extension) are directly defined
within the syntax of types.
In our system instead, we compute these operations on the types during typing.
It is thus currently impossible to postpone the extension or
deletion of a field with label $\ell$ that is affected by a row variable, until
the point where the row variable will be instantiated and, in that case, we must resort to an approximation.
Cardelli and Mitchell must however define syntactic equivalence relations on
operators and fields, and their system lacks principal typing, as well.
\par Row polymorphism and extensible records are implemented using predicate on
types by \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{harper91}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} and, later, by \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{gaster96}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} under the name qualified types.
In the latter, positive information is given in the type and negative
information (absent field) in the predicate.
\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{morris19}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} use qualified types with uninterpreted predicates for
concatenation and membership of fields.
Their system can be instantiated to most of the standard approaches of the literature.
We aimed to minimize changes to the type syntax of CDuce and Elixir, opting to refrain from incorporating qualified types.
On the theory side, we wished to keep the implementation of records as total
functions---as already present in the semantic subtyping framework---while in
systems with qualified types, records are constructed from partial functions.
\par A convenient way to define extensible records is with scoped labels
\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{leijen05}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, where labels may appear several times in a record, the most
recent occurrence shadowing earlier ones.
\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{paszke23}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} recently extended the formalism to deal with first-class labels,
first-class rows and concatenation.
This gives a simple formulation of types, albeit too syntactic for our
semantic approach to typing and subtyping, and our desire to interpret records as
quasi-constant functions.
Using scoped labels would impose more structure on the interpretation of
records, and therefore could weaken the intuitive understanding of set-like
operations and types as sets.
\par Variants, the dual of records, are studied in a semantic subtyping setting by
\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna16}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}, where they show that adopting full-fledged union (and
intersection and negation) types as well as let-polymorphism gives a
more intuitive and expressive theory of polymorphic variants.
However, as we showed in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:need}, our semantic interpretation seems
to break this duality, insofar as these same features are not enough to encode record
polymorphism too.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Presence polymorphism}
\par\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{remy94}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} describes records types with fields that can be either present or absent,
as indicated by an additional annotation.
He shows how to add presence polymorphism over these annotations, yielding
records parametric in the absence or presence of some
fields.
\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{garrigue15}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} proposes a weaker system,
where constraints apply to single variables (rather than being distributed over row
and presence variables), and where absent fields are determined
by intersecting type constraints. \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{garrigue15}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} justifies this choice by the fact that it
yields types that are simpler to understand, since they do not require
different variables of different sorts. This simplicity is the reason why it is
the system used for OCaml. Our work shares
Garrigue's simplicity, since only row variables are
visible to the programmer and, under the hood, field-type variables---which range over types augmented with $\bot$---provide
a natural notion of presence polymorphism. Field-type variables can be instantiated with
$\bot$ for an absent field, with a type $t$ for a mandatory field, but also with
$t\lor\bot$ for an optional field of type $t$.
To our knowledge, our work is the first to allow presence polymorphism over
optional fields: in the existing literature, instantiated field-types of polymorphic records
can just be either present or absent, but not both.
Moreover, integrating presence polymorphism in our framework is
straightforward since field-type variables are almost handled as
ordinary type
variables: it is the kinding system that enables the extra $\bot$
possibility (for an example-based comparison between presence
polymorphism of \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{remy94}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} and ours, see \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:app-presence}).
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Relation between different kinds of polymorphism}
\par Our type system features three kinds of parametric polymorphism: on types, rows, and field-types.
It also features subtype polymorphism and ad-hoc polymorphism via
intersection and union types. The question naturally arises: how are these concepts interconnected, and where do they overlap?
It is folklore that unrestricted intersections combined with parametric
polymorphism encode a form of bounded quantification, as described in the
introduction (see also\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna24}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Section 2)}).
\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{tang23}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} formally compare the expressiveness of row and presence
polymorphism to \emph{structural} subtyping, for calculi with records and
variants.
More precisely, they encode diverse subtyping using relevant polymorphic
systems.
Whether their techniques can be adapted to gather insights into semantic
subtyping, or polymorphism in the semantic subtyping framework, remains an open question.
\par\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{xie20}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} show that row and bounded polymorphism can be encoded with
disjoint polymorphism, obtained by adding parametric polymorphism to a system
with \emph{disjoint} intersections, and having a disjointedness predicate in the
quantification of types.
Contrary to our intersections, that are uninhabited if applied to separate types,
disjoint intersections type a merge operator that generalizes the disjoint
concatenation of extensible data types, like records, to arbitrary types.
The deletion operator of extensible data types can also be generalized to
arbitrary types using disjoint polymorphism and a merge operator. This operator
once again differs from our set exclusion operation $t_{1}\smallsetminus t_{2}=t_{1}\wedge\neg t_{2}$.
\par\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{parreaux22}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} present MLstruct, an extension with set-theoretic types of
MLsub\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{dolan17}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, based on \emph{algebraic subtyping}.
Their approach differs from ours, as it provides less expressive subtyping and type connectives.
For instance, intersections cannot type general overloaded functions, and a
union of records in MLstruct is equivalent to the single record with the union
applied component-wise.
Keeping unions of records distinct is a crucial feature of our system (cf., the code in lines
\ref{23} to \ref{29} in the introduction) and a major difficulty for the
tallying problem.
The gain from this reduced expressivity is the existence of principal types (a property absent in semantic subtyping even for simple monomorphic types),
the ability to encode record extension and deletion without the need
for row polymorphism,
and maintaining the duality between variants and records.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Effect systems} Record calculi and row polymorphism had a significant impact on
effect systems. As suggested by an anonymous reviewer, our work may usefully apply to that setting. For instance, in the language Koka\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{leijen14}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, the introduction of row polymorphism for effects is justified by the fact that inference cannot solve constraints for unions. This yields to approximations as the ones we pointed out in the last examples of Section\penalty\ \ref{sec:need}. But in our system we are able to keep separate unions while having row polymorphism. This should in principle allow the system to track effects more finely than in\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{leijen14}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}. For instance, consider this example directly derived from\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{leijen14}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}{\small\begin{alltt}
\ function foo(f,g) \lx@text@lbrace if (random()) then \lx@text@lbrace f(); error("hi") \lx@text@rbrace else \lx@text@lbrace g(); print("hi") \lx@text@rbrace \lx@text@rbrace
\end{alltt}}
If $t_{1}\to\varepsilon\,t_{2}$ is the type of functions that can be applied to an input $t_{1}$ and produce an output of type $t_{2}$ and effect $\varepsilon$, then using our row polymorphism we should be able to give the example above the type $\forall\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}.(()\to\langle\penalty\ |\epsilon_{1}\rangle\,(),()\to\langle\penalty\ |\epsilon_{2}\rangle\,())\to((\langle\text{rnd},\text{exn}|\epsilon_{1}\rangle\cup\langle\text{rnd},\text{io}|\epsilon_{2}\rangle)\;())$, which states that {foo} can either produce the {exn} effect (by erroring) or the {io} effect (by printing) but not both. The system for Koka would not be able to distinguish the two effects.
This suggests that exploring the use of our system in effect systems could be a fruitful direction for future work.
\par\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Practice}
\par Efficient compilation of polymorphic or extensible records
has been widely explored by researchers such as \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{gaster96,ohori95}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}.
These works advocate moving away from Rémy's formalism.
Ohori's calculus, in particular, stores information in elaborated kinds.
It was expanded to extensible records by \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{alves21}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}, but with no
mention of the compilation method.
Yet, \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{hillerstroem16}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} provide a compelling abstract machine for a calculus
employing this formalism, serving as a foundation for their language Links.
Regarding general-purpose languages, several of them propose either
a flavor of set-theoretic types (like Typescript \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{typescript}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} or Flow
\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{facebookflow}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}), sometimes based on the theory of semantic subtyping
(CDuce, Luau\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{luausemsub}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, Elixir\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{elixirtypes}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, or Ballerina\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{ballerina-typing}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}), or polymorphic extensible records (like
Purescript \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{freeman13}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} or OCaml) but, to our knowledge, none of them
offers both of these features, as we propose in this work.
\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par
\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Conclusion}
\par We designed a type system featuring set-theoretic types and semantic
subtyping for record calculi with row and presence polymorphism.
We instantiated this type system on a specific \textlambda-calculus incorporating
record selection, extension, and deletion, and devised a unification ({tallying}) algorithm.
We believe there are at least five key takeaways from our work:
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@\emph{Intersection types and parametric polymorphism fall short}: while their combination is expressive, it cannot fully address practical examples that row polymorphism can handle effectively.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@\emph{Surprising similarity in subtyping algorithms}: the subtyping algorithms for monomorphic and polymorphic records are strikingly and disturbingly similar. This similarity is ``disturbing'' because of the extensive effort we had to put in to finally prove that the polymorphic case is obtained from the monomorphic case by adding just a single non-recursive case that tests whether a row variable belongs to a finite set.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@\emph{Comparable type inference}: from an algorithmic and complexity perspective, type inference for row variable polymorphism closely parallels that for parametric polymorphism.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@\emph{Distinct semantics}: records represent total functions on the set of all labels, while rows (and consequently row variables) represent partial functions. This subtle distinction introduces significant challenges and subtleties in the formal treatment.
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@\emph{Set-theoretic types disrupt type dualities}: incorporating
set-theoretic types and semantic subtyping undermines the traditional duality between variant and record types.
\end{enumerate}
Our next goal is to implement our results in the CDuce compiler, thereby enhancing the language with row polymorphism. We closely adhered to the
theory used for CDuce, in that
record types were simply extended with row variables and presence polymorphism,
leveraging the union connective and an existing constant for undefinedness.
Our model and algorithms, specifically for subtyping and tallying, naturally extend
the existing ones, therefore their implementation will not affect the performance of the type-checker.
While we did not address the problem of type
reconstruction, \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna24b}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} provide the theory and an
implementation of type reconstruction for an ML-like language that uses
CDuce's tallying library as a black box. We are confident that
integrating our tallying algorithm into that system and adding records to
the language should yield, with some extra effort, a
reconstruction system with polymorphic records.
\par CDuce is, of course, more complex than our record calculus.
Likewise, any type system for a dynamic language needs to
account for features like pattern matching, type cases, guards, or type narrowing.
At first sight, these
features seem mostly orthogonal to the introduction of row polymorphism.
Our hope is to be able to integrate the latter seamlessly into any existing
set-theoretic type system with semantic subtyping, such as Elixir
\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna24a}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, Ballerina \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{ballerina-typing}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, Luau \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{luausemsub}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, or Erlang
\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{schimpf22}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}. We are closely monitoring the ongoing efforts to port
CDuce's tallying algorithm into the Elixir compiler.
\par Our record calculus lacks first-class labels.
\cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna23a}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} defines a system in which records can be
used both as ``structs'' (i.e., records without first-class labels) and as
dictionaries/maps with first-class labels. His work is carried out in the
same setting as ours, set-theoretic types and
records as quasi-constant functions, but without row polymorphism. However, some
solutions proposed by \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna23a}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} are explicitly motivated by
having a system that could be easily extended with row and presence
polymorphism, which is why we believe that merging the two systems
should not pose any fundamental issue.
The actual expressiveness of a system with first-class labels where operations on
records are not part of the syntax of types remains to be investigated.
\par

our examples in Section~\refsec:example}. We also thank the anonymous
reviewers for their insightful comments and suggestions, especially one
reviewer who highlighted the connection with effect systems discussed in
