\lx@newpage\par\par\@@numbered@section{appendix}{toc}{APPENDIX FOR TYPES}
\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Kinding System}
The kinding rules for types are given in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:kinding}.
\begin{figure}\begin{equation*}\frac{}{\mathbb{0}:\kappa}\frac{t:\kappa}{\neg t:\kappa}\frac{t_{1}:\kappa t_{2}:\kappa}{t_{1}\lor t_{2}:\kappa}\frac{}{\alpha:\star}\frac{}{b:\star}\frac{t_{1}:\star t_{2}:\star}{t_{1}\to t_{2}:\star}\\
\frac{\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset}:\mathrm{Row}(\emptyset)}{\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}:\star}\frac{\forall\ell\in L_{1}.\tau_{\ell}:\star_{\bot}}{\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{1}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{2}}:\mathrm{Row}(L_{2})}\quad\textsc{right={$\begin{array}[]{l}\varsigma\in\{\epsilon,\textbf{..}\}\\
L_{1}\cap L_{2}=\emptyset\end{array}$}}\frac{\forall\ell\in L_{1}.\tau_{\ell}:\star_{\bot}}{\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{1}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{2}}:\mathrm{Row}(L_{2})}\quad\textsc{right={$\begin{array}[]{l}L_{1}\cap L_{2}=\emptyset\\
\rdef(\rho)=\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}(L_{1}\cup L_{2})\end{array}$}}\frac{}{\theta:\star_{\bot}}\frac{}{\bot:\star_{\bot}}\frac{t:\star}{t:\star_{\bot}}\end{equation*}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{3}}{Kinding rules}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 3}}{\small Kinding rules}}}
\end{figure}
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Example of a Presence Polymorphic Type}
\par Presence polymorphism has been introduced by \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{remy94}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} to let a field be
polymorphic in its presence.
A presence variable $\theta$ can be instantiated to one of
$\{\texttt{abs},\texttt{pre}\}$.
Our calculus also supports presence polymorphism thanks to field variables.
Let us reproduce an example of a presence polymorphic type declaration from
\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{remy94}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, that we will then transcribe to our setting.
{\@@listings@block{11}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@frame\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{{type}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{tree}}}(!{\@listingGroup{}{$\theta$}}!){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{Leaf}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{{of}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{Int}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbar{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{Node}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{{of}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{left}}}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{pre}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{tree}}}(!{\@listingGroup{}{$\theta$}}!),{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{right}}}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{pre}}}.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{tree}}}(!{\@listingGroup{}{$\theta$}}!),{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{annot}}}:!{\@listingGroup{}{$\theta$}}!.{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{int}}}\textbraceright\@lst@endline}}{}}}Instantiating $\theta$ to {abs} gives the type of trees with no
annotations on the nodes,
while instantiating it with {pre} gives the type of trees with integers
annotated on the nodes.
\par But there are several problems: adding yet another kind of polymorphism
adds a bit of work, and most of all, even absent fields must have a type
attached.
In \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{remy94}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, this can cause losing unification of two semantically
equivalent records, if for instance one has a field $\ell$ of type
{abs.int}, and the other a field $\ell$ of type {abs.bool}.
To avoid this problem, \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{tang23}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} requires every record value to be
annotated with its type, so that they can forget about absent types, and also so
that they can have deterministic typing.
So for instance the superfluous type
$\{\ell_{1}=M;\ell_{2}=N\}^{\{\ell_{1}:\texttt{pre}.A;\ell_{2}:\texttt{abs}.B\}}$ is equivalent to
$\{\ell_{1}=M\}^{\{\ell_{1}:\texttt{pre}.A\}}$.
\par In CDuce, there is no presence polymorphism at all, and the tree type defined
previously cannot be expressed without code redundancy.
In our formalism, presence polymorphism is simply obtained by means of field
variables, and adding support for it in CDuce would make it possible to write
the following (note that we use unions instead of tagged unions):
{\@@listings@block{12}{{\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@numbers\lst@@@set@frame\lst@@@set@rulecolor\lst@@@set@language\lst@@@set@frame\small\@lst@startline{}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_keyword}{{type}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{tree}}}(!{\@listingGroup{}{$\theta$}}!){\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}={\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{Int}}}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbar{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}\textbraceleft{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{left}}}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{tree}}}(!{\@listingGroup{}{$\theta$}}!),{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{right}}}:{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{tree}}}(!{\@listingGroup{}{$\theta$}}!),{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_space}{ }}{\@listingGroup{ltx_lst_identifier}{{annot}}}:!{\@listingGroup{}{$\theta$}}!\textbraceright\@lst@endline}}{}}}Fields do not have any superfluous type information when they are absent, so we
do not have the redundant record expressions, and do not need to put type
annotations directly on the values.
Moreover, in CDuce and in our type system, fields can be optional.
This is achieved thanks to union types (an optional field is encoded as $t\vee\bot$).
We do not know of any presence polymorphic type system dealing with optional
types.
\par We could instantiate the type of the example to any field-type, such as $\text{{Int}}$ for
a tree with all nodes annotated by an integer, $\text{{Int}}\vee\bot$ for a tree
with some nodes annotated by an integer, $\bot$ for a tree with no
annotation, and even for instance to a type variable $\alpha$, to create
a type parametric in the type of its annotation, but where the annotation
is mandatory on every node.
In Rémy's system, having polymorphism also on the type of the
annotations would require having two parameter variables (for presence and for type).
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Models}
\par In this section we give the detailed technical development to define
our subtyping relation.
\par To interpret record values we follow\penalty\ \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{frisch04}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} and represent
a record value by a quasi-constant function that maps labels into
either values
(i.e., the elements of $\mathcal{D}$) or $\bot$. Quasi-constant
functions are total functions that map all but a finite set of elements of their
domain into the same value (called default value). Thus record values
can be represented by quasi-constant functions whose default value is
$\bot$ (see\penalty\ \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna23a}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} for a more detailed explanation). Formally, let us write $\mathcal{D}_{\bot}$ for
$\mathcal{D}\cup\{\bot\}$ where $\bot$ is a distinguished element not in
$\mathcal{D}$. We represent our record values as quasi-constant functions
from $\mathcal{L}$ to $\mathcal{D}_{\bot}$ and, thus, interpret record types
as sets of these functions.
\par The formal definition of quasi-constant function has been given in
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:qcf} and is repeated below for convenience.
See \ref{restatable:quasiconstant}
Although this
notation is not univocal (unless we require $z_{i}\not=z$ and the $\ell_{i}$'s to be pairwise distinct), this is largely sufficient for the
purposes of this work. If $(Z_{\ell})_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}}$ is a family of
subsets of $Z$ indexed by $\mathcal{L}$, we denote by
$\hskip 3.0pt{\scriptsize\triangleright}\hskip-9.0pt\prod_{\ell{\in}\mathcal{L}}Z_{\ell}$ the subset of
$\mathcal{L}\rightarrowtriangle Z$ formed by all quasi-constant functions $r$ such that
$r(\ell)\in Z_{\ell}$ for all $\ell\in\mathcal{L}$ (intuitively,
$\hskip 3.0pt{\scriptsize\triangleright}\hskip-9.0pt\prod_{\ell{\in}\mathcal{L}}Z_{\ell}$ is a ``type'' of quasi-constant functions).
\par Next we have to give an interpretation for the
variables. \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna11}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} tell us that type variables must be
interpreted as sets in the domain $\mathcal{D}$. Therefore, an
interpretation for type variables is a function in
$\mathcal{V}_{t}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{D})$. Field variables are not much harder,
since the only difference with type variables is that their
interpretation can contain $\bot$, and therefore it is a function in
$\mathcal{V}_{f}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{D}_{\bot})$. More difficult is the
interpretation of row variables, since these are mapped
into \emph{rows}, that is, \emph{partial} quasi-constant functions on
$\mathcal{L}$. Let us write
$\mathcal{L}\mathrlap{\;\;\raisebox{0.8pt}{\tiny/}}\rightarrowtriangle\mathcal{D}_{\bot}$ for the partial quasi-constant
functions from $\mathcal{L}$ to $\mathcal{D}_{\bot}$. Thus, an interpretation
of row variables must map an element of $\mathcal{V}_{r}$ into a set of functions in $\mathcal{L}\mathrlap{\;\;\raisebox{0.8pt}{\tiny/}}\rightarrowtriangle\mathcal{D}_{\bot}$. However, for a
given $\rho$ we cannot consider any element in
$\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{L}\mathrlap{\;\;\raisebox{0.8pt}{\tiny/}}\rightarrowtriangle\mathcal{D}_{\bot})$: we need that the functions in
the interpretation of $\rho$ are total on $\rdef(\rho)$. Formally, we
have:
\begin{definition}Let $\eta$ be a function in
$\mathcal{V}_{r}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{L}\mathrlap{\;\;\raisebox{0.8pt}{\tiny/}}\rightarrowtriangle\mathcal{D}_{\bot})$. We say that $\eta$
is \emph{well kinded} if for every $\rho\in\mathcal{V}_{r}$ and for every
$f\in\eta(\rho)$, $f$ is a (total) quasi-constant function in
$\rdef(\rho)\rightarrowtriangle\mathcal{D}_{\bot}$. We denote by
$\mathcal{V}_{r}\mathrlap{\;\;\raisebox{0.2pt}{\small$\circ$}}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{L}\mathrlap{\;\;\raisebox{0.8pt}{\tiny/}}\rightarrowtriangle\mathcal{D}_{\bot})$ the set of
well-kinded functions.
\end{definition}
In conclusion our interpretation of types will be parametric in an assignment $\eta$ for the variables, which will be a function in $$\mathcal{H}\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}(\mathcal{V}_{t}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{D}))\cup(\mathcal{V}_{f}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{D}_{\bot}))\cup(\mathcal{V}_{r}\mathrlap{\;\;\raisebox{0.2pt}{\small$\circ$}}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{L}\mathrlap{\;\;\raisebox{0.8pt}{\tiny/}}\rightarrowtriangle\mathcal{D}_{\bot}))$$
\par The next step is to define the domain $\mathcal{D}$ in which to give
the interpretation of types. This is quite simple for us since it suffices to take the
model defined by\penalty\ \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna11}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} and replace products by
quasi-constant functions. The hard problem for defining this model, and
thus the interpretation of types, is to give an interpretation of the
function spaces, but this problem was solved by\penalty\ \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{frisch08}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} whose
solution is
reused by\penalty\ \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna11}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}. In a nutshell we want to define an
interpretation function
$\llbracket.\rrbracket:\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{D})$. Since the elements of
$\mathcal{D}$ represent the values of the language, then $\mathcal{D}$ must
contain the set $\mathcal{C}$ of constants of the language, the quasi-constant functions (to represent
record values), and the functions
from $\mathcal{D}$ to $\mathcal{D}$, but the last containment is impossible for cardinality
reasons. The solution by\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{frisch08}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} is to associate to every
domain $\mathcal{D}$ and function $\llbracket.\rrbracket:\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{D})$ a unique
\emph{extensional interpretation}
$\mathbb{E}(\cdot):\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}})$
which fixes the semantic of the type constructors, and then to accept as a valid
interpretation of the types only the pairs $(\llbracket.\rrbracket,\mathcal{D})$
such that for all $\eta$, $\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\eta}=\emptyset\iff\mathbb{E}(t)_{\eta}=\emptyset$.
\par We invite the reader to refer to \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna11}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{, Section 2.2)}}} for a
more detailed explanation of how the extensional interpretation
works and to \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{frisch08}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} for full details. Henceforth, we just present
how to extend the extensional interpretation of \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna11}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} to
include quasi-constant functions and the interpretation of
row-variables, and pinpoint the differences between the two definitions.
We suppose to be given an interpretation
$\mathbb{B}:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})$ of basic types into
sets of constants. Given a set $S$ we use the notation $\overline{S}$
to denote its complement in an appropriate universe: this notation is in particular used for the
set
\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\mathcal{P}(\overline{\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket\times\overline{\llbracket t_{2}\rrbracket}})$}
which corresponds to interpreting the elements in $t_{1}\to t_{2}$ as
binary relations, namely as elements of the set $\{\ f\subseteq\mathcal{D}^{2}\mid\text{for all }(d_{1},d_{2}){\in}f,\text{ if }d_{1}{\in}\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket_{\eta}\text{ then }d_{2}{\in}\llbracket t_{2}\rrbracket_{\eta}\ \}$.
\par\begin{definition}Let $\mathcal{D}$ be a set. The \emph{extensional domain}
of $\mathcal{D}$ is defined as:
$\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}}=\mathcal{C}+\mathcal{D}+\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D}_{\Omega})+(\mathcal{L}\rightarrowtriangle\mathcal{D}_{\bot})$
where $\Omega$ and $\bot$ are two different distinguished elements not in $\mathcal{D}$.
\par Let ${\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}:\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{D})$
be an interpretation of types parametric in a well-kinded
interpretation of variables.
The associated \emph{extensional interpretation} of types is the unique function
$\mathbb{E}(\cdot):\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}})$
such that:
\par\noindent$\begin{array}[]{rl}\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{0})_{\eta}&=\emptyset\\
\mathbb{E}(\alpha)_{\eta}&=\eta(\alpha)\\
\mathbb{E}(b)_{\eta}&=\mathbb{B}(b)\\
\mathbb{E}(\neg t)_{\eta}&=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}}\smallsetminus\mathbb{E}(t)_{\eta}\\
\mathbb{E}(t_{1}\lor t_{2})_{\eta}&=\mathbb{E}(t_{1})_{\eta}\cup\mathbb{E}(t_{2})_{\eta}\\
\mathbb{E}(t_{1}\to t_{2})_{\eta}&=\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\mathcal{P}(\overline{\parbox[10.7pt][c]{48.0pt}{$\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket_{\eta}\times\overline{\parbox[8.8pt][c]{18.0pt}{$\llbracket t_{2}\rrbracket_{\eta}$}}$}}\,)$}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{rl}\mathbb{E}({\texttt{R}})_{\eta}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\bigcup_{{\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}\in\eta(\rho)}\left(\hskip 3.0pt{\scriptsize\triangleright}\hskip-9.0pt\prod_{\ell\in\fin({\texttt{R}})}\llbracket{\texttt{R}}(\ell)\rrbracket^{\operatorname{fld}}_{\eta}\sqcup{\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}\right)&\text{if $\tail({\texttt{R}})=\rho$}\\
\hskip 3.0pt{\scriptsize\triangleright}\hskip-9.0pt\prod_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}}\llbracket{\texttt{R}}(\ell)\rrbracket^{\operatorname{fld}}_{\eta}&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\end{array}$
\par\noindent where
\par$\begin{array}[]{rll}\llbracket t\rrbracket^{\operatorname{fld}}_{\eta}&={\llbracket t\rrbracket}_{\eta}&\text{if }t\not=\neg t^{\prime}\text{ and }t\not=t_{1}\vee t_{2}\\
\llbracket\theta\rrbracket^{\operatorname{fld}}_{\eta}&=\eta(\theta)\\
\llbracket\bot\rrbracket^{\operatorname{fld}}_{\eta}&=\{\bot\}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{rll}\llbracket\tau_{1}\lor\tau_{2}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{fld}}_{\eta}&=\llbracket\tau_{1}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{fld}}_{\eta}\cup\llbracket\tau_{2}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{fld}}_{\eta}\\
\llbracket\neg\tau\rrbracket^{\operatorname{fld}}_{\eta}&=(\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}}\cup\{\bot\})\smallsetminus\llbracket\tau\rrbracket^{\operatorname{fld}}_{\eta}\end{array}$
\end{definition}
Notice that the induction used in the definition is well-founded
thanks to the contractivity condition in the definition of types and
the fact that field-types are inductively defined.
\par The extensional interpretation is defined with respect to some domain
$\mathcal{D}$ and interpretation $\llbracket.\rrbracket$, and maps types into a
domain $\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{D}}$ that contains $\mathcal{D}$,
constants $\mathcal{C}$ to interpret basic types,
sets of binary relations $\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D}_{\Omega})$ to
interpret function types, and quasi constant functions
$\mathcal{L}\rightarrowtriangle\mathcal{D}_{\bot}$ to interpret record types. The fact
that functions are binary relations that can yield a distinguished
element $\Omega$ (which, intuitively, represents a type error) is a
standard technique of semantic subtyping to avoid $\mathbb{1}\to\mathbb{1}$ to be a supertype of
all function types: since it does not play any specific role in our
work we will not further
comment on it (see\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{frisch08}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})} for a detailed explanation
or\penalty\ \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna23a}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{, Section 3.2)}}} for a shorter one).
\par The definitions for the extensional interpretation given on the right-hand side in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{d:extint}
are the same as those by\penalty\ \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna11}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}. They state that the
empty type is interpreted as the empty set, the
interpretation of the type variables is given by $\eta$, that unions
and negations are interpreted as set-theoretic unions and
complements, and that functions types are interpreted as sets of
binary relations whose output is in the codomain if the input is in
the domain.
\par The novelty of our definition is the interpretation of record
types given on the left-hand side. There are two cases. The easy case is when the tail of the
record type is either $\epsilon$ or $\textbf{..}$: in that case the
interpretation of the record type is the set of all quasi constant
functions in $\mathcal{L}\rightarrowtriangle\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{D}_{\bot})$ that map a label
$\ell$ into an element of the interpretation of ${\texttt{R}}(\ell)$ (recall
that for $\ell\not\in\fin({\texttt{R}})$, ${\texttt{R}}(\ell)$ is $\bot$
for $\tail({\texttt{R}})=\epsilon$ and $\mathbb{1}\lor\bot$ for $\tail({\texttt{R}})=\textbf{..}$).
If instead $\tail({\texttt{R}})$ is a row variable $\rho$, then $\mathcal{L}$ is
partitioned in two, the sets $\fin({\texttt{R}})$ and---by well-kindedness---$\rdef(\rho)$, and the interpretation of ${\texttt{R}}$ will be the set of
quasi-constant functions in $\mathcal{L}\rightarrowtriangle\mathcal{D}_{\bot}$ obtained by
unioning two partial functions: a function
in $\hskip 3.0pt{\scriptsize\triangleright}\hskip-9.0pt\prod_{\ell\in\fin({\texttt{R}})}\llbracket{\texttt{R}}(\ell)\rrbracket^{\operatorname{fld}}_{\eta}$ for the $\fin({\texttt{R}})$ labels of $\mathcal{L}$, and a
function in $\eta(\rho)$ for the remaining labels of $\mathcal{L}$. There
is a caveat: fields can map labels both into values and
$\bot$. Therefore, the interpretation of field-types must be
slightly different from that of types, since it must map $\bot$ into
$\{\bot\}$ and the negation of a field-type is the complement
with respect to $\mathcal{D}_{\bot}$, rather than $\mathcal{D}$: the $\llbracket.\rrbracket^{\operatorname{fld}}$ does just that.
\par Given a domain $\mathcal{D}$ and a set-theoretic interpretation of the
types into this domain, they form a \emph{model} if the interpretation
and the associated extensional interpretation have the same zeros:
\begin{definition}Let $\mathcal{D}$ be a domain and
${\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket}:\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{D})$. The
pair $(\mathcal{D},{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket})$ is a \emph{model} if and only, if for
all $t\in\mathcal{T}$ and $\eta\in\mathcal{H}$,
${\llbracket t\rrbracket}_{\eta}=\emptyset\iff\mathbb{E}(t)_{\eta}=\emptyset$.
\end{definition}
Every model induces a subtyping relation on types:
\par\begin{definition}If $(\mathcal{D},{\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket})$
is a model, then it induces a subtyping relation defined as follows:
$$t_{1}\leq t_{2}\hbox{\;\;$\iff$\hskip-16.90094pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 11.38109pt}\forall\eta.{\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket_{\eta}}\subseteq{\llbracket t_{2}\rrbracket_{\eta}}$$\end{definition}
As explained by \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{frisch04}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{, Section 2.6)}}}, the interest of defining of a \emph{model} is that we can work
with the interpretation of the model ``\emph{as if}'' the interpretation
of the type
constructors (in particular, the function type constructor) were defined as their
extensional interpretation. So when deducing the properties for the
subtyping relation of a model---and just for the subtyping
relation---we can assume that \raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\mathcal{P}(\overline{\parbox[10.7pt][c]{48.0pt}{$\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket_{\eta}\times\overline{\parbox[8.8pt][c]{18.0pt}{$\llbracket t_{2}\rrbracket_{\eta}$}}$}}\,)$},
even if this is impossible for cardinality reasons.
\par\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:model} specifies which characteristics a model must
have to induce a subtyping relation (that behaves ``\emph{as if}''), but it does not define any
particular model nor, thus, any particular subtyping relation. In what follows
we define a concrete interpretation domain $\mathcal{D}$ (whose elements
are defined by induction) and two specific interpretations, and prove
that they satisfy the conditions to be models, since they both have
the same zeros as the extensional interpretation (of one of them). This
yields two equivalent definitions of a concrete subtyping relation we
are going to use in the rest of this presentation.
We will define:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@An interpretation $\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}$ parametrized by an assignment $\eta$,
for which subtyping $t_{1}\leq^{\operatorname{q}}t_{2}$ is defined as
$\forall\eta.\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}\subseteq\llbracket t_{2}\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}$;
(the index $q$ stands for \emph{{q}uantified}, since subtyping is
quantified on all variable interpretations);
\par\itemize@item@An interpretation of types directly into sets $\llbracket t\rrbracket$, that avoids
quantification over $\eta$, and for which subtyping $t_{1}\leq t_{2}$ is defined
directly as $\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket t_{2}\rrbracket$ (notice
the absence of a variable interpretation argument $\eta$).
\end{itemize}
\par The interpretation of types is mutually recursive with interpretations of rows and
field-types.
Both of these will also give rise to subtyping relations.
We will use the same notation $\leq$ for the relations in $\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T}$, $\mathcal{T}_{\bot}\times\mathcal{T}_{\bot}$ and
$\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{R}$.
\par\par\par\par\par This interpretation in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:indint} induces a subtyping relation that it is easy to work with, since it got rid of the
interpretation $\eta$ for the variables. We can consider the
interpretation $\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket:\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{D})$ as a
function in $\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{D})$ that is constant
on its second argument: if we apply \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:frischsubtyping} to it, then the subtyping relation is
defined as simply as
$t_{1}\leq t_{2}\hbox{\;\;$\iff$\hskip-16.90094pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 11.38109pt}\llbracket t_{1}\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket t_{2}\rrbracket$.
But getting rid of $\eta$ makes it difficult to prove that this interpretation is a model and, thus,
that when considering the properties of this subtyping relation, we can work ``as if'' the interpretation
of type constructors were as in the extensional interpretation. To overcome this difficulty we define a second
interpretation, on the same domain, but this interpretation disregards the indexes
of the elements and uses an assignment $\eta$ to interpret the variables.
\begin{definition}We define a ternary predicate $(D:T)_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}$
(``the element $D$ belongs to $T$ under assignment $\eta$''),
by induction on the pair $(D,T)$ ordered lexicographically.
The only differences with the predicate $(D:T)$ (apart from
recursive calls to the appropriate predicate), are:
\@@amsalign
We define the interpretations $\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}$,
$\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{qfld}}$ and $\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{qrow}}$ as expected.
\end{definition}
While the interpretation of type and field variables is
straightforwardly given by $\eta$, the interpretation of row variables
is less evident.
The first line of the interpretation of a row is the same as
in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:indint}: in both definitions this line deals with the case when
the tail of $r$ is not a row variable.
The second line covers the case for $\tail(r)=\rho$: it checks that ${\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}\in\eta(\rho)$, where ${\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}$
is obtained by restricting the quasi-constant function on the left to the
definition space of $\rho$.
\par Our goal is to prove that both interpretations give a model of types.
Formally, this corresponds to proving the following equivalences:
Let $\mathbb{E}(\cdot)$ be the extensional interpretation of
$\llbracket.\rrbracket^{\operatorname{q}}$. For all $t\in\mathcal{T}$:
\begin{equation}(\forall\eta.\mathbb{E}(t)_{\eta}=\emptyset)\iff(\forall\eta.\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}=\emptyset)\iff\llbracket t\rrbracket=\emptyset\end{equation}
The leftmost ``iff'' proves that $(\mathcal{D},\llbracket.\rrbracket^{\operatorname{q}})$ is a
model, while the rightmost one proves that the subtyping relation
induced by $\llbracket.\rrbracket$ is the same as the one induced by the model
$\llbracket.\rrbracket^{\operatorname{q}}$ (since
$\forall\eta.\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}\subseteq\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}\iff\forall\eta.\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}\cap(\mathcal{D}{\smallsetminus}\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}})=\emptyset\iff\forall\eta.\llbracket s{\wedge}\neg t\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}=\emptyset\iff\llbracket s{\wedge}\neg t\rrbracket=\emptyset\iff\llbracket s\rrbracket\subseteq\llbracket t\rrbracket$).
\par For the first equivalence, we prove the following, more precise, statement.
\begin{lemma}For all type $t$, for all $\eta$,
$$\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}=\emptyset\iff\mathbb{E}(t)_{\eta}=\emptyset$$\end{lemma}
\@proof For all $d$, we show $(d:t)_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}\iff d\in\mathbb{E}(t)_{\eta}$
by induction on $t$ in both directions.
This induction is well-founded because the cases for type constructors do not
use induction, $\mathbb{E}(t)_{\eta}$ is defined on top of
$\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}$, and the number of type connectives is finite by
regularity of the types.
\par We start with the left-to-right implication and detail the case $t={\texttt{R}}$.
By hypothesis there is ${\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}=\langle\!|(\ell=\delta_{\ell})_{\ell\in L},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle_{\emptyset}^{V}$
such that $(\mathrm{Rec}({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud})^{V^{\prime}}:{\texttt{R}})_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}$.
By hypothesis, $\forall\ell\in L.(\delta_{\ell}:{\texttt{R}}(\ell))_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}$,
so $\delta_{\ell}\in\llbracket{\texttt{R}}(\ell)\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{qfld}}$,
and $\forall\ell\in\mathcal{L}\smallsetminus L.(\bot^{\emptyset}:{\texttt{R}}(\ell))_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}$,
so $\bot^{\emptyset}\in\llbracket{\texttt{R}}(\ell)\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{qfld}}$.
It is easy to see that this implies respectively
$\delta_{\ell}\in\llbracket{\texttt{R}}(\ell)\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{fld}}$
and $\bot^{\emptyset}\in\llbracket{\texttt{R}}(\ell)\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{fld}}$.
Moreover, if $\tail(\rho)=\rho$, then
$\langle\!|(\ell=\delta_{\ell})_{\ell\in L\cap\rdef(\rho)},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle_{\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}\rdef(\rho)}^{V}\in\eta(\rho)$.
So ${\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}\in\mathbb{E}_{\eta}({\texttt{R}})$.
\par Now, for the right-to-left implication.
Let ${\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}=\langle\!|(\ell=\delta_{\ell})_{\ell\in L},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle_{\emptyset}^{V}\in\mathbb{E}_{\eta}({\texttt{R}})$.
For all $\ell\in\fin({\texttt{R}})$, we have by hypothesis $\delta\in\llbracket{\texttt{R}}(\ell)\rrbracket^{\operatorname{fld}}$, which implies
$(\delta:{\texttt{R}}(\ell))_{\eta}^{\operatorname{qfld}}$.
Let $\ell\notin\fin({\texttt{R}})$.
If $\ell\in L_{1}$, then $(\delta_{\ell}:{\texttt{R}}(\ell))_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}$ holds.
If $\ell\notin L_{1}$: if $\tail({\texttt{R}})\in\mathcal{V}$, by definition ${\texttt{R}}(\ell)=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot$, and if $\tail({\texttt{R}})\notin\mathcal{V}$, ${\texttt{R}}(\ell)=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot$,
or ${\texttt{R}}(\ell)=\bot$.
In any case, $(\bot^{\emptyset}:{\texttt{R}}(\ell))_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}$ holds.
Finally, if $\tail({\texttt{R}})=\rho\in\mathcal{V}$, then
$\langle\!|(\ell=\delta_{\ell})_{\ell\in L\cap\rdef(\rho)},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle_{\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}\rdef(\rho)}^{V}\in\eta(\rho)$.
\qed\end@proof
\par We have thus shown that the subtyping relation generated by
$\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket^{\operatorname{q}}$ has the expected properties described by
$\mathbb{E}(\cdot)$.
In particular, it is a \emph{set-theoretic} model because type operators are
interpreted as set operators.
\par Since it will be easier to work directly with interpretations as sets rather
than to quantify over $\eta$, we now show that the subtyping relation generated
by $\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket$ is equivalent to the parametrized one.
We show that equivalence not only on types, but also on field-types and rows.
The main element of the proof is the canonical assignment $\hat{\eta}$, defined as
\@@amsalign
\par\begin{lemma}For every $T\in\mathcal{T}_{\bot}\cup\mathcal{R}$,
$\llbracket T\rrbracket=\llbracket T\rrbracket_{\hat{\eta}}^{\operatorname{q}}$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof For any $D$ and $T$ we prove that $(D:T)\iff(D:T)_{\hat{\eta}}^{\operatorname{q}}$
by induction on $(D,T)$.
The only interesting case is when $T=r$
and $D=\langle\!|(\ell=\delta_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{1}},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle_{L_{2}}^{V}$.
For all $\ell\in L_{1}$ we have $(\delta_{\ell}:r(\ell))\iff(\delta_{\ell}:r(\ell))_{\hat{\eta}}^{\operatorname{q}}$ by induction hypothesis.
Let $\ell\notin L_{1}$.
We show that $(\bot^{\emptyset}:r(\ell))\iff(\bot^{\emptyset}:r(\ell))_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}$ by induction on $r(\ell)$.
This induction is well-founded : for $r(\ell)=\bot$, both propositions
are true and they are false for any other type constructor (in particular
$r(\ell)=\theta$). The inductive cases on type operators are
straightforward.
If $\tail(r)\notin\mathcal{V}$, we are done.
Otherwise, let $\tail(r)=\rho$.
On the left side, we have $\rho\in V$.
On the right side, we have
$\langle\!|(\ell=\delta_{\ell})_{\ell\in L\cap\rdef(\rho)},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle_{\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}\rdef(\rho)}^{V}\in\hat{\eta}(\rho)$.
By definition of $\hat{\eta}(\rho)$, this is equivalent to $\rho\in V$.
\qed\end@proof
\par\begin{lemma}Let $W\in\mathcal{P}_{\!\textup{fin}}(\mathcal{V})$ and $T_{W}=\{T\in\mathcal{T}_{\bot}\cup\mathcal{R}\mid\vars(T)\subseteq W\}$.
For every $T\in T_{W}$,
$$\llbracket T\rrbracket_{\hat{\eta}}^{\operatorname{q}}=\emptyset\iff\forall\eta.\llbracket T\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}=\emptyset.$$\end{lemma}
\@proof The right-to-left implication is trivial, by instantiation of the quantifier
by $\eta^{\prime}$.
The left-to-right implication is by contraposition: for an arbitrary $W$ and $T_{W}$, we prove
$\forall T\in T_{W}.(\exists\eta.\llbracket T\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}\neq\emptyset\implies\llbracket T\rrbracket_{\hat{\eta}}^{\operatorname{q}}\neq\emptyset)$.
For this, we define the functions $F_{W}^{\eta}:\mathcal{D}_{\bot}\cup\mathcal{D}_{\operatorname{row}}\cup\{\Omega\}\to\mathcal{D}_{\bot}\cup\mathcal{D}_{\operatorname{row}}\cup\{\Omega\}$
as $F_{W}^{\eta}(\Omega)=\Omega$ and:
$$F_{W}^{\eta}(D)=\begin{cases}c^{\hat{V}(D)}&\text{if }D=c;\\
\{(F_{W}^{\eta}(d_{1}),F_{W}^{\eta}(\partial_{1})),\dots,(F_{W}^{\eta}(d_{n}),F_{W}^{\eta}(\partial_{n}))\}^{\hat{V}(D)}&\text{if }D=\{(d_{1},\partial_{1}),\dots,(d_{n},\partial_{n})\}^{V};\\
\mathrm{Rec}({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud})^{\hat{V}(D)}&\text{if }D=\mathrm{Rec}({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud})^{V};\\
\bot^{\hat{V}(D)}&\text{if }D=\bot^{V};\\
\langle\!|(\ell=F_{W}^{\eta}(\delta_{\ell}))_{\ell\in L_{1}},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle_{L_{2}}^{\hat{V}(D)}&\text{if }D=\langle\!|(\ell=\delta_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{1}},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle_{L_{2}}^{V}\end{cases}$$
where $\hat{V}(D)=\{\alpha\in W\mid D\in\eta(\alpha)\}\cup\{\theta\in W\mid D\in\eta(\theta)\}\cup\{\rho\in W\mid D\in\eta(\rho)\}$.
The finiteness of $W$ ensures that $\hat{V}$ is finite.
We prove the following statement, for an arbitrary $\eta$ and by induction on
$(D,T)$ ordered lexicographically:
$$\forall T\in T_{W}.\forall D\in\mathcal{D}_{\bot}\cup\mathcal{R}.(D:T)_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}\implies(F_{W}^{\eta}(D):T)_{\hat{\eta}}^{\operatorname{q}}$$
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$T=\alpha$.
We have
$(F_{W}^{\eta}(D):\alpha)_{\hat{\eta}}^{\operatorname{q}}\iff\alpha\in\Tag(F_{W}^{\eta}(D))\iff\alpha\in\hat{V}(D)\iff D\in\eta(\alpha)\mathrel{\mathsf{and}}\alpha\in W\iff(D:\alpha)_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}$.
The last equivalence holds by the hypothesis that $T\in T_{W}$.
The case for $T=\theta$ is similar.
\par\itemize@item@$T=\bot$ and $D=\bot^{V}$.
$(F_{W}^{\eta}(\bot^{V}):\bot)_{\hat{\eta}}^{\operatorname{q}}=(\bot^{\hat{V}(D)}:\bot)_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}$ holds.
\par\itemize@item@$T={\texttt{R}}$ and $D=\mathrm{Rec}({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud})^{V}$.
By hypothesis, we have $({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}:\operatorname{row}({\texttt{R}}))_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}$.
By induction, this implies
$(F_{W}^{\eta}({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}):\operatorname{row}({\texttt{R}}))_{\hat{\eta}}^{\operatorname{q}}$
and thus $(F_{W}^{\eta}(D):{\texttt{R}})_{\hat{\eta}}^{\operatorname{q}}$.
\par\itemize@item@$d=\langle\!|(\ell=\delta_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{1}},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle_{L_{2}}^{V}$
and $t=r$.
The statement holds for labels in and outside of $L_{1}$ by induction
hypothesis.
If $\tail(r)\notin\mathcal{V}$, we are done.
Let $\tail(r)=\rho$.
By hypothesis, $\rho\in W$.
By definition of the predicate, there is
${\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}=\langle\!|(\ell=\delta_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{1}\cap\rdef(\rho)},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle_{\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}\rdef(\rho)}^{V}\in\eta(\rho)$.
As in the case for type variables, we show:
$(F_{W}^{\eta}({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}):\rho)_{\hat{\eta}}^{\operatorname{q}}\iff\rho\in\Tag(F_{W}^{\eta}({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}))\iff\rho\in\hat{V}({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud})\iff D\in\eta(\rho)\mathrel{\mathsf{and}}\rho\in W$.
\par\itemize@item@Other cases can be found in \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{petrucciani19}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Lemma 2.8)}
or are direct by falsity of the premise.
\qed\end{itemize}
\end@proof
\par\begin{lemma}For all $t_{1}$ and $t_{2}$,
$t_{1}\leq^{\operatorname{q}}t_{2}\iff t_{1}\leq t_{2}$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof By definition, \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:hateta_to_eta} and \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:hatmu}, we show:
$$t_{1}\leq^{\operatorname{q}}t_{2}\iff\forall\eta.\llbracket t_{1}{\smallsetminus}t_{2}\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}=\emptyset\iff\llbracket t_{1}{\smallsetminus}t_{2}\rrbracket_{\hat{\eta}}^{\operatorname{q}}=\emptyset\iff\llbracket t_{1}{\smallsetminus}t_{2}\rrbracket=\emptyset\iff t_{1}\leq t_{2}\qed$$
\end@proof
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Subtyping Relation}
\par\begin{lemma}Let $r$ be an atomic row of definition space $\mathcal{L}\smallsetminus L_{r}$ and $L$ such
that $\fin(r)\subseteq L\subseteq\rdef(r)$. Then, $$r\simeq\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=r(\ell)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}}\wedge\boldsymbol{\langle}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\tail(r)\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}}\simeq\bigwedge_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\langle}\ell=r(\ell)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}}\wedge\boldsymbol{\langle}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\tail(r)\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}}$$where $L^{\prime}=\fin(r)$ if $\tail(r)\in\mathcal{V}$ and $L^{\prime}=L$ otherwise.
\end{lemma}
\@proof Straightforward by the definition of the models.
\qed\end@proof
\par The function $\Phi$ we define in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:subtyping} to decide subtyping
crucially relies on the formula we give in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:subtyping}.
It gives a characterization of the emptiness of $\bigwedge_{r\in P}r\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in N}\neg r$.
While function $\Phi$ is stated on records, we prefer to state this lemma on
rows, as we will refer back to it in that way for tallying
(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{app:tallying}).
The corollary for records follows immediately by $t_{1}\leq t_{2}\iff\operatorname{row}(t_{1})\leq\operatorname{row}(t_{2})$ when $t_{1},t_{2}\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
This lemma generalizes to rows and polymorphic record types the
decomposition of monomorphic ones defined
by \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{frisch04}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}.
The main difference is the addition the third
condition on line\penalty\ \eqref{eq:subtyping_vars},
that checks whether a row variable appears both in the positive and in the
negative fragment.
\begin{lemma}Let $P$ and $N$ be sets of atomic row types $r$ each of definition space
$\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}L_{r}$.
Let $L$ be a finite set of labels such that $\bigcup_{r\in P\cup N}\fin(r)\subseteq L\subseteq\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}L_{r}$. Let
$P_{\mathcal{V}}=\{r\in P\mid\tail(r)\in\mathcal{V}\}$ and likewise for $N_{\mathcal{V}}$.
For every $r$, we define its default type $\Def(r)$ as: $\Def(r)=\bot$ if $r$ is closed, and
$\Def(r)=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot$ otherwise.
The relation
$\bigwedge_{r\in P}r\leq\bigvee_{r\in N}r$
holds {iff}
$\forall\iota:N\to L\cup\{\underline{\penalty\ \penalty\ }\}$,
\@@amsalign
\end{lemma}
\@proof In the following, we let $L_{i}=\fin(r_{i})$ and $\varsigma_{i}=\tail(r_{i})$.
Using \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:normalize_row_easy}, we decompose the conjunction into:
\begin{equation}\bigwedge_{r_{p}\in P}(\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=r_{p}(\ell))_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}}\wedge\boldsymbol{\langle}L_{p}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma_{p}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}})\wedge\bigwedge_{r_{n}\in N}(\vee_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\langle}\ell=\neg{r_{n}(\ell)}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}}\vee\neg\boldsymbol{\langle}L_{n}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}})\end{equation}
Where $L_{i}^{\prime}=L_{i}$ if $r_{i}\in\mathcal{V}$ and $L_{i}^{\prime}=L$ otherwise.
We can distribute the intersection of the elements of $N$ on the right of
\eqref{eq:ten} over the unions in the second brackets.
We obtain a union of intersections of, each time, $|N|$ elements, where each
intersection is a possible combination of the individual rows present in the
second line.
Each combination is described by a function $\iota:N\to L\cup\{\_\}$, where $\iota(r_{n})=\ell$ means that the element $\boldsymbol{\langle}\ell=\neg{r_{n}(\ell)}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}}$ is present in the combination given by $\iota$, while
$\iota(r_{n})=\_$ means that the element $\neg\boldsymbol{\langle}L_{n}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}}$ is present
in the combination.
For each $r_{n}\in N$, let us write
$r^{n}_{\ell}=\boldsymbol{\langle}\ell=\neg{r_{n}(\ell)}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}}$ and
$r^{n}_{-}=\neg\boldsymbol{\langle}L_{n}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}}$.
Therefore the row in\penalty\ \eqref{eq:ten} is equivalent to:
\begin{equation}\bigwedge_{r_{p}\in P}(\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=r_{p}(\ell))_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}}\wedge\boldsymbol{\langle}L_{p}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma_{p}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}})\wedge\bigvee_{\iota:N\to L\cup\{\_\}}(\bigwedge_{r_{n}\in N}r^{n}_{\iota(r_{n})})\end{equation}
By distributing the intersection over the union we obtain
\begin{equation}\bigvee_{\iota:N\to L\cup\{\_\}}\left(\bigwedge_{r_{p}\in P}(\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=r_{p}(\ell))_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}}\wedge\boldsymbol{\langle}L_{p}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma_{p}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}})\wedge\bigwedge_{r_{n}\in N}r^{n}_{\iota(r_{n})}\right)\end{equation}
A union is empty if and only if each summand of the union is
empty. Therefore the row above is empty if and only if for all $\iota:N\to L\cup\{\_\}$,
the following is empty:
\@@amsalign
\par Let:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$r_{\iota}^{1}=\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\bigwedge_{r_{p}\in P}r_{p}(\ell)\wedge\bigwedge_{r_{n}\in\iota^{-1}(\ell)}\neg{r_{n}(\ell)})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}}$;
\par\itemize@item@$r_{\iota}^{2}=\bigwedge_{r_{p}\in P_{\bar{\mathcal{V}}}}\boldsymbol{\langle}L\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma_{p}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}}\wedge\bigwedge_{r_{n}\in\iota^{-1}(\_)\cap N_{\bar{\mathcal{V}}}}\neg\boldsymbol{\langle}L\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}}$;
\par\itemize@item@$r_{\iota}^{3}=\bigwedge_{r_{p}\in P_{\mathcal{V}}}\boldsymbol{\langle}L_{p}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma_{p}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}}\wedge\bigwedge_{r_{n}\in\iota^{-1}(\_)\cap N_{\mathcal{V}}}\neg\boldsymbol{\langle}L_{n}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma_{n}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{r}}$.
\end{itemize}
We can see that $r_{\iota}^{1}$ is empty {iff}
condition\penalty\ \eqref{eq:subtyping_dom} holds,
$r_{\iota}^{2}$ is empty {iff} condition\penalty\ \eqref{eq:subtyping_nvars} does
(in the case where
$P_{\bar{\mathcal{V}}}$ is empty, notice that the intersection is equal to $\mathbb{1}\lor\bot$), and $r_{\iota}^{3}$ is empty {iff}
condition\penalty\ \eqref{eq:subtyping_vars} holds.
We directly obtain that if one of the conditions holds, then the row
$r_{\iota}$ is empty.
We now show that if $r_{\iota}$ is empty, then there is $1\leq i\leq 3$ such
that $r_{\iota}^{i}$ is empty.
\par For this, we suppose that none of the subtypes is empty and build an element
${\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}\in\llbracket r_{\iota}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{row}}$.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@Since $r_{\iota}^{1}$ is not empty, for all $\ell\in L$ there is an
element $\delta_{\ell}^{1}\in\llbracket\bigwedge_{r_{p}\in P}r_{p}(\ell)\wedge\bigwedge_{r_{n}\in\iota^{-1}(\ell)}\neg r_{n}(\ell)\rrbracket^{\operatorname{fld}}$.
\par\enumerate@item@Since $r_{\iota}^{2}$ is not empty, there is an element
$\langle\!|(\ell=\delta^{2}_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{2}},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle^{V_{2}}\in\llbracket r_{\iota}^{2}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{row}}$.
\par\enumerate@item@Since $r_{\iota}^{3}$ is not empty, there is an element ${\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}_{3}\in\llbracket r_{\iota}^{3}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{row}}$.
However, the restrictions on the set of elements in
$\llbracket r_{\iota}^{3}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{row}}$ only concern their tags so that any
element ${\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}^{\prime}$ with $\Tag({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}^{\prime})=\Tag({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}_{3})$ and $\rdef({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}^{\prime})=\rdef({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}_{3})$ is in $\llbracket r_{3}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{row}}$.
Let $V_{3}=\Tag({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}_{3})$.
\end{enumerate}
We build the element
$\langle\!|(\ell=\delta^{1}_{\ell})_{\ell\in L},(\ell=\delta^{2}_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{2}{\smallsetminus}L},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle^{V_{3}}$.
This element belongs to $\llbracket r_{\iota}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{row}}$, which is a
contradiction.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Subtyping Algorithm}
\par Naively implementing the above subtyping formula requires backtracking.
Indeed, for all map $\iota:N\to L\cup\{\_\}$, we have to check if subtyping
holds on one of the labels $\ell\in L$.
On that recursive call, since the types are coinductive, we need to assume
that the type we are checking is empty.
So, we are collecting a series of emptiness assumptions along the call stack.
If later a contradiction arises, we need to backtrack to the point where the
wrong assumption was introduced, to then take another branch, in our case, check
subtyping for another $\ell$.
Our function $\Phi$ avoids backtracking to compute subtyping more efficiently,
following \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{frisch04}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Chapter 7)}.
\par See \ref{restatable:correctsubalg}
\@proof If ${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\simeq\mathbb{0}$, the result holds.
Otherwise, we prove this by induction on the cardinality of $N$.
In the following, given a variable $\rho\in V_{p}$, let us write $L_{\rho}$ for
$\mathcal{L}\smallsetminus\rdef(\rho)$.
\par If $N=\emptyset$, the union $\bigvee_{{\texttt{R}}\in N}{\texttt{R}}$ is empty.
So the statement holds if and only if ${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\simeq\mathbb{0}$,
since $\Phi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},\emptyset)=\texttt{false}$,
and $\bigwedge_{\rho\in V_{p}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ is never empty.
\par Now, let $N=N^{\prime}\cup\{{\texttt{R}}\}$.
Let $L=\fin({\texttt{R}}_{\circ})$.
${\texttt{R}}$ can be decomposed as
$\bigwedge_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell={\texttt{R}}(\ell)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$,
where $L^{\prime}=L$ if $\varsigma\notin\mathcal{V}$ and $L^{\prime}=\fin({\texttt{R}})$ otherwise.
The left-hand side of the statement is thus equivalent to
${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\wedge\bigwedge_{\rho\in V_{p}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge(\bigvee_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\neg{\texttt{R}}(\ell)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\vee\neg\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}})\leq\bigvee_{n\in N^{\prime}}{\texttt{R}}_{n}$.
We can distribute the intersection over the unions.
We must then prove an equivalence between $({\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\simeq\emptyset\mathrel{\mathsf{or}}\Phi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},N))$ and:
\@@amsalign
We now have to verify that each of these statements are equivalent to
$\Phi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},N)$.
\par We start with \eqref{eq:correct_subalg1}.
Let $\ell\in L$ and let ${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}^{\ell}={\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\wedge\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\neg{\texttt{R}}(\ell)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
By the induction hypothesis, we have that ${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}^{\ell}\wedge\bigwedge_{\rho\in V_{p}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\leq\bigvee_{n\in N^{\prime}}{\texttt{R}}_{n}\iff{\texttt{R}}_{\circ}^{\ell}\simeq\mathbb{0}$ or $\Phi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ}^{\ell},V_{p},N^{\prime})$.
Since ${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\nsimeq\mathbb{0}$, ${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}^{\ell}\simeq\mathbb{0}\iff{\texttt{R}}_{\circ}(\ell)\leq{\texttt{R}}(\ell)$.
\par We continue with \eqref{eq:correct_subalg2}.
We define $\Psi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}})$ as
$(\varsigma=\textbf{..}\mathrel{\mathsf{or}}\varsigma=\tail({\texttt{R}}_{\circ})\mathrel{\mathsf{or}}\varsigma\in V_{p})$.
We show that\penalty\ \eqref{eq:correct_subalg2} is equivalent to:
$\Psi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}})\mathrel{\mathsf{or}}(\neg\Psi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}})\mathrel{\mathsf{and}}\Phi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},N^{\prime}))$.
It is easy to show using \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:subtyping} that ${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\wedge\bigwedge_{\rho\in V_{p}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\neg\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ is empty {iff}
$\Psi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}})$ holds.
In particular, the first condition of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:subtyping} never holds since
$L^{\prime}=L$ when $\varsigma\notin\mathcal{V}$.
Then, there are two cases.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\wedge\bigwedge_{\rho\in V_{p}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\neg\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\leq\mathbb{0}$.
This means that $\Psi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}})$ holds, and also that
${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\wedge\bigwedge_{\rho\in V_{p}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\neg\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\leq\bigvee_{n\in N^{\prime}}{\texttt{R}}_{n}$.
\par\enumerate@item@${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\wedge\bigwedge_{\rho\in V_{p}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\neg\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\nleq\mathbb{0}$.
This means that $\Psi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}})$ does not hold.
Thus, there are two possible cases: either (a) $\tail({\texttt{R}}_{\circ})=\epsilon$ and $\varsigma=\textbf{..}$, or (b) $\varsigma=\rho\notin V_{p}$.
We show that
${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\wedge\bigwedge_{\rho\in V_{p}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\neg\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\leq\bigvee_{{\texttt{R}}\in N^{\prime}}{\texttt{R}}$ is equivalent to
${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\wedge\bigwedge_{\rho\in V_{p}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\leq\bigvee_{{\texttt{R}}\in N^{\prime}}{\texttt{R}}$.
From this, we use the induction hypothesis to obtain the equivalence with
$\Phi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},N^{\prime})$ (since ${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\nsimeq\mathbb{0}$).
\par The right-to-left implication of the equivalence is trivial.
For the converse implication, we use \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:subtyping} on
${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\wedge\bigwedge_{\rho\in V_{p}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\leq\neg\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\vee\bigvee_{{\texttt{R}}\in N^{\prime}}{\texttt{R}}$.
By the hypothesis (a) and (b) in the corresponding cases, the second and
third conditions of that lemma never hold.
Thus, we have by hypothesis that
$\forall\iota:N^{\prime}\cup{\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}}\to L\cup\{\_\}.\exists\ell\in L.{\texttt{R}}_{\circ}(\ell)\leq\bigvee_{{\texttt{R}}\in\iota^{-1}(\ell)}{\texttt{R}}(\ell)$.
The implication holds because $\forall\ell\in L.\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}(\ell)=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot$.
\end{enumerate}
\par Summing up, we have proved by induction that if
${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\wedge\bigwedge_{\rho\in V_{p}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ is not empty,
checking that it is a subtype of $\bigvee_{n\in N}{\texttt{R}}_{n}$ is equivalent to
checking both these two propositions:
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$\forall\ell\in L\;.\;(\;({\texttt{R}}_{\circ}(\ell)\leq{\texttt{R}}(\ell))\quad\textsf{or}\quad\Phi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\wedge\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\neg{\texttt{R}}(\ell)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}},V_{p},N^{\prime})\;)$
\par\enumerate@item@$(\Psi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}))\quad\textsf{or}\quad(\neg\Psi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}})\textsf{ and }\Phi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},N^{\prime}))$
\end{enumerate}
To conclude, notice that if
$(\neg\Psi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}})\textsf{ and }\Phi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},N^{\prime}))$
holds, then by induction hypothesis we have
${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\wedge_{\rho\in V_{p}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\leq\bigvee_{n\in N^{\prime}}{\texttt{R}}_{n}$
and, {a fortiori}, ${\texttt{R}}_{\circ}\wedge\bigwedge_{\rho\in V_{p}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L_{\rho}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\leq{\texttt{R}}\vee\bigvee_{n\in N^{\prime}}{\texttt{R}}_{n}$.
It is therefore useless to check the other proposition $(1)$ above, which thus
must be checked only when $\Psi({\texttt{R}}_{\circ},V_{p},\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}})$ holds.
This yields
\@@amsalign
which corresponds to the second clause of the definition of $\Phi$.
\qed\end@proof
\par See \ref{restatable:termsubalg}
\@proof The number of disjoint types in a DNF is finite.
Also, the preprocessing of a conjunction of record types can be defined for
all such types and computed in a finite number of steps.
Finally, in function $\Phi$, the number of elements in the third parameter
decreases at each recursive call.
Moreover, the subtyping relation on non-record types is decidable
\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna11}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, from which we get decidability of the subtyping relation on
field-types.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Substitutions}
\par\begin{lemma}Let $\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{\bm{q}}}$ be the appropriate interpretation
among $\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}$,
$\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{qfld}}$ and
$\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{qrow}}$.
For every $T$, $\sigma$ and $\eta$, if $\eta^{\prime}$ is defined by
$\eta^{\prime}(X)=\llbracket\sigma(X)\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{\bm{q}}}$,
then $\llbracket T\sigma\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\bm{q}}}_{\eta}=\llbracket T\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\bm{q}}}_{\eta^{\prime}}$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof For an arbitrary $\sigma$ and $\eta$, we show that
$$\forall T\in\mathcal{T}_{\bot}\cup\mathcal{R}.\forall D\in\mathcal{D}_{\bot}\cup\mathcal{D}_{\operatorname{row}}.(D:T\sigma)_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}\iff(D:T)_{\eta^{\prime}}^{\operatorname{q}}$$
by induction on $(D,T)$ and with $\eta^{\prime}$ defined as before.
We detail two cases, the others are straightforward.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$T=\alpha$ and $D=d$.
On the left, we have $(d:\alpha\sigma)^{\operatorname{q}}_{\eta}=(d:\sigma(\alpha))_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}$
and on the right $(d:\alpha)^{\operatorname{q}}_{\eta^{\prime}}=d\in\eta^{\prime}(\alpha)=d\in\llbracket\sigma(\alpha)\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}=(d:\sigma(\alpha))^{\operatorname{q}}$.
\par\itemize@item@$T=\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$
and $D=\mathrm{Rec}\,\langle\!|(\ell=\delta_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{d}},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle^{V}$.
The case for rows is similar.
We have $T\sigma=\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}L\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\sigma(\rho)\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
Let ${\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}=\langle\!|(\ell=\delta_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{d}\smallsetminus L},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle_{L}^{V}$.
On the left, we have:
$$(D:T\sigma)^{\operatorname{q}}_{\eta}=(\forall\ell\in L_{d}.(\delta_{\ell}:T(\ell)\sigma)^{\operatorname{q}}_{\eta})\mathrel{\mathsf{and}}(\forall\ell\notin L_{d}.(\bot^{\emptyset}:T(\ell)\sigma)_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}\mathrel{\mathsf{and}}({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}:\sigma(\rho))^{\operatorname{q}}_{\eta}$$By induction hypothesis, the first two conditions are equivalent to
$\forall\ell\in L_{d}.(\delta_{\ell}:T(\ell))^{\operatorname{q}}_{\eta^{\prime}}$
and $\forall\ell\notin L_{d}.(\bot^{\emptyset}:T(\ell))_{\eta^{\prime}}^{\operatorname{q}}$.
By the same reasoning as in the previous case,
the last condition is equivalent to
$({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}:\rho)_{\eta^{\prime}}^{\operatorname{q}}$,
which altogether give $(D:T)_{\eta^{\prime}}^{\operatorname{q}}$.
\qed\end{itemize}
\end@proof
\par See \ref{restatable:subpreservesub}
\@proof By definition, $t_{1}\leq t_{2}\iff\llbracket t_{1}{\smallsetminus}t_{2}\rrbracket=\emptyset$.
By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:same_zeros_typeinter}, this is equivalent to
$\forall\eta.\llbracket t_{1}{\smallsetminus}t_{2}\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}$.
In particular, this holds for $\eta^{\prime}$ defined as in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:rowsub_commute},
so that $\llbracket t_{1}{\smallsetminus}t_{2}\rrbracket_{\eta^{\prime}}^{\operatorname{q}}=\emptyset$.
By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:rowsub_commute}, this implies $\llbracket(t_{1}{\smallsetminus}t_{2})\sigma\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}=\emptyset$ which means $t_{1}\sigma\leq t_{2}\sigma$.
\qed\end@proof
