\par\par\@@numbered@section{appendix}{toc}{APPENDIX FOR LANGUAGE}
\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Syntax and Semantics}
\par\begin{definition}The top-level variables of a type (resp.\ field-type, row) are defined as
$\tlv(t)=\tlv^{\prime}(t)\cap\mathcal{V}_{t}$ (resp.\ $\tlv(\tau)=\tlv^{\prime}(\tau)\cap\mathcal{V}_{f}$, $\tlv(r)=\tlv^{\prime}(r)\cap\mathcal{V}_{r}$).
$$\begin{array}[]{ll}\tlv^{\prime}(\alpha)=\{\alpha\}\\
\tlv^{\prime}(\theta)=\{\theta\}\\
\tlv^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\ell=\tau,\ldots,\ell=\tau\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L})=\tlv^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\tau,\ldots,\ell=\tau\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}})=\{\rho\}\\
\tlv^{\prime}(T_{1}\vee T_{2})=\tlv^{\prime}(T_{1})\cup\tlv^{\prime}(T_{2})\\
\tlv^{\prime}(\neg T)=\tlv^{\prime}(T)\\
\tlv^{\prime}(T)=\emptyset&\text{ otherwise}\end{array}$$\end{definition}
\par\begin{definition}Given a type term $T$, we write $\vars(T)$ the set of variables
occurring in it.
The following equalities hold.
$$\begin{array}[]{ccc}\vars(\alpha)=\{\alpha\}&\vars(T_{1}\to T_{2})=\vars(T_{1})\cup\vars(T_{2})&\vars(b)=\emptyset\\
\vars(\theta)=\{\theta\}&\vars(T_{1}\vee T_{2})=\vars(T_{1})\cup\vars(T_{2})&\vars(\bot)=\emptyset\\
\vars(\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{1}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{2}})=\{\varsigma\}\cap\mathcal{V}_{r}&\vars(\neg T)=\vars(T)&\vars(\mathbb{0})=\emptyset\\
\vars({\texttt{R}})=\vars(\operatorname{row}({\texttt{R}}))\end{array}$$\end{definition}
\par\begin{lemma}If $t_{1}\leq t_{2}$ with $t_{2}\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$
then $t_{1}\backslash\ell\leq t_{2}\backslash\ell$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof We show that for any $t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$, we have
$\llbracket t\backslash\ell\rrbracket^{\operatorname{row}}=\bigcup_{(\mathrm{Rec}({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud})^{V}:t)}{\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}\backslash\ell$,
where $\langle\!|(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle_{\emptyset}^{V}\backslash\ell=\{\langle\!|(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L{\smallsetminus}\ell},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle_{\{\ell\}}^{(V\smallsetminus V_{\ell})\cup V^{\prime}}|V^{\prime}\subseteq V_{\ell}\}$,
where for all $\ell\in L$, $V_{\ell}\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}\{\rho\mid\ell\in\dom(\rho)\}$.
\par We start with $\bigcup_{(\mathrm{Rec}({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud})^{V}:t)}{\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}\backslash\ell\subseteq\llbracket t\backslash\ell\rrbracket^{\operatorname{row}}$.
Let $(\mathrm{Rec}(\langle\!|(\ell=\delta_{\ell})_{\ell\in L\cup\{\ell\}},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle_{\emptyset}^{V})^{V_{0}}:t)$
and $t$ in DNF.
Let ${\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}_{\ell}=\langle\!|(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle_{\{\ell\}}^{(V\smallsetminus V_{\ell})\cup V^{\prime}}$ with $V^{\prime}\subseteq V_{\ell}$.
The proof is by induction on the top-level type connectives of $t$.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$t=\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$\ell\in L^{\prime}$ or $\varsigma\notin\mathcal{V}_{r}$.
Then $t\backslash\ell=\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\ell\}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\{\ell\}}$
and it is clear that $({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}_{\ell}:t\backslash\ell)$.
\par\itemize@item@$\ell\notin L^{\prime}$ and $\varsigma=\rho\in\mathcal{V}_{r}$.
Then $t\backslash\ell=\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\{\ell\}}$.
By construction, for each $\ell\in L^{\prime}$, we have either
$(\delta_{\ell}:t(\ell))$ if $\ell\in L$, or
$(\bot^{\emptyset}:t(\ell))$ otherwise.
So $({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}_{\ell}:t\backslash\ell)$ since there is no constraint on the
tagged set of variables in the interpretation of $t\backslash\ell$.
\end{itemize}\par\itemize@item@$t=\neg\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$\ell\in L^{\prime}$ or $\varsigma\notin\mathcal{V}_{r}$.
Without loss of generality, we can suppose $\ell\in L^{\prime}$ even in the
second case.
If $\tau_{\ell}\neq\mathbb{1}\lor\bot$, then
$t\backslash\ell=\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\{\ell\}}$ and $({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}_{\ell}:t\backslash\ell)$ is
trivial.
If $\tau_{\ell}=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot$, then $t\backslash\ell=\neg\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L^{\prime}\smallsetminus\{\ell\}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\{\ell\}}$
and there is $\ell^{\prime}\neq\ell$ such that $(\delta_{\ell}:t(\ell))$ is false,
or $(\bot^{\emptyset}:t(\ell))$ is false.
Thus, $({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}_{\ell}:t\backslash\ell)$.
\par\itemize@item@$\ell\notin L^{\prime}$ and $\varsigma=\rho\in\mathcal{V}_{r}$.
Then $t\backslash\ell=\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\{\ell\}}$ and $({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}_{\ell}:t\backslash\ell)$ is trivial.
\end{itemize}\par\itemize@item@$t=t_{1}\wedge t_{2}$.
We have $t\backslash\ell=t_{1}\backslash\ell\wedge t_{2}\backslash\ell$.
By induction hypothesis, $({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}\backslash\ell:t_{i}\backslash\ell)$
for $i\in\{1,2\}$, so $({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}\backslash\ell:t\backslash\ell)$.
\par\itemize@item@$t=t_{1}\vee t_{2}$.
We have $t\backslash\ell=t_{1}\backslash\ell\vee t_{2}\backslash\ell$.
By induction hypothesis, there is $i\in\{1,2\}$ such that
$({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}\backslash\ell:t_{i}\backslash\ell)$.
Thus, $({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}\backslash\ell:t\backslash\ell)$.
\end{itemize}
\par Now, we consider $\llbracket t\backslash\ell\rrbracket^{\operatorname{row}}\subseteq\bigcup_{(\mathrm{Rec}({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud})^{V}:t)}{\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}\backslash\ell$.
If $t\leq\mathbb{0}$, then by definition $t\backslash\ell\leq\mathbb{0}$.
Otherwise, let $({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}_{\ell}:t\backslash\ell)$ and
$t=\bigvee_{i\in I}\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P_{i}}{\texttt{R}}\wedge\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in N_{i}}\neg{\texttt{R}}$.
For each $i\in I$, let $P_{\mathcal{V}}^{i}=\{{\texttt{R}}\in P_{i}\mid\tail({\texttt{R}})=\rho\mathrel{\mathsf{and}}\ell\notin\rdef(\rho)\}$, similarly for $N_{\mathcal{V}}^{i}$.
By the reasoning of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:decomposition},
we have \@@amsalign
where $\operatorname{row}({\texttt{R}})\backslash{\ell}$ is the operation defined on positive atomic row
types in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:cutrow}.
This operation coincides with ${\texttt{R}}\backslash\ell$ on a positive and atomic ${\texttt{R}}$.
For each $i$ and $N^{\prime}_{i,\mathcal{V}}$, we have
$t_{N^{\prime}_{i,\mathcal{V}}}\backslash\ell=\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P_{i}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\operatorname{row}({\texttt{R}})\backslash{\ell}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in N_{i}^{\prime}{\smallsetminus}N^{\prime}_{i,\mathcal{V}}}\neg\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\operatorname{row}({\texttt{R}})\backslash\ell\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}})$.
By hypothesis, there are $i\in I$ and $N^{\prime}_{i,\mathcal{V}}$ such that
$({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}_{\ell}:t_{N^{\prime}_{i,\mathcal{V}}}\backslash\ell)$.
Let $\delta_{\ell}$ be such that $(\delta_{\ell}:\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P_{i}}{\texttt{R}}(\ell)\wedge\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in N^{\prime}_{i}}\neg{\texttt{R}}(\ell))$.
Let $V=(\Tag({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}_{\ell})\cup\{\tail({\texttt{R}})\mid{\texttt{R}}\in P^{i}_{\mathcal{V}}\})\smallsetminus\{\tail({\texttt{R}})\mid{\texttt{R}}\in N^{\prime}_{i,\mathcal{V}}\}$.
We take ${\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}$ to be ${\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}_{\ell}$ completed by $\ell=\delta$ and with
$\Tag({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud})=V$ and we have $(\mathrm{Rec}({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud})^{V}:t)$.
Since $\{\tail({\texttt{R}})\mid{\texttt{R}}\in P^{i}_{\mathcal{V}}\}$ and $\{\tail({\texttt{R}})\mid{\texttt{R}}\in N^{\prime}_{i,\mathcal{V}}\}$ are
subsets of $V_{\ell}$, we have ${\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}_{\ell}\in{\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}\backslash\ell$.
\par For the second case to work, we need to show that for all $t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$,
$t\backslash{\ell}\simeq\spl(t)\backslash{\ell}$,
where $\spl(t)$ is the type obtained by the previous decomposition.
First, let $t=\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P}{\texttt{R}}\wedge\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in N}\neg{\texttt{R}}$.
The proof is by induction on $|N|$.
Let $P_{\mathcal{V}}$ and $N_{\mathcal{V}}$ be defined as before relative to $P$ and $N$.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$N=\emptyset$. Then, $$\spl(t)=\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P}{\texttt{R}}(\ell)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\operatorname{row}({\texttt{R}})\backslash{\ell}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P_{\mathcal{V}}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\fin({\texttt{R}})\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\tail({\texttt{R}})\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$$and $\spl(t)\backslash\ell=\bigwedge_{R\in P}\operatorname{row}({\texttt{R}})\backslash{\ell}=t\backslash\ell$.
\par\itemize@item@$N=N_{0}\cup\{{\texttt{R}}_{n}=\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\tau\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\}$ where $r$ is an atomic row.
By equivalence, this covers all cases where $\tail({\texttt{R}}_{n})\notin\mathcal{V}_{r}$
and the ones where $\tail({\texttt{R}}_{n})=\rho$ and $\ell\notin\rdef(\rho)$.
Let $t_{0}=\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P}{\texttt{R}}\wedge\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in N_{0}}\neg{\texttt{R}}$,
so that $t\backslash\ell=t_{0}\backslash\ell\wedge{\texttt{R}}_{n}\backslash\ell$.
The type $t$ is decomposed as follows:
\@@amsalign
There are two cases.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$\tau=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot$.
Since $\neg\tau=\mathbb{0}$, the disjunction with $k=0$ is empty,
and the DNF of $\spl(t)$ is equal to
\@@amsalign
which is equal to $\spl(t_{0})\wedge\neg\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
So by induction hypothesis and since ${\texttt{R}}_{n}\backslash\ell=\neg r$, $$\spl(t)\backslash\ell=\spl(t_{0})\backslash\ell\wedge\neg r\simeq t_{0}\backslash\ell\wedge\neg r=t\backslash\ell$$\par\enumerate@item@$\tau\neq\mathbb{1}\lor\bot$.
Then, $\spl(t)\backslash\ell$ is equal to
\@@amsalign
So that $\spl(t)\backslash\ell$ is trivially equivalent to
$\spl(t_{0})\backslash\ell$,
and by induction hypothesis and the fact that ${\texttt{R}}_{n}\backslash\ell=\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\ell}$ we have
$\spl(t_{0})\backslash\ell\simeq t_{0}\backslash\ell=t\backslash\ell$.
\end{enumerate}\par\itemize@item@$N=N_{0}\cup\{{\texttt{R}}_{n}=\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}(\ell^{\prime}=\tau_{\ell}^{\prime})_{\ell^{\prime}\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\}$ where $\ell\notin L$.
Let $t_{0}=\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P}{\texttt{R}}\wedge\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in N_{0}}\neg{\texttt{R}}$,
so that $t\backslash\ell=t_{0}\backslash\ell\wedge{\texttt{R}}_{n}\backslash\ell$.
The type $t$ is decomposed as follows:
\@@amsalign
Thus, $\spl(t)\backslash$ is trivially equivalent to the type in case (2) above
(with $\neg\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell^{\prime}=\tau_{\ell}^{\prime})_{\ell^{\prime}\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\{\ell\}}$ instead of
$\neg r$ in the second line), which is equal to $\spl(t_{0})\backslash\ell$
and we conclude in the same way since here also ${\texttt{R}}_{n}\backslash\ell=\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\{\ell\}}$.
\end{itemize}
Now, if $t=\bigvee_{i\in I}t_{i}$ where the $t_{i}$'s are conjunctions,
we have $t\backslash\ell=\bigvee_{i\in I}(t_{i}\backslash\ell)\simeq\bigvee_{i\in I}(\spl(t_{i})\backslash\ell)=\spl(t)\backslash\ell$.
\qed\end@proof
\par\begin{lemma}Let $v=\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}v_{1}\;\texttt{with}\;\ell\;\texttt{=}\;v_{2}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$.
If there is a derivation $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}v:t$,
then there are derivations
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}v_{1}:t_{1}\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ and
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}v_{2}:t_{2}$ such that
$\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t_{2}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}t_{1}\backslash{\ell}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\leq t$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof By induction on $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}v:t$, with a case analysis on the last
rule used, that has to be of ({Ext}), ({Inter}) or
({Sub}).
\begin{description}\description@item@[\rulename{Ext}]Straightforward.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Inter}]We apply the induction hypothesis twice.
Since both types obtained are supertypes of
$\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t_{2}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}t_{1}\backslash\ell\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$,
their intersection is also.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Sub}]By induction hypothesis and transitivity of subtyping.
\qed\end{description}
\end@proof
\par\begin{lemma}Let $e$ be an expression and $t$ a type.
If $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t$ and $e\leadsto e^{\prime}$,
then $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e^{\prime}:t$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof The proof is by induction on the derivation of $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t$
and by a case analysis on the last rule used in the derivation of
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t$.
We detail the cases related to the rules for records, for the rest, see
{e.g.} \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{frisch08}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}.
\begin{description}\description@item@[\rulename{Emp}]$e=\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$, so it does not reduce.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Ext}]$e=\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}e_{1}\;\texttt{with}\;\ell\;\texttt{=}\;e_{2}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$.
Necessarily, we have $e^{\prime}=\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}e_{1}^{\prime}\;\texttt{with}\;\ell\;\texttt{=}\;e_{2}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$
or $e^{\prime}=\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}e_{1}\;\texttt{with}\;\ell\;\texttt{=}\;e_{2}^{\prime}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$ and this is direct by
induction hypothesis.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Sel}]If $e=e_{0}.\ell\leadsto e_{0}^{\prime}.\ell=e^{\prime}$
or if $e=\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}e_{1}\;\texttt{with}\;\ell^{\prime}\;\texttt{=}\;e_{2}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}.\ell$ and the reduction occurs in
$e_{1}$ or $e_{2}$, this is direct by induction hypothesis.
Otherwise, we have $e=\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}v\;\texttt{with}\;\ell^{\prime}\;\texttt{=}\;v^{\prime}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}.\ell$ and
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}v\;\texttt{with}\;\ell^{\prime}\;\texttt{=}\;v^{\prime}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}:\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:inversion}, there are derivations
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}v:t_{1}\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell^{\prime}=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$
and $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}v^{\prime}:t_{2}$
such that $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell^{\prime}=t_{2}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}t_{1}\backslash\ell^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@If $\ell=\ell^{\prime}$, then $e^{\prime}=v^{\prime}$.
Since $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t_{2}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}t_{1}\backslash\ell\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$,
we have $t_{2}\leq t$ and we conclude by ({Sub}).
\par\itemize@item@If $\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}$, then $e^{\prime}=v.\ell$.
Since $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell^{\prime}=t_{2}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}t_{1}\backslash\ell^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$,
we have $t_{1}\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t,\ell^{\prime}=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
We conclude by rules\penalty\ ({Sub}) and\penalty\ ({Sel}).
\end{itemize}\par\description@item@[\rulename{Del}]If $e=\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\backslash\ell\leadsto\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$,
since $\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\backslash\ell=\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$, we can use the same
derivation.
If $e=\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}e_{1}\;\texttt{with}\;\ell^{\prime}\;\texttt{=}\;e_{2}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\backslash\ell$ and the reduction
occurs in $e_{1}$ or $e_{2}$, this is direct by induction hypothesis.
Otherwise, we have $e=\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}v\;\texttt{with}\;\ell^{\prime}\;\texttt{=}\;v^{\prime}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}\backslash\ell$.
By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:inversion}, there are derivations
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}v:t_{1}\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell^{\prime}=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$
and $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}v^{\prime}:t_{2}$
such that $\boldsymbol{\{}t_{1}\;\texttt{with}\;\ell^{\prime}\;\texttt{=}\;t_{2}\boldsymbol{\}}\leq t$.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@If $\ell=\ell^{\prime}$, then $e^{\prime}=v\backslash\ell$.
Since $t_{1}\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$, $v$ can be typed with
$t_{1}=t_{1}\backslash\ell=\boldsymbol{\{}t_{1}\;\texttt{with}\;\ell\;\texttt{=}\;t_{2}\boldsymbol{\}}\backslash\ell$.
We conclude because $\boldsymbol{\{}t_{1}\;\texttt{with}\;\ell\;\texttt{=}\;t_{2}\boldsymbol{\}}\leq t$.
\par\itemize@item@If $\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}$, then $e^{\prime}=\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}v\backslash\ell\;\texttt{with}\;\ell^{\prime}\;\texttt{=}\;v^{\prime}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$.
There is a derivation $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}v\backslash\ell:t_{1}\backslash\ell$.
Since $\boldsymbol{\{}t_{1}\backslash\ell\;\texttt{with}\;\ell^{\prime}\;\texttt{=}\;t_{2}\boldsymbol{\}}=\boldsymbol{\{}t_{1}\;\texttt{with}\;\ell^{\prime}\;\texttt{=}\;t_{2}\boldsymbol{\}}\backslash\ell$,
we conclude by rule\penalty\ ({Ext}).
\end{itemize}\par\description@item@[\rulename{Inst}]Direct by induction hypothesis.
\qed\end{description}
\end@proof
\par\begin{lemma}Let $v$ be a value such that
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}v:t$ with $t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
Then $v$ has one of the forms $\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$ or $\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}v_{1}\;\texttt{with}\;\ell\;\texttt{=}\;v_{2}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof It is easy to see that a derivation for $v$ is obtained by a rule
({Emp}) followed by rules\penalty\ ({Ext}), ({Inter}) or
({Sub}).
Remark that there are no rules\penalty\ ({Inst}) because it is impossible to
derive a polymorphic type $t$ for $v$, in particular since for instance
$\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\epsilon\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\nleq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
Moreover, we can show by induction on the depth of the derivation that if
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}v:t$ is derivable, then $t\nsimeq\mathbb{0}$.
The proof is by induction on the derivation.
\begin{description}\description@item@[\rulename{Emp}]This is the base case, where $v=\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Ext}]$v$ is of the second form.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Inter}]$t_{1}$ and $t_{2}$ are reducible to disjunctive normal
forms $t_{1}^{\texttt{R}}\wedge t_{1}^{\prime}$ and $t_{2}^{\texttt{R}}\wedge t_{2}^{\prime}$,
such that $t_{1}^{\texttt{R}},t_{2}^{\texttt{R}}\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ and by hypothesis,
$t_{1}^{\prime}\wedge t_{2}^{\prime}\leq\mathbb{0}$.
We can show by induction on the derivation of $v$ that this last property
does not hold if $v$ is not a record expression.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Sub}]We have $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}v:t^{\prime}\leq t$.
$t^{\prime}\nleq\mathbb{0}$ since $v$ is a value, so we can apply the induction
hypothesis.
\qed\end{description}
\end@proof
\par\begin{lemma}Let $e$ be a well-typed closed expression, that is,
$\emptyset\mid\emptyset\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t$ for some $t$.
If $e$ is not a value, then
there exists an expression $e^{\prime}$ such that $e\leadsto e^{\prime}$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof The proof is by induction on the derivation of $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t$
and by a case analysis on the last rule used in the derivation of
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t$.
We detail the cases related to records, for the rest, see
{e.g.}\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{frisch08}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}.
\begin{description}\description@item@[\rulename{Emp}]$e=\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$ is a value.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Ext}]$e=\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}e_{1}\;\texttt{with}\;\ell\;\texttt{=}\;e_{2}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$.
If $e_{1}$ or $e_{2}$ can be reduced, $e$ can also.
Otherwise, $e_{1}$ and $e_{2}$ are values by induction and so is $e$.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Del}]$e=e_{0}\backslash\ell$.
If $e_{0}$ can be reduced, so can $e$.
Otherwise, we have by induction hypothesis that $e_{0}$ is a value.
By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:generation}, either $e_{0}=\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}v\;\texttt{with}\;\ell^{\prime}\;\texttt{=}\;v^{\prime}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$
and $e$ reduces with $\mathsf{[R}_{\mathsf{del}}^{=}\mathsf{]}$ or $\mathsf{[R}_{\mathsf{del}}^{\neq}\mathsf{]}$,
or $e_{0}=\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$ and $e$ reduces with
$\mathsf{[R}_{\mathsf{emp}}\mathsf{]}$.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Sel}]$e=e_{0}.\ell$.
If $e_{0}$ can be reduced, so can $e$.
Otherwise, we have by induction hypothesis that $e_{0}$ is a value.
By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:generation}, $e_{0}=\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}v\;\texttt{with}\;\ell^{\prime}\;\texttt{=}\;v^{\prime}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$ or $e_{0}=\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$.
In the first case, $e$ reduces with
$\mathsf{[R}_{\mathsf{del}}^{=}\mathsf{]}$ or $\mathsf{[R}_{\mathsf{del}}^{\neq}\mathsf{]}$.
The second case is impossible, since there is no derivation for $e_{0}$ of
type $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Inst}]Directly by induction hypothesis.
\qed\end{description}
\end@proof
\par See \ref{restatable:typesoundness}
\@proof Consequence of \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{l:sr} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{l:progress}.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Algorithmic Type System}
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Field selection}
\par We remind here the definition of the field selection operator.
See \ref{restatable:typesel}
For an arbitrary type $t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\mathbb{1}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$, we define $t.\ell\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}(\dnf(t\wedge\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}))$.
\par\begin{lemma}Let $t=\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P}{\texttt{R}}\wedge\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in N}\neg{\texttt{R}}$ and $\rho\in\mathcal{V}_{r}$ such that $\rho\notin\tlv(\operatorname{row}(t))$.
Then, $t\wedge\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}\rdef(\rho)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\leq\mathbb{0}\iff t\leq\mathbb{0}$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof Because $\rho\notin\tlv(\operatorname{row}(t))$,
the set of elements $\mathrm{Rec}({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud})\in\llbracket t..\rrbracket$ is exactly the
elements of $\llbracket t\rrbracket$, where $\rho$ is added from $\Tag({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud})$.
\qed\end@proof
\par\begin{lemma}Let $t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\mathbb{1}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
Then, for all $t_{\ell}$, $t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t_{\ell}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\iff t.\ell\leq t_{\ell}$.
In particular, $t.\ell\leq\mathbb{1}$ and $t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t.\ell\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:decomposition}, $t$ is equivalent to:
\@@amsalign
where $P_{\mathcal{V}}=\{{\texttt{R}}\in P\mid\tail({\texttt{R}})=\rho\mathrel{\mathsf{and}}\ell\in\rdef(\rho)\}$, similarly for $N_{\mathcal{V}}$,
and because for any atom ${\texttt{R}}$, $\operatorname{row}({\texttt{R}})\backslash{\{\ell\}}={\texttt{R}}\backslash\ell$.
Then, for any $t_{\ell}$ it is clear that $t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t_{\ell}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ is
equivalent to $t.\ell\leq t_{\ell}$.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par\par\begin{corollary}Let $t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\mathbb{1}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ and $[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}$ be a set of substitutions.
Then $(\bigwedge_{i\in I}t\sigma_{i}).\ell\leq\bigwedge_{i\in I}t.\ell\sigma$.
\end{corollary}
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Taming non-structural rules}
\par To prove soundness and completeness of the algorithmic type system, we go
through an intermediate type system, where the intersection rule is n-ary, and
the introduction of a term variable can perform a renaming of polymorphic variables.
The introduction of a renaming aims at eliminating trivial instantiations (only
renamings) in the uses of ({Inst}).
More details are given by
\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna24b}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Section I.1)}.$$\frac{}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}x:\Gamma(x)\sigma}\quad\textsc{left=({Var}),
right={$\begin{array}[]{l}x\in\dom(\Gamma)\\[-1.99168pt]
\dom(\sigma)\cap\Delta=\emptyset\\[-1.99168pt]
\text{and $\sigma$ is a renaming}\end{array}$}}\quad\frac{(\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:t_{i})_{i\in I}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:\bigwedge_{i\in I}t_{i}}\quad\textsc{left=({Inter}),
right={$|I|>0$}}$$
Rules different from ({Inst}) and ({Var}) are the same as in the
declarative type system.
\par\begin{lemma}$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:t\iff\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{D}}e:t$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof The left-to-right direction is straightforward since the rules in the
intermediate system generalize the ones of the declarative system.
The right-to-left direction is obtained by replacing instances of
({Var}) by instances of ({Var}) followed by ({Inst}) in
the declarative system, and by replacing occurences of n-ary intersections by
$n-1$ ({Inter}) nodes.
\qed\end@proof
\par Next, we want to restrict derivations in the intermediate system to a canonical
form, where the apparition of ({Inst}) and ({Sub}) nodes is controlled.
For convenience, we introduce the following rule macro:
$$\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:s}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:t}\quad\textsc{left=({$\sqsubseteq$}),right={$s\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t$}}$$
which is a stands for ({Sub}) when $s\leq t$ and otherwise for:
\begin{equation*}\frac{\frac{\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:s}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:s\sigma_{i}}\hskip 8.19447pt\textsc{Left=({Inst}),right={$\forall i\in I$}}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:\bigwedge_{i\in I}s\sigma_{i}}\quad\textsc{Left=({Inter})}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:t}\quad\textsc{Left=({Sub})}\end{equation*}
\par\begin{definition}A derivation is \emph{canonical} if
every ({Inst}) node it contains is part of a ({$\sqsubseteq$})
pattern and
every ({$\sqsubseteq$}) and ({Sub}) nodes are either:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@The premise of an ({Del}) or ({Sel}) node, or
\par\itemize@item@The first premise of a ({Ext}) node, or
\par\itemize@item@One of the premises of an ({Abs}) or ({App}) node.
\end{itemize}\end{definition}
\par\begin{lemma}A derivation of $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:t$ can be transformed into a
derivation $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:t\sigma$, for any renaming $\sigma$ such
that $\dom(\sigma)\cap\Delta=\emptyset$, without changing the structure of
the derivation.
\end{lemma}
\@proof As in \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna24b}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Lemma I.14)}.
\qed\end@proof
\par\begin{lemma}Let $I$, $\Delta$ and $t_{i}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t_{i}$ for all $i\in I$. Then,
$\bigwedge_{i\in I}t_{i}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}\bigwedge_{i\in I}t_{i}$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof As in \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna24b}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Proposition I.15)}.
\qed\end@proof
\par\begin{lemma}Let $s^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}s$, $\ell$ and $r$ of definition space $\mathcal{L}\smallsetminus\ell$
such that $\vars(s^{\prime})\cap\vars(r)\subseteq\Delta$.
Then, $\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=s^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=s\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof Let $\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i\in I}$ such that $\bigwedge_{i\in I}s^{\prime}\sigma_{i}\leq s$,
with $\dom(\sigma)\subseteq\vars(s^{\prime})$.
We have $\bigwedge_{i\in I}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=s^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\sigma_{i}\simeq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\bigwedge_{i\in I}s^{\prime}\sigma_{i}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=s\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
\qed\end@proof
\par\begin{lemma}Any derivation of $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:t$ can be transformed into a
canonical derivation of $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:t^{\prime}$, where
$t^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof By induction on the size of the derivation and through a case analysis on the
root of the derivation tree used.
\begin{description}\description@item@[\rulename{Inst} or \rulename{Sub}]We remove the root and let its
premise be the new one.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Inter}]Let $t=\bigwedge_{i\in I}s_{i}$.
By induction hypothesis, for all $i\in I$ we have derivations
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:s_{i}^{\prime}$ with $s_{i}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}s_{i}$.
By rule ({Inter}), we have a derivation of
$t^{\prime}=\bigwedge_{i\in I}s_{i}^{\prime}$, and $t^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t$ is verified by
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:leqsubcommute}.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Const}, \rulename{Var}, \rulename{Emp}]Alredy canonical.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Ext}]Let $e=\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}e_{1}\;\texttt{with}\;\ell\;\texttt{=}\;e_{2}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}$ and
$t=\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t_{2}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}t_{1}\backslash\ell\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
By induction hypothesis, we have canonical derivations
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e_{1}:t_{1}^{\prime}$ and $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e_{2}:t_{2}^{\prime}$ with $t_{1}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t_{1}$ and $t_{2}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t_{2}$.
By rule\penalty\ ({$\sqsubseteq$}), we derive $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e_{1}:t_{1}$.
Let $t^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t_{2}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\ell\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}{t_{1}}$.
Rule\penalty\ ({Ext}) node gives a canonical derivation of
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:t^{\prime}$.
We can suppose that the variables in $t_{2}^{\prime}$ and $t_{1}$ are disjoint
(otherwise we use \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:renaming}), and conclude $t^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t$ by
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:extrec_leqsub}.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Abs}]By induction hypothesis, for each $i\in I$ we have derivations
$\Delta\cup\vars(t)\mid\Gamma,x:t_{i}\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:s_{i}^{\prime}$ with $s_{i}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}s_{i}$.
By rule ({$\sqsubseteq$}), we have derivations
$\Delta\cup\vars(t)\mid\Gamma,x:t_{i}\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:s_{i}$ and we conclude by
rule\penalty\ ({Abs}).
\par\description@item@[\rulename{App}, \rulename{Sel}, \rulename{Del}]Similar to the
previous case.
\qed\end{description}
\end@proof
\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Soundness and completeness}
\par See \ref{restatable:soundnessalgo}
\@proof By induction on the algorithmic typing derivation.
By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:equiv_intermediate}, it sufices to give a derivation
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:t$.
We proceed by a case analysis on the last rule used in the derivation.
\begin{description}\description@item@[\rulename{Const}, \rulename{Var}]Straightforward.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Abs}]By induction hypothesis, for each $i\in I$ we have
$\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}\mid\Gamma,x:t_{i}\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:s_{i}^{\prime}$.
Since $s_{i}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}}s_{i}$, we derive
$\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}\mid\Gamma,x:t_{i}\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:s_{i}$ by rule
({$\sqsubseteq$}).
We conclude by rule\penalty\ ({Abs}) in the declarative system.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{App}]By hypothesis, we have $t\in t_{1}\bullet_{\Delta}t_{2}$,
so $t$ is such that there are two substitution sets with
$\bigwedge_{j\in J}t_{1}\sigma_{j}\leq\bigwedge_{i\in I}t_{2}\sigma_{i}\to t$.
By induction hypothesis and ({$\sqsubseteq$}),
we obtain $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e_{1}:\bigwedge_{j\in J}t_{1}\sigma_{j}$
and $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e_{2}:\bigwedge_{i\in I}t_{2}\sigma_{i}$.
By\penalty\ ({Sub}), we have $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e_{1}:\bigwedge_{i\in I}t_{2}\sigma_{i}\to t$.
We conclude by rule\penalty\ ({App}) in the declarative system.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Emp}]Straightforward.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Ext}]By induction hypothesis, we have
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:t$, $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e^{\prime}:t^{\prime}$
and sets of substitutions $[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}$ such that $\bigwedge_{i\in I}t\sigma_{i}\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ and
$r=(\bigwedge_{i\in I}t\sigma_{i})\backslash\ell$.
By rules\penalty\ ({Inst}) and\penalty\ ({Inter}), we have
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:\bigwedge_{i\in I}t\sigma_{i}$,
and we conclude with rule\penalty\ ({Del}).
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Del}]Similar to the case for\penalty\ ({Ext}), without the
derivation of $t^{\prime}$.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Sel}]By induction hypothesis, we have
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:t$ and a set of substitutions $[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}$ such that $\bigwedge_{i\in I}t\sigma_{i}\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\mathbb{1}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$
and $t_{\ell}=(\bigwedge_{i\in I}t\sigma_{i}).\ell$.
By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:correct_sel}, we have $\bigwedge_{i\in I}t\sigma_{i}\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t_{\ell}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$, so we conclude with rule\penalty\ ({$\sqsubseteq$})
and\penalty\ ({Sel}).
\qed\end{description}
\end@proof
\par\par\par See \ref{restatable:completenessalgo}
\@proof By \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{l:equiv_intermediate} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{l:canonical} we transform the input derivation
into a canonical derivation $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:s$,
where $s\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t$.
The proof is by induction on that derivation (where we use
({$\sqsubseteq$}) instead of the corresponding pattern).
In the end, we obtain $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e:t^{\prime}$ with $t^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}s$
and thus conclude since by transitivity of $\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}$, we have $t^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t$.
\begin{description}\description@item@[\rulename{Const}, \rulename{Var}]Straightforward.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Abs}]By induction hypothesis, for each $i\in I$, we have
$\Delta\cup\Delta^{\prime}\mid\Gamma,x:t_{i}\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e:s_{i}$
with $s_{i}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}s_{i}$.
We conclude with rule\penalty\ ({Abs}) in the algorithmic system.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{App}]By induction hypothesis, we have
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e_{1}:t$
and $\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e_{2}:s$,
where $t\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t_{1}\to t_{2}$ and $s\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t_{1}$.
So $t_{2}\in t\bullet_{\Delta}s$ since $(t_{1}\to t_{2})\cdot t_{1}=t_{2}$.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Emp}]Straightforward.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Ext}]By induction hypothesis, we have
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e:s$,
a set of substitutions such that
$[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}\Vdash s\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$,
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e^{\prime}:s^{\prime}$ and $s^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t^{\prime}$.
Thus, we have $[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}\Vdash s\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
Since $t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
Let $r=(\bigwedge_{i\in I}t\sigma_{i})\backslash\ell$.
By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:cutrow_correct}, we have $r\leq t\backslash\ell$, so that
$\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=s^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}{t\backslash\ell}$.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Del}]By induction hypothesis, we have
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e:s$ and a set of substitutions such that
$[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}\Vdash s\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
Thus, we have $[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}\Vdash s\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
Let $r=(\bigwedge_{i\in I}t\sigma_{i})\backslash\ell$.
By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:cutrow_correct}, we have $r\leq t\backslash\ell$, so that
$\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}t\backslash\ell\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Sel}]By induction hypothesis, we have
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{A}}e:s$ and a set of substitutions such that
$[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}\Vdash s\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
Thus, we have $[\sigma_{i}]_{i\in I}\Vdash s\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\mathbb{1}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
Let $t_{\ell}=(\bigwedge_{i\in I}s\sigma_{i}).\ell$.
We have $t_{\ell}\in\amalg_{\Delta}^{\ell}(s)$, and by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:correct_sel}
$t_{\ell}\leq t$ so $t_{\ell}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}t$.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{$\leqsub{}$}]Straightforward by induction hypothesis and
transitivy of $\sqsubseteq$.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Sub}]Straightforward by induction hypothesis, inclusion of
$\leq$ in $\sqsubseteq$ and transitivity of $\sqsubseteq$.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Inter}]By hypothesis, there is $I$ and derivations
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:s_{i}$ for all $\{s_{i}\}_{i\in I}$,
with $t=\bigwedge_{i\in I}s_{i}$.
Since the derivation is canonical, we know that each of these derivations
ends with (the same kind of) structural rule.
According to the other cases, for all $i\in I$ we have derivations
$\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash_{\scriptscriptstyle m}e:s_{i}^{\prime}$, where $s_{i}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}s_{i}$.
By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:leqsubcommute} we have $t^{\prime}=\bigwedge_{i\in I}s_{i}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq_{\Delta}\bigwedge_{i\in I}s_{i}=t$.
\qed\end{description}
\end@proof
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsubsection}{toc}{Alternative incomplete type system}
\par\begin{figure}\begin{equation*}\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash e:t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash e^{\prime}:t^{\prime}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}e\;\texttt{with}\;\ell\;\texttt{=}\;e^{\prime}\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}:\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=t^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}t\backslash\ell\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}}\quad\textsc{left=({Ext})}\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash e:t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash e\backslash\ell:\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\ell=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}t\backslash\ell\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}}\quad\textsc{left=({Del})}\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash e:t}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash e.\ell:t.\ell}\quad\textsc{left=({Sel})}\end{equation*}The rules ({Const}), ({Var}), ({Abs}),
({App}), ({Emp}) are the same as in the algorithmic system.
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{4}}{Alternative algorithmic system}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 4}}{\small Alternative algorithmic system}}}\end{figure}
\par\begin{lemma}The type system in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:altrules} is sound with respect to the
declarative type system.
\end{lemma}
\@proof Similar to the proof of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{t:soundness_algo}.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par\@@numbered@section{appendix}{toc}{APPENDIX FOR TALLYING}
\par We start with giving formal definitions of several notions mentioned in the text.
\par\begin{definition}A constraint $(T_{1},c,T_{2})$ is a triple such that
$c\in\{\leq,\geq\}$ and $(T_{1},T_{2})\in(\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T})\cup(\mathcal{T}_{\bot}\times\mathcal{T}_{\bot})\cup(\mathcal{R}\times\mathcal{R})$.
$T_{1}$ and $T_{2}$ must be of the same kind which, in particular,
implies $\rdef(T_{1})=\rdef(T_{2})$ if $T_{1}$ and $T_{2}$ are rows.
We denote with $\mathcal{C}$ the set of all constraints.
\par Given two sets of constraints
$\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})$, we define
their union as $\mathcal{S}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{S}_{2}=\mathcal{S}_{1}\cup\mathcal{S}_{2}$ and
their intersection as $\mathcal{S}_{1}\sqcap\mathcal{S}_{2}=\{C_{1}\cup C_{2}\mid C_{1}\in\mathcal{S}_{1},C_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{2}\}$.
\end{definition}
\par\begin{definition}Given a constraint-set $C\subseteq\mathcal{C}$, the set of type row and
field variables occurring in $C$ is defined as
$\vars(C)=\bigcup_{(T_{1},c,T_{2})\in C}\vars(T_{1})\cup\vars(T_{2})$,
with $\vars(T_{i})$ defined in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:vars}.
\end{definition}
\par\begin{definition}Let $V$ and $\Delta$ be sets of variables and $L$ a set of labels.
An ordering $O_{X}$ on $V$ is an injective map from $V$ to $\mathbb{N}$,
an ordering $O_{\ell}$ on $L$ is an injective map from $L$ to $\mathbb{N}$.
An ordering $O$ on $V$ and $L$ is defined as a lexicographic ordering on
$\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}$ according to $O_{X}$ and $O_{\ell}$, where:
$O(\rho.\ell)=(O_{X}(\rho),O_{\ell}(\ell))$,
$O(\rho\backslash{L})=(O_{X}(\rho),O^{\prime}(\rho,L))$ and
$O(X)=(O_{X}(X),0))$ otherwise;
for all $X_{1}\notin\Delta$ and $X_{2}\in\Delta$ of the same
kind, $O(X_{1})<O(X_{2})$;
and $O^{\prime}(\rho,L)$ is an integer obtained in a canonical way from the set $L\cup\rdef(\rho)$ and different from any $O_{\ell}(\ell)$.
\end{definition}
\par\begin{definition}We introduce two new constructors:
(1)\penalty\ $\rho.\ell$, that we treat as a field variable, and
(2) $\rho\backslash{L}$, that we treat as a row variable of definition space $\rdef(\rho){\smallsetminus}L$.
Substitution is extended to $(\rho.\ell)\sigma=\tau$ if $\sigma(\rho)\simeq\boldsymbol{\langle}\ell=\tau\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L^{\prime}}$, and $(\rho\backslash{L})\sigma=r$ if
$\sigma(\rho)\simeq\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L\cap\rdef(\rho)}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L^{\prime}}$.
It is undefined if $\sigma(\rho)$ does not have any of these shapes.
\end{definition}
\noindent Hereafter, the name $\rho$ ranges over row variables and constructors
$\rho\backslash{L}$, the name $\theta$ ranges over field
variables and constructors $\rho.\ell$, and the name $X$ ranges
over all kinds of variables plus these new
constructors.
We consider $\rho\backslash{L}$ up to the equivalence generated by
identifying $\rho\in\mathcal{V}_{r}$ with $\rho\backslash{L}$ for all $L\subseteq\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}\rdef(\rho)$,
and $(\rho\backslash{L_{1}})\backslash{L_{2}}$ with $\rho\backslash{(L_{1}\cup L_{2})}$.
With an abuse of notation we write $\rho$ for the row
$\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}\rdef(\rho)}$---in particular in rows constraints (e.g., $(\rho,c,r)$)---when no confusion arises.
\par\begin{definition}Let $C\subseteq\mathcal{C}$ be a constraint-set.
A solution to $C$ is a substitution $\sigma$ such that
$\forall(T_{1},\leq,T_{2}){\in}C\,.\,T_{1}\sigma\leq T_{2}\sigma$ and
$\forall(T_{1},\geq,T_{2}){\in}C\,.\,T_{1}\sigma\geq T_{2}\sigma$ hold.
If $\sigma$ is a solution to $C$, we write $\sigma\Vdash C$.
In particular, $T_{1}\sigma$ and $T_{2}\sigma$ must be defined, that is,
for all $\rho\in\dom(\sigma)$:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@[-]$\forall\ell\in\mathcal{L}.\ \rho.\ell\in\vars(C)\implies\exists\tau.\exists r.\sigma(\rho)\simeq\boldsymbol{\langle}\ell=\tau\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}\rdef(\rho)}$, and
\par\itemize@item@[-]$\forall L\subseteq\mathcal{L}.\ \rho\backslash{L}\in\vars(C)\implies\exists(\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}.\exists r.\sigma(\rho)\simeq\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}\rdef(\rho)}$.
\end{itemize}where $\vars(C)$ is the set of all type/row/field variables occurring in $C$.
\end{definition}
\noindent The tallying algorithm
is parametric on a total order $O$ on variables, used to
ensure that the step ({5}) of tallying produces
contractive types (see \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{pr:wellformedness}).
A set of constraints is \emph{well-ordered} if for all constraints of the shape
$(X,c,T)$ and for all variables in $T$ that are not
occurring under a type constructor, $O(X)<O(X^{\prime})$ holds (we call such variables the \emph{toplevel
variables} of $T$).
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Examples Regarding the Restriction of Solutions to Atomic Rows}
\par We give two examples where we discuss considering only atomic rows instead
of Boolean combinations of them in the grammar.
The first one shows that we can find tallying solutions even for unions of
records thanks to the unification technique.
The second demonstrates that this technique is not enough to recover all desired
solutions, and therefore that Boolean combinations of rows, as we adopt them in
our system, are welcome.
\par\begin{example}Let us consider the types of the example on \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{ex:figure}, that we
rewrite as follows:
$t_{1}=\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}p=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\to\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}p=\text{{Float}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ and
$t_{2}=\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}s=\texttt{"circle"},p=\text{{Int}},d=\text{{Float}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\epsilon\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\vee\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}s=\texttt{"polygon"},p=\text{{Int}},e=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\epsilon\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
We would like to be able to unify the parameter of the function with the
argument, even though the latter is a union.
As explained in \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, \lx@sectionsign C.2.1)}, the problem of computing $t_{1}\bullet_{\emptyset}t_{2}$ can be reduced to solving $\{(t^{\prime},\leq,t_{2}\to\alpha)\}$,
where $\alpha$ is a fresh variable, and $t^{\prime}=\bigwedge_{i\in I}t_{1}\sigma_{i}$,
where the $\sigma_{i}$ are renamings of $\rho$.
The cardinality of $I$ will be increased during the search for a solution.
\par With a cardinality $|I|=1$, we need in particular to find a solution for the
constraint $(\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}p=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}},\geq,t_{2})$.
A component-wise unification gives the most precise solution (since we
restrict ourselves to atomic rows):
$\sigma(\rho)=\boldsymbol{\langle}s=\texttt{"circle"}\vee\texttt{"polygon"},d=\text{{Float}}\vee\bot,e=\text{{Int}}\vee\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\epsilon\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\{s\}}$.
\par This is however not the solution we want.
Incrementing the cardinality of $I$, we now look for a solution of
$\{((\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}p=Int\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{1}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\to\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}p=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{1}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}})\wedge(\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}p=Int\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{2}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\to\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}p=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{2}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}),\leq,t_{2}\to\alpha)\}$.
Thus, we look in particular for a solution to the constraint
$(\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}p=Int\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{1}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\vee\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}p=Int\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{2}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}},\geq,t_{2})$.
A component-wise unification gives the solution
$\sigma(\rho_{1})=\boldsymbol{\langle}s=\texttt{"circle"},d=\text{{Float}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\epsilon\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\{s\}},\sigma(\rho_{2})=\boldsymbol{\langle}s=\texttt{"polygon"},e=\text{{Int}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\epsilon\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\{s\}}$,
thanks to which we retrieve the desired solution, even with the restriction
that rows are all atomic.
\end{example}
\par\begin{example}This second examples illustrates why considering only atomic rows is not satisfactory.
Take $t=\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\to\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ and
$s=\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}a=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\neg\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\epsilon\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$ and let us look for a solution of
$t\bullet_{\emptyset}s$.
As in the previous example, this problem can be reduced to
solving $\{(t^{\prime},\leq,s\to\alpha)\}$, where $\alpha$ is a fresh variable,
and $t^{\prime}=\bigwedge_{i\in I}t\sigma_{i}$, where the $\sigma_{i}$ are renamings of $\rho$.
\par At first, we try $|I|=1$, so we look for a solution of $\{(\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\to\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}},\leq,\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}a=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\neg\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\epsilon\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\to\alpha)\}$.
After normalization, we obtain the constraint-set
$\{(\alpha,\geq,\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}),(\rho,\geq,\boldsymbol{\langle}a=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset}\wedge\neg\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\epsilon\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset})\}$.
If we were to consider only atomic rows, we could only give the solution
$\sigma(\rho)=\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset}$ and $\sigma(\alpha)=\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$.
\par To try to find a more precise solution, we run tallying again after
incrementing the cardinal of $I$.
This yields the following constraint-set:
$\{((\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{1}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\to\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{1}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}})\wedge(\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{2}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\to\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{2}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}),\leq,s\to\alpha)\}$,
which normalizes first to
$\{(\alpha,\geq,\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{1}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{2}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}),(\rho_{1}\vee\rho_{2},\geq,\boldsymbol{\langle}a=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset}\wedge\neg\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\epsilon\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset})\}$,
and then (assuming $O(\rho_{1})\leq O(\rho_{2})$) to
$\{(\alpha,\geq,\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{1}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}\wedge\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{2}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}),(\rho_{1},\geq,\boldsymbol{\langle}a=\bot\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset}\wedge\neg\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\epsilon\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset}\wedge\neg\rho_{2})\}$.
With atomic rows only, we still do not have a satisfactory solution,
and further expansions do not help.
\end{example}
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{General Decomposition of Rows}
\par\begin{definition}Let $r=\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{1}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{2}}$
and $L$ a finite set of labels.
We define $r\backslash{L}=\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{1}{\smallsetminus}L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{2}\cup L}$,
where $\varsigma^{\prime}=\textbf{..}$ if $\varsigma\in\mathcal{V}$ and $L\nsubseteq L_{1}\cup L_{2}$,
and $\varsigma^{\prime}=\varsigma$ otherwise.
\end{definition}
\par\begin{lemma}Let $r$ be an atomic row and $L$ a set of labels.
Let $r^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\langle}L\cap\fin(r)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\backslash{(L\cap\fin(r))}\boldsymbol{\rangle}$
if $\tail(r)=\rho$ and $\rdef(\rho)\cap L\neq\emptyset$, and
$r^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\langle}L\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\backslash{L}\boldsymbol{\rangle}$ otherwise.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$r\simeq\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=r(\ell))_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge r^{\prime}\simeq\bigwedge_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\langle}\ell=r(\ell)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge r^{\prime}$
\par\enumerate@item@$\boldsymbol{\langle}L\cap\fin(r)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\backslash{(L\cap\fin(r))}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\simeq\boldsymbol{\langle}L\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\backslash{L}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge\boldsymbol{\langle}\fin(r)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\tail(r)\boldsymbol{\rangle}$ if $\tail(r)\in\mathcal{V}$.
\end{enumerate}\end{lemma}
\@proof If $r^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\langle}L\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\backslash{L}\boldsymbol{\rangle}$, the proof is straightforward.
Otherwise, let $r=\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{1}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{2}}$, with $L\nsubseteq L_{1}\cup L_{2}$.
For the first item, since $r(\ell)=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot$ for any $\ell\notin L_{1}\cap L_{2}$, we must show
$r\simeq\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L\cap L_{1}},(\ell=\mathbb{1}\lor\bot)_{\ell\in L\smallsetminus L_{1}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge\boldsymbol{\langle}L\cap L_{1},(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{1}\smallsetminus L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\rangle}$.
For the second item, we must show
$\boldsymbol{\langle}L\cap L_{1},(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{1}\smallsetminus L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\rangle}\simeq\boldsymbol{\langle}L,(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{1}{\smallsetminus}L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge\boldsymbol{\langle}\fin(r)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\rangle}$.
Both of them are straightforward.
\qed\end@proof
\par\begin{lemma}Let $P$ and $N$ be sets of atomic rows of the same definition space and $L$ be a
finite set of labels.
Let $P_{\mathcal{V}}=\{r\in P\mid\tail(r)=\rho\mathrel{\mathsf{and}}L\cap\rdef(\rho)\neq\emptyset\}$,
similarly for $N_{\mathcal{V}}$.
The relation $\bigwedge_{r\in P}r\leq\bigvee_{r\in N}r$
holds {iff} for every map
$\iota:N\to L\cup\{\_\}$,
for every $N^{\prime}\subseteq\iota^{-1}(\_)\cap N_{\mathcal{V}}$:
\@@amsalign
\end{lemma}
\par\@proof For each $r\in P_{\mathcal{V}}\cup N_{\mathcal{V}}$,
let $r_{-}=\boldsymbol{\langle}L\cap\fin(r)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\backslash{(L\cap\fin(r)}\boldsymbol{\rangle}$
and for each $r\in(P\cup N)\smallsetminus(P_{\mathcal{V}}\cup N_{\mathcal{V}})$,
let $r_{-}=\boldsymbol{\langle}L\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\backslash{L}\boldsymbol{\rangle}$.
\par Using \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:normalize_row}, we decompose the type
in the statement into:
\begin{equation}\bigwedge_{r\in P}(\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=r(\ell))_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge r_{-}\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in N}(\bigvee_{\ell\in L}\neg\boldsymbol{\langle}\ell=r(\ell)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\vee\neg r_{-})\end{equation}
We can distribute the intersection of the elements of $N$ on the right of
\eqref{eq:first-step-decomposition} over the unions in the second brackets.
We obtain a union of intersections of, each time, $|N|$ elements, where each
intersection is a possible combination of the individual types present in the
second line.
Each combination is described by a function $\iota:N\to L\cup\{\_\}$, where $\iota(n)=\ell$ means that the element $\boldsymbol{\langle}\ell=\neg r(\ell)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}$ is present in the combination given by $\iota$, while
$\iota(n)=\_$ means that the element $\neg r_{-}$ is present in the
combination.
For each $r\in N$ and $\ell\in L$, let us write $r_{\ell}=\boldsymbol{\langle}\ell=r(\ell)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}$.
Therefore the type in\penalty\ \eqref{eq:first-step-decomposition} is equivalent to:
\begin{equation}\bigwedge_{r\in P}(\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=r(\ell))_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge r_{-})\wedge\bigvee_{\iota:N\to L\cup\{\_\}}(\bigwedge_{r\in N}\neg r_{\iota(r)})\end{equation}
By distributing the intersection over the union we obtain
\begin{equation}\bigvee_{\iota:N\to L\cup\{\_\}}\left(\bigwedge_{r\in P}(\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=r(\ell))_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge r_{-})\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in N}\neg r_{\iota(r)}\right)\end{equation}
A union is empty if and only if each summand of the union is
empty. Therefore the type above is empty if and only if for all $\iota:N\to L\cup\{\_\}$,
the following type is empty:
\@@amsalign
Let $r_{1}=\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell={\textstyle{\bigwedge_{r\in P}}}r(\ell)\wedge{\textstyle{\bigwedge_{r\in\iota^{-1}(\ell)}}}\neg r(\ell))_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}$.
The last type is equivalent to
\@@amsalign
This type is empty if and only if the conjunctions are all empty for each
$\iota$ and $N^{\prime}\subseteq\iota^{-1}(\_)\cap N_{\mathcal{V}}$.
Take $\iota$ and $N^{\prime}$ and let $r_{2}=\bigwedge_{r\in P}\boldsymbol{\langle}L\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\backslash{L}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in\iota^{-1}(\_)\smallsetminus N^{\prime}}\neg\boldsymbol{\langle}L\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\backslash{L}\boldsymbol{\rangle}$
and $r_{3}=\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in P_{\mathcal{V}}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\fin(r)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\tail(r)\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in N^{\prime}}\neg\boldsymbol{\langle}\fin(r)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\tail(r)\boldsymbol{\rangle}$.
Let $r_{\iota}=\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq 3}r_{i}$.
It is immediate that $r_{1}$ is empty {iff} the first condition of the
statement holds, $r_{2}$ is empty {iff} the second does,
and $r_{3}$ is empty {iff} the third does.
We directly obtain that if one of the conditions holds, then the type
$r_{\iota}$ is empty.
We now show that if $r_{\iota}$ is empty, then there is $1\leq i\leq 3$ such
that $r_{i}$ is empty.
\par For this, we suppose that none of the subtypes is empty and build an element
${\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}\in\llbracket r_{\iota}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{row}}$.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@Since $r_{1}$ is not empty, for all $\ell\in L$ there is an
element $\delta_{\ell}^{1}\in\llbracket\bigwedge_{r\in P}r(\ell)\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in\iota^{-1}(\ell)}\neg r(\ell)\rrbracket^{\operatorname{fld}}$.
\par\enumerate@item@Since $r_{2}$ is not empty, there is an element
$\langle\!|(\ell=\delta^{2}_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{2}},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle^{V_{2}}\in\llbracket r_{2}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{row}}$.
\par\enumerate@item@Since $r_{3}$ is not empty, there is an element ${\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}_{3}\in\llbracket r_{3}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{row}}$.
However, the restrictions on the set of elements in
$\llbracket r_{3}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{row}}$ only concern their tags so that any
element ${\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}^{\prime}$ with $\Tag({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}^{\prime})=\Tag({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}_{3})$ and $\rdef({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}^{\prime})=\rdef({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}_{3})$ is in $\llbracket r_{3}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{row}}$.
Let $V_{3}=\Tag({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}_{3})$.
\end{enumerate}
We build the element
$\langle\!|(\ell=\delta^{1}_{\ell})_{\ell\in L},(\ell=\delta^{2}_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{2}{\smallsetminus}L},\_=\bot^{\emptyset}|\!\rangle^{V_{3}}$.
This element belongs to $\llbracket r_{\iota}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{row}}$, which is a
contradiction.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Explanation of Normalization of Constraints}
\par Before giving a formal description of the whole normalization algorithm as
inference rules in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:normalization},
we describe it as a recursive function $\norm(t,M)$ that takes a type
$t$ as input and returns a set of normalized constraint-sets necessary for $t\leq\mathbb{0}$ to hold.
The additional argument $M$ is a set of visited types, that guarantees
termination of recursion on infinitary types.
We consider the implicit constant parameter $\Delta$ containing the monomorphic variables.
\par We first rewrite each constraint $(T,c,T^{\prime})$ into a set of constraints
$\{(T_{i},\leq,\mathbb{0})\}_{i\in I}$, where $T_{i}$ is a conjunction of
atoms (basic types, arrows, records), type and field variables (in the
latter case,
$\bot$ can also be present), or their negations. This first constraint-set is obtained by transforming the type into DNF and
putting each summand of the outer union into a separate constraint.
For each constraint $(T_{i},\leq,\mathbb{0})$ we then isolate the smallest
(w.r.t., the order $O$) top-level type variable or
field variable $X$ not in $\Delta$, to obtain a constraint
of the form $(X,c,T_{i}^{\prime})$, that gives a lower (i.e., $c$ is $\geq$ when $X$ is
negated in $T_{i}$) or an upper (i.e., $c$ is $\leq$ otherwise)
bound on that variable, where
$T_{i}^{\prime}$ is obtained from $T_{i}$ simply by removing $X$.
\par There may be no variable in $T_{i}$, or they may all be in the parameter $\Delta$.
A variable in $\Delta$ is monomorphic, cannot be instantiated and is treated as
a constant.
In that case, we erase monomorphic variables because they cannot help to satisfy
the constraint.
On basic types, we can directly see if the constraint holds.
For arrow constructors, we apply subtyping to decompose the types into
constraints on their subtypes.
Until now, all these steps (apart from dealing with field variables) are similar
to those of the existing tallying algorithm for type variables by\penalty\ \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}}.
For a conjunction of record atoms, the technique is more elaborated.
\par The normalization of a conjunction of records $t$ is defined as the
normalization of its underlying row through an auxiliary procedure on rows:
$$\norm(t,M)=\operatorname{norm}_{\operatorname{row}}(\operatorname{row}(t),M\cup\{t\})$$
\par The main technical part of the normalization step of tallying is defining this
auxiliary procedure.
It uses the following two operators.
\begin{definition}Let $r=\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{1}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{2}}$, $\Delta$ be a
set of variables, $\ell\in\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}L_{2}$ and $L\in\mathcal{P}_{\!\textup{fin}}(\mathcal{L})$.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$r[\ell]\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}\rho.\ell$ if $\varsigma=\rho\notin\Delta$ and $\ell\in\rdef(\rho)$, and $r[\ell]\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}r(\ell)$ otherwise.
\par\itemize@item@$\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{1}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{2}}\backslash^{\!\Delta}{L}\hbox{\;\;$=$\hskip-8.36511pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 2.84526pt}\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{1}{\smallsetminus}L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\varsigma^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{2}\cup L}$;
where if $\varsigma=\rho$ and $\rdef(\rho)\cap L\neq\emptyset$:
$\varsigma^{\prime}=\textbf{..}$ if $\rho\in\Delta$ and $\varsigma^{\prime}=\rho\backslash{L}$
otherwise; and $\varsigma^{\prime}=\varsigma$ otherwise.
\end{itemize}\end{definition}
\par Let us consider a row in DNF
$r_{0}=\bigwedge_{r\in P}r\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in N}\neg r$.
Let $\rho_{0}$ be the smallest top-level variable of $r_{0}$ not in $\Delta$ and
$L=\rdef(r_{0}){\smallsetminus}\rdef(\rho_{0})$,
or $L=\emptyset$ if no such variable exists.
Let $S_{\Delta}=\{r\in S\mid\tail(r)=\rho\in\Delta\mathrel{\mathsf{and}}\rdef(\rho)\cap L\neq\emptyset\}$, for $S=P,N$.
Normalization $\operatorname{norm}_{\operatorname{row}}(r_{0},M)$ is defined as:
\begin{equation*}\bigsqcap_{\iota:N\to L\cup\{\_\}}\Bigg(\bigsqcup_{\ell\in L}\operatorname{norm}_{\operatorname{fld}}\Big(\bigwedge_{r\in P}r[\ell]\wedge\hskip-5.69054pt\bigwedge_{r\in\iota^{-1}(\ell)}\hskip-5.69054pt\neg{r[\ell]},M\Big)\sqcup\hskip-1.99997pt\bigsqcap_{N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}}\Bigg(\operatorname{norm}_{\operatorname{tl}}\Big(\bigwedge_{r\in P}(r\backslash^{\!\Delta}{L})\wedge\hskip-5.69054pt\bigwedge_{r\in\iota^{-1}(\_)\smallsetminus N^{\prime}\in S}\hskip-5.69054pt\hskip-5.69054pt\neg(r\backslash^{\!\Delta}{L}),M\Big)\Bigg)\end{equation*}
where $\mathcal{N}=\{N^{\prime}\subseteq\iota^{-1}(\_)\cap N_{\Delta}\mid\bigwedge_{r\in P_{\Delta}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\fin(r)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\tail(r)\boldsymbol{\rangle}\nleq\bigvee_{r\in N^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\fin(r)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\tail(r)\boldsymbol{\rangle}\}$.
\par Here, the idea of the algorithm is the following.
If there is no polymorphic top-level variable (these can only be row variables),
we let $L=\emptyset$, which does not decompose the row, and only calls the
function $\operatorname{norm}_{\operatorname{tl}}(r_{0},M)$ recursively.
This function decomposes the record over the whole set of
top-level labels using the formula for subtyping.
We do not loose solutions doing so: due to the absence of top-level polymorphic
variables, the emptiness of $r_{0}$ can only be satisfied by the emptiness of
one of the components.
\par If there is a smallest polymorphic top-level variable $\rho_{0}$, we take $L=\rdef(r_{0})\smallsetminus\rdef(\rho_{0})$.
In other words, we take $L$ to be the set of labels on the left of the variable
$\rho_{0}$.
In this way, we decompose the row on one side over the elements in $L$, which
does not affect $\rho_{0}$.
These elements are handled by the auxiliary function $\operatorname{norm}_{\operatorname{fld}}$, that
normalizes constraints on fields in a way similar to what is done on types.
On the other side, we obtain constraints over $\rho_{0}$, where $\rho_{0}$ is in a
row of the shape $\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{0}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}\rdef(\rho_{0})}$.
Then, the recursive call to $\operatorname{norm}_{\operatorname{tl}}$ singles out the variable $\rho_{0}$ in
order to obtain an upper or lower bound for $\rho_{0}$, as we do for type and
field variables (the definitions of $\operatorname{norm}_{\operatorname{fld}}$ and $\operatorname{norm}_{\operatorname{tl}}$ are given below).
\par While $\rho_{0}$ is not affected by the decomposition thanks to our choice of $L$,
there can be polymorphic top-level variables $\rho^{\prime}$ such that $\rdef(\rho^{\prime})\cap L\neq\emptyset$. This is why we have introduced the two new operators
$r[\ell]$ and $r\backslash^{\!\Delta}{L}$.
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Fields}
\par A field-type is always equivalent to a DNF that is a disjunction of conjunctions
of either of the shape
$\tau\wedge\bigwedge_{\theta\in P}\theta\wedge\bigwedge_{\theta\in N}\theta$, where $\tau$ is either $\bot$ or a type $t$.
If there is a smallest variable $\theta_{0}\in P\cup N$ not in $\Delta$, we
single out this variable, in the same way as is done for type variables in our
algorithm and in \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}.
If all top-level field variables are monomorphic:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@If $\tau=t$, $\tau$ can be instantiated to an empty type only if $t$
can, so we apply $\norm(t,M)$.
\par\itemize@item@If $\tau=\bot$, $\tau$ can never be instantiated to an empty type
since $\bot\nleq\mathbb{0}$, so normalization fails.
\end{itemize}
We use the notation $X\lightning T$ to indicate that $X$ is the
smallest top-level variable in $T$.
$$\operatorname{norm}_{\operatorname{fld}}(\tau,M)=\begin{cases}\{\{(\theta_{0},\leq,\neg\tau\vee\bigvee_{\theta\in P{\smallsetminus}\{\theta_{0}\}}\neg\theta\vee\bigvee_{\theta\in N}\theta)\}\},&\text{ if }\exists\theta_{0}\in P.\theta_{0}\lightning\tau\\
\{\{(\tau\wedge\bigwedge_{\theta\in P}\theta\wedge\bigwedge_{\theta\in N{\smallsetminus}\{\theta_{0}\}}\neg\theta\leq\theta_{0})\}\},&\text{ if }\exists\theta_{0}\in N.\theta_{0}\lightning\tau\\
\norm(t,M),&\hskip-50.00008pt\text{ if }\tau^{\prime}=t\mathrel{\mathsf{and}}(P\cup N)\smallsetminus\Delta=\emptyset\\
\emptyset,&\hskip-50.00008pt\text{ if }\tau^{\prime}=\bot\mathrel{\mathsf{and}}(P\cup N)\smallsetminus\Delta=\emptyset\end{cases}$$
\par\par\@@unnumbered@section{paragraph}{toc}{Tails}
\par By design, the input of this function is a row such that:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@Either there is a polymorphic top-level variable that is an a row $r_{0}$
with $\fin(r)=\emptyset$.
Then, we single out this variable on the left of a new constraint.
\par\itemize@item@Or there is no polymorphic top-level row variable.
In that case, we decompose the row over all the labels using the subtyping
formula.
\end{itemize}
$$\operatorname{norm}_{\operatorname{tl}}(r,M)=\begin{cases}\{\{(\rho_{0},\leq,\bigvee_{r\in P{\smallsetminus}\{r_{0}\}}\neg r\vee\bigvee_{r\in N}r)\}\},&\text{ if }r_{0}\in P\\
\{\{(\rho_{0},\geq,\bigwedge_{r\in P}r\wedge_{r\in N{\smallsetminus}\{\rho\}}\neg r)\}\},&\text{ if }r_{0}\in N\\
\bigsqcap_{\iota\in I}\bigsqcup_{\ell\in L}\operatorname{norm}_{\operatorname{fld}}\Big(\bigwedge_{r\in P}r(\ell)\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in\iota^{-1}(\ell)}\neg r(\ell),M\Big),&\text{if }\tlv(r)\subseteq\Delta\end{cases}$$
where in the two first cases, there is $\rho_{0}\in(P\cup N)\smallsetminus\Delta$
such that there is $r_{0}\in P\cup N$ with $r_{0}=\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho_{0}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}\rdef(r)}$,
and where in the last case:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$L=\bigcup_{r\in P\cup N}\fin(r)$;
\par\itemize@item@$P_{\Delta}=\{r\in P\mid\tail(r)\in\mathcal{V}\}$
and $N_{\Delta}=\{r\in N\mid\tail(r)\in\mathcal{V}\}$;
\par\itemize@item@$I=\{\iota:N\to L\cup\{\_\}\mid(\forall r_{\circ}\in\iota^{-1}(\_)\smallsetminus N_{\Delta}.\bigwedge_{r\in P}\Def(r)\nleq\Def(r_{\circ}))\mathrel{\mathsf{and}}(\forall r_{0}\in\iota^{-1}(\_)\cap N_{\Delta}.\forall r_{p}\in P_{\Delta}.\tail(r_{\circ})\neq\tail(r_{p}))\}$,
where $\Def(r)$ is defined as in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:subtyping}.
\end{itemize}
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Constraint Normalization}
\par\par\begin{figure}\begin{framed}
\begin{equation*}\frac{}{\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}\emptyset\rightsquigarrow\{\emptyset\}}\quad\textsc{right=({Nempty})}\frac{(\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}\{(T_{i},c_{i},T_{i}^{\prime})\}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}_{i})_{i\in I}}{\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}\{(T_{i},c_{i},T_{i}^{\prime})\mid i\in I\}\rightsquigarrow\sqcap_{i\in I}\mathcal{S}_{i}}\quad\textsc{right=({Njoin})}\frac{\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}(T,\geq,T^{\prime})\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}}{\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}(T^{\prime},\leq,T)\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}}\quad\textsc{right=({Nsym})}\frac{\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}\{(T\wedge\neg T^{\prime},\leq,\mathbb{0})\}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}T^{\prime}\neq\mathbb{0}}{\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}\{(T,\leq,T^{\prime})\}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}}\quad\textsc{right=({Nzero})}{}\frac{\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}\{(\dnf(T),\leq,\mathbb{0})\}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}\hskip-10.00002ptT\neq\dnf(T)}{\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}\{(T,\leq,\mathbb{0})\}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}}\quad\textsc{right=({Ndnf})}{}\frac{\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}\{(T_{i},\leq,\mathbb{0})\mid i\in I\}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}}{\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}\{(\vee_{i\in I}\,T_{i},\leq,\mathbb{0})\}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}}\quad\textsc{right=({Nunion})}{}\end{equation*}
{\small Where $T_{i}$ in\penalty\ ({Nunion}) are single normal forms.}
