\end{figure}
\par\begin{figure}\begin{framed}
\begin{equation*}\frac{t\in\Sigma\tlv(t)=\emptyset}{\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}\{(t,\leq,\mathbb{0})\}\rightsquigarrow\{\emptyset\}}\quad\textsc{right=({Nhyp})}\frac{\Sigma\cup\{t\}\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}^{\star}}\{(t,\leq,\mathbb{0})\}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}t\notin\Sigma}{\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}\{(t,\leq,\mathbb{0})\}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}}\quad\textsc{right=({Nassum})}\frac{\tlv(T)=\emptyset P}(\mathcal{S}_{P^{\prime}}^{1}\sqcup\mathcal{S}_{P^{\prime}}^{2})}\quad\textsc{right=({Narrow})}\frac{\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}\{(\operatorname{row}(\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P}{\texttt{R}}\wedge\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in N}\neg{\texttt{R}}),\leq,\mathbb{0})\}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}}{\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}^{\star}}\{(\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in \end{figure}
\par\begin{figure}\begin{framed}
\begin{equation*}\frac{\forall\iota:N{\to}L{\cup}\{\_\}.{\begin{cases}\forall\ell\in L.\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}\{(\bigwedge_{r\in N)\rho\notin\Delta\exists r^{\prime}\in P\cup N.r^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L}}{\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}\{(r_{0}=\bigwedge_{r\in P}r\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in N}\neg r,\leq,\mathbb{0})\}\rightsquigarrow\{\single(\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L},r_{0})\}}\quad\textsc{right=({Ntail-tlv})}\end{equation*}
\end{framed}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{7}}{Normalization rules for row single normal forms}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 7}}{\small Normalization rules for row single normal forms}}}
\end{figure}
\par We formalize normalization as a judgment $\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}C\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}$, which
states that under the environment $\Sigma$ (which, informally, contains the
types that have already been processed at this point), $C$ is normalized to
$\mathcal{S}$.
The main judgment is derived according to the rules in
\lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:nrules-all}, \lx@cref{refnum}{fig:nrules-typ} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{fig:nrules-rec}.
Given a type, field or row variable $X$ and a conjunction of types, field-types or rows respectively, we define
$\single(X,T\wedge X)=\{(X,\leq,\neg T)\}$ and
$\single(X,T\wedge\neg X)=\{(X,\geq,T)\}$.
We call single normal form a DNF that has no topmost disjunction.
\par If $\emptyset\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}C\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}$, then $\mathcal{S}$ is the result of the
normalization of $C$.
We now prove soundness and termination of the constraint normalization
algorithm.
\par\begin{definition}We define the family $(\leq_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ of subtyping relations as $$t\leq_{n}s\hbox{\;\;$\iff$\hskip-16.90094pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 11.38109pt}\forall\eta.\llbracket t\rrbracket_{n}\eta\subseteq\llbracket s\rrbracket_{n}\eta$$where $\llbracket\cdot\rrbracket_{n}$ is the rank $n$ interpretation of a type,
defined as $$\llbracket t\rrbracket_{n}\eta=\{d\in\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\eta}\mid\domheight(d)\leq n\}$$and $\domheight(d)$ is the height of an element in $\mathcal{D}$, defined as
\@@amsalign
\end{definition}
\par\begin{lemma}Let $t\leq\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@lbrace}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\texttt{\lx@text@rbrace}}$. Then,
$t\leq_{n+1}\mathbb{0}\iff\operatorname{row}(t)\leq_{n}\mathbb{0}$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof By definition and a trivial well-founded
induction on type operators, we have
$\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}=\{\mathrm{Rec}({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud})^{V}\mid{\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}\in\llbracket\operatorname{row}(t)\rrbracket_{\eta}^{\operatorname{q}}\}$.
Thus, by definition of height, we have
$\llbracket t\rrbracket_{n+1}^{\operatorname{q}}\eta=\{\mathrm{Rec}({\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud})^{V}\mid{\mkern 3.0mu\mathchar 22\mkern-7.0mu\raisebox{-0.7pt}{$\mathchar 22$}\mkern-11.0mud}\in\llbracket\operatorname{row}(t)\rrbracket_{n}^{\operatorname{q}}\eta\}$.
\qed\end@proof
\par\begin{definition}Given a constraint-set $C$ and a substitution $\sigma$, we define the rank $n$
satisfaction predicate $\Vdash_{n}$ as $$\sigma\Vdash_{n}C\hbox{\;\;$\iff$\hskip-16.90094pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\rm\scriptsize def}\hskip 11.38109pt}\forall(T_{1},\leq,T_{2})\in C.T_{1}\leq_{n}T_{2}\mathrel{\mathsf{and}}\forall(T_{1},\geq,T_{2})\in C.T_{1}\geq_{n}T_{2}$$\end{definition}
\par\begin{lemma}\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$\sigma\Vdash_{0}C$ for all $\sigma$ and $C$.
\par\enumerate@item@$\sigma\Vdash C\iff\forall n.\sigma\Vdash_{n}C$.
\end{enumerate}\end{lemma}
\@proof Consequence of \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Lemma C.7)} and
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:subtyping_rectorow_levels}.
\qed\end@proof
\par\begin{definition}Given a conjunction of atomic rows
$r_{0}=\bigwedge_{r\in P}r\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in N}r$,
a finite set of labels and a set of variables, we define marshalling as
$$\marshall(r,L,\Delta)=\bigwedge_{r\in P{\smallsetminus}P_{0}}r\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in N{\smallsetminus}N_{0}}\neg r\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in P_{0}}\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=r[\ell])_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\backslash^{\!\Delta}{L}\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in N_{0}}\neg\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=r[\ell])_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\backslash^{\!\Delta}{L}\boldsymbol{\rangle}$$where $P_{0}=\{r\in P\mid\tail(r)=\rho\notin\Delta\mathrel{\mathsf{and}}\rdef(\rho)\cap L\neq\emptyset\}$, similarly for $N_{0}$.
\end{definition}
\par\begin{lemma}If $\sigma\Vdash\{(\marshall(r,L,\Delta),\leq,\mathbb{0}\}$, then
$\sigma\Vdash\{(r,\leq,\mathbb{0})\}$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof We show that $\marshall(r_{0},L,\Delta)\sigma=r_{0}\sigma$.
Let $r=\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=r(\ell))_{\ell\in L_{1}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\boldsymbol{\rangle}\in P_{0}\cup N_{0}$
and $r^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=r[\ell]_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\backslash^{\!\Delta}{L}\boldsymbol{\rangle}$.
Then, $r^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=r(\ell)_{\ell\in L_{1}},(\ell=\rho.\ell)_{\ell\in L{\smallsetminus}L_{1}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\backslash{L}\boldsymbol{\rangle}$.
We have $\sigma(\rho)\simeq\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L{\smallsetminus}L_{1}}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}\rdef(\rho)}$
because $\sigma$ is a solution for
$\{\marshall(r_{0},L,\Delta),\leq,\mathbb{0}\}$.
Therefore, $r\sigma=\marshall(r,L,\Delta)\sigma$.
\qed\end@proof
\par Given a set $\Sigma$ of types and rows, we write $C(\Sigma)$ for the
constraint-set $\{(T,\leq,\mathbb{0})\mid T\in\Sigma\}$.
\par\begin{lemma}Let $C$ be a constraint-set.
If $\emptyset\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}C\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}$, then for all constraint-set
$C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}$ and all substitution $\sigma$, we have
$\sigma\Vdash C^{\prime}\implies\sigma\Vdash C$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof We prove the following stronger statements.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@Assume $\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}C\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}$.
For all $C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}$, $\sigma$ and $n$,
if $\sigma\Vdash_{n}C(\Sigma)$ and $\sigma\Vdash_{n}C^{\prime}$,
then $\sigma\Vdash_{n}C$.
\par\enumerate@item@Assume $\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}^{\star}}C\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}$.
For all $C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}$, $\sigma$ and $n$,
if $\sigma\Vdash_{n}C(\Sigma)$ and $\sigma\Vdash_{n}C^{\prime}$,
then $\sigma\Vdash_{n+1}C$.
\end{enumerate}
The cases ({Nempty}), ({Njoin}), ({Nsym}),
({Nzero}), ({Ndnf}), ({Nunion}), ({Nhyp}),
({Nassum}), ({Nbasic}) and ({Narrow}) are given
in\penalty\ \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Lemma C.10)}.
\begin{description}\description@item@[\rulename{Ntlv}]Let $T_{0}=T\wedge\bigwedge_{X\in P}X\wedge\bigwedge_{X\in N}\neg X$
and $X^{\prime}$ be the smallest type variable with respect to the
order in $P\cup N$.
If $X^{\prime}\in P\smallsetminus\Delta$, then we have
$\sigma\Vdash_{n}\{(X^{\prime},\leq,\neg T_{X^{\prime}})\}$
with $T_{X^{\prime}}=T\wedge\bigwedge_{X\in P{\smallsetminus}X}X\wedge\bigwedge_{X\in N}\neg X$,
thus $(X^{\prime})\sigma\leq_{n}\neg T_{X^{\prime}}\sigma$.
This is equivalent to $T\sigma\leq_{n}\mathbb{0}$,
and we conclude $\sigma\Vdash_{n}\{(T,\leq,\mathbb{0})\}$.
If $X^{\prime}\in N\smallsetminus\Delta$, the result follows as well.
If $X^{\prime}\in\Delta$, then $P\cup N\subseteq\Delta$ by
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:ordering}.
We have $\llbracket T_{0}\rrbracket=\{D\in\llbracket T\rrbracket\mid P\subseteq\Tag(D)\mathrel{\mathsf{and}}N\cap\Tag(D)=\emptyset\}$.
Since $T_{0}$ is non empty, the variables in $P$ and $N$ are different, and
since those variables cannot be instantiated,
we can satisfy $T_{0}\leq\mathbb{0}$ if and only if $T\leq\mathbb{0}$
is satisfied.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Nopt}]Direct by emptiness of $\mathcal{S}$.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Nrec}]Let $t=\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in P}{\texttt{R}}\wedge\bigwedge_{{\texttt{R}}\in N}\neg{\texttt{R}}$.
By induction, we have $\sigma\Vdash_{n}\{(\operatorname{row}(t)\leq\mathbb{0})\}$.
This is by definition equivalent to $\operatorname{row}(t)\sigma\leq_{n}\mathbb{0}$,
thus $\operatorname{row}(t\sigma)\leq_{n}\mathbb{0}$
and by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:subtyping_rectorow_levels} $t\sigma\leq_{n+1}\mathbb{0}$,
which concludes $\sigma\Vdash_{n+1}\{(t\leq\mathbb{0})\}$.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Nrow}]The result is direct if
$\mathcal{S}=\emptyset$.
Otherwise, we have $C^{\prime}=\bigcup_{\iota\in N\to L\cup\{\_\}}C_{\iota}^{\prime}$,
where $C_{\iota}^{\prime}\in\bigsqcup_{\ell\in L}\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{\iota}\sqcup\bigsqcap_{N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}}\mathcal{S}_{N^{\prime}}^{\iota}$.
For all $\iota:N\to L\cup\{\_\}$,
there are two cases.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@In the first case, there is $\ell\in L$ such that $C_{\iota}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{\iota}^{\ell}$.
Then, by induction, $$\sigma\Vdash_{n}\{(\bigwedge_{r\in P}r[\ell]\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in\iota^{-1}(\ell)}\neg r[\ell]\leq\mathbb{0})\}$$\par\enumerate@item@In the second case, we have
$C_{\iota}^{\prime}=\bigcup_{N^{\prime}}C_{\iota}^{N^{\prime}}$,
where $C_{\iota}^{N^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{S}^{\iota}_{N^{\prime}}$.
For all $N^{\prime}$, there are two subcases.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@In the first subcase, $N^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}$.
Then, by induction, $$\sigma\Vdash_{n}\{(\bigwedge_{r\in P}r\backslash^{\!\Delta}{L}\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in\iota^{-1}(\_){\smallsetminus}N^{\prime}}\neg(r\backslash^{\!\Delta}{L})\leq\mathbb{0})\}$$\par\enumerate@item@In the second subcase, $N^{\prime}\in\iota^{-1}(\_)\cap N_{\Delta}\smallsetminus\mathcal{N}$.
Then we have $$\sigma\Vdash_{n}{\{(\bigwedge_{r\in P_{\Delta}}\boldsymbol{\langle}\fin(r)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\tail(r)\boldsymbol{\rangle}\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in N^{\prime}}\neg\boldsymbol{\langle}\fin(r)\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\tail(r)\boldsymbol{\rangle},\leq,\mathbb{0})\}}$$\end{enumerate}\end{enumerate}In other words,
$\forall\iota:N\to L\cup\{\_\}.\forall N^{\prime}\subseteq\iota^{-1}(\_)\cap N_{\Delta}:$
\@@amsalign
Let $P_{0}=\{r\in P\mid\tail(r)=\rho\notin\Delta\mathrel{\mathsf{and}}\rdef(\rho)\cap L\neq\emptyset\}$,
same for $N_{0}$.
By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:decomposition}, the definition of substitution
and the fact that $r[\ell]=r(\ell)$ and $r\backslash^{\!\Delta}{L}=r\backslash{L}$ for all $r\notin P_{0}\cup N_{0}$,
we have $\marshall(r_{0},L,\Delta)\sigma\leq_{n}\mathbb{0}$,
that is $\sigma\Vdash\{\marshall(r_{0},L,\Delta),\leq,\mathbb{0})\}$,
By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:marshall}, we conclude $\sigma\Vdash_{n}\{(r_{0}\leq\mathbb{0})\}$.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Ntail-mono}]The result is direct if $\bigsqcap_{\iota\in I}\bigsqcup_{\ell\in L}\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{\iota}=\emptyset$.
Otherwise, we have $C^{\prime}=\bigcup_{\iota\in I}C_{\iota}^{\prime}$,
where $\mathcal{S}_{\iota}^{\prime}\in\bigsqcup_{\ell\in L}\mathcal{S}_{\iota}^{\ell}$.
By definition of $\sqcup$, for all $\iota\in I$, there is $\ell\in L$
such that $C_{\iota}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{\iota}^{\ell}$.
By induction, $\sigma\Vdash_{n}\{(\bigwedge_{r\in P}r(\ell)\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in\iota^{-1}(\ell)}\neg r(\ell))\}$.
In other words, $$\forall\iota\in I.\exists\ell\in L.\bigwedge_{r\in P}r(\ell)\sigma\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in\iota^{-1}(\ell)}\neg r(\ell)\sigma\leq_{n}\mathbb{0}$$Moreover, by hypothesis that $L\subseteq\bigcup_{r\in P\cup N}\fin(r)$.
Also, for each $\iota\notin I$,
one of the two conditions\penalty\ \eqref{eq:subtyping_nvars}
or\penalty\ \eqref{eq:subtyping_vars} of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:subtyping} is satisfied.
By this corollary, $\bigwedge_{r\in P}r\sigma\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in N}\neg r\sigma\leq_{n}\mathbb{0}$.
We conclude $\sigma\Vdash_{n}\{(\bigwedge_{r\in P}r\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in N}\neg r\leq\mathbb{0})\}$.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Ntail-tlv}]Let $r_{0}=\bigwedge_{r\in P}r\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in N}r$.
By hypothesis, there is $r^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\backslash{L_{1}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{2}}\in P\cup N$
such that $\rho\lightning_{O}r^{\prime}$.
There are two similar cases, we detail the one where $r^{\prime}\in P$.
Let $r_{0}^{\prime}=\bigwedge_{r\in P{\smallsetminus}\{r^{\prime}\}}\neg r\wedge\bigwedge_{r\in N}r$.
By hypothesis, we have $\sigma\Vdash_{n}\{(r^{\prime}\sigma\leq\neg r_{0}^{\prime})\}$,
thus $r^{\prime}\sigma\leq_{n}r_{0}^{\prime}\sigma$.
This is equivalent to $r_{0}\sigma\leq_{n}\mathbb{0}$,
and we conclude $\sigma\Vdash_{n}\{(r_{0}\leq\mathbb{0})\}$.
\qed\end{description}
\end@proof
\par We introduce a notion of plinth generalizing the one of \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{frisch04}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} to
types, field-types and rows.
This notion is used to prove termination of the algorithm.
\begin{definition}A plinth $\beth\subset\mathcal{T}_{\bot}\cup\mathcal{R}$ is a set of types, field-types and rows with the following properties:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$\beth$ is finite;
\par\itemize@item@$\beth$ contains $\mathbb{0}$, $\mathbb{1}$, $\bot$, $\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\emptyset}$ and is closed under
Boolean connectives ($\vee$, $\wedge$, $\neg$);
\par\itemize@item@for all type $t_{1}\to t_{2}\in\beth$, we have $t_{1}\in\beth$ and
$t_{2}\in\beth$;
\par\itemize@item@for all type ${\texttt{R}}\in\beth$, we have $\operatorname{row}({\texttt{R}})\in\beth$;
\par\itemize@item@for all row $r\in\beth$ of definition space $\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}L_{r}$,
let $L$ be the set of labels appearing explicitely in $\beth$
and $V$ the set of row variables in $\beth$, we have:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@for all $\ell\in L$, $r(\ell)\in\beth$
\par\itemize@item@for all $L^{\prime}\subseteq L$, $\forall V^{\prime}\subseteq V$,
$r\backslash^{\!V^{\prime}}{L^{\prime}}\in\beth$
and $\marshall(r,L^{\prime},V^{\prime})\in\beth$.
\end{itemize}\end{itemize}\end{definition}
Every finite set of types, field-types and types is included in a plinth.
Indeed, for a regular type $t$, the set of its subtrees $S$ is finite, while
rows and field-types are inductively defined.
The definition of the plinth ensures that the closure of $S$ under Boolean
connectives is also finite.
Moreover, if a type, field-type or row belongs in a plinth, the set of its
subtrees also does.
Finally, if a record or a row belongs to a plinth, the rows obtained by
marshalling or by removing fields also belongs to the plinth, but only if these
operations are done with respect to the set of labels present in the plinth, in
order to guarantee finiteness of the plinth.
\par\begin{lemma}Let $C$ be a finite constraint set.
The normalization of $C$ terminates.
\end{lemma}
\@proof Let $B$ be the set of types occuring in $C$.
As $C$ is finite, $B$ is finite as well.
Let $\beth$ be a plinth such that $B\subseteq\beth$.
Then, when we normalize a constraint $(t,\leq,\mathbb{0})$ during the process of
$\emptyset\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}C$, $t$ would belong to $\beth$.
We prove the lemma by induction on $(|\beth\smallsetminus\Sigma|,U,|C|)$,
$U$ is the number of unions $\vee$ occurring in the constraint-set $C$ (over
any kind) plus the number of constraints $(T_{1},\leq,T_{2})$ where
$T_{2}\neq\mathbb{0}$ or $T_{1}$ is not in DNF, and $C$ is the
constraint-set to be normalized.
We detail the original cases, others are described in the proof \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Lemma
C.14)}.
\begin{description}\description@item@[\rulename{Nopt}]Terminates immediately.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Nrec}]None of the indices decreases, but the next rule to apply must be one of
({Nrow}), ({Ntail-mono}) or ({Ntail-tlv}).
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Nrow}]Although $(|\beth\smallsetminus\Sigma|,U,|C|)$ may not change, the next rule to apply must be
one of ({Ndnf}), ({Nhyp}), ({Nassum}),
({Ntlv}), ({Nopt}) for $\mathcal{S}_{\ell}^{\iota}$
and ({Ntail-tlv}) for $\mathcal{S}_{N^{\prime}}^{\iota}$.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Ntail-mono}]Although $(|\beth\smallsetminus\Sigma|,U,|C|)$ may
not change, the next rule to apply must be one of
({Ndf}), ({Nhy}), ({Nassum}), ({Ntlv}),
({Nopt}).
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Ntail-tlv}]Terminates immediately.
\qed\end{description}
\end@proof
\par\begin{definition}A constraint is said to be \emph{normalized} if it is of the shape
$(X,c,T)$, where $X$ and $T$ are of the same kind.
A constraint-set is \emph{normalized} if all constraints are.
\end{definition}
\par\begin{lemma}Let $C$ be a constraint-set and $\emptyset\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}C\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}$.
Then, all constraint-sets $C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}$ are normalized.
\end{lemma}
\@proof Straightforward by induction on the algorithm derivation.
\qed\end@proof
\par\begin{lemma}Let $C$ be a constraint-set and $\emptyset\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}C\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}$.
Then, $\mathcal{S}$ is finite.
\end{lemma}
\@proof It is easy to prove that each normalizing rule generates a finite set of
finite sets of normalized constraints.
\qed\end@proof
\par\begin{definition}Let $C$ be a normalized constraint-set and $O$ an ordering on $\vars(C)$
and on the labels occurring in $C$.
We say $C$ is well-ordered if for all normalized constraint
$(X_{1},c,T)\in C$ and for all $X_{2}\in\tlv(T)$,
$O(X_{1})<O(X_{2})$ holds.
\end{definition}
\par\begin{lemma}Let $C$ be a constraint-set and $\emptyset\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}C\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}$.
Then for all normalized constraint-set $C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}$,
$C^{\prime}$ is well-ordered.
\end{lemma}
\@proof There are two different ways to generate normalized constraints:
\begin{description}\description@item@[\rulename{Ntlv}]We single out the type variable $X^{\prime}$ whose
order is minimum, because $\tlv(T)=\emptyset$.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Ntail-tlv}]We single out the row variable $\rho$ whose
order is minimum.
\qed\end{description}
\end@proof
\par\par\par\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Constraint Merging and Saturation}
\par\begin{figure}\begin{framed}
\begin{equation*}\frac{\forall i\in I.(X,\geq,T_{i})\in C|I|\geq 2}{\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M}}C\rightsquigarrow(C\smallsetminus\{(X,\geq,T_{i})\mid i\in I\}\cup\{(X,\geq,\bigvee_{i\in I}T_{i})\})}\quad\textsc{right=({MLB})}\frac{\forall i\in I.(X,\leq,T_{i})\in C|I|\geq 2}{\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M}}C\rightsquigarrow(C\smallsetminus\{(X,\leq,T_{i})\mid i\in I\}\cup\{(X,\leq,\bigvee_{i\in I}T_{i})\})}\quad\textsc{right=({MUB})}\end{equation*}
\end{framed}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{8}}{Merging rules}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 8}}{\small Merging rules}}}
\end{figure}
\par\begin{figure}\begin{framed}
\begin{equation*}\frac{\Sigma_{p},C_{\Sigma}\cup\{(X,\geq,T_{1}),(X,\leq,T_{2})\}\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}C\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}(T_{1},T_{2})\in\Sigma_{p}}{\Sigma_{p},C_{\Sigma}\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\{(X,\geq,T_{1}),(X,\leq,T_{2})\}\cup C\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}}\quad\textsc{right=({Shyp})}\frac{(T_{1},T_{2})\notin\Sigma_{p}\emptyset\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}\{(T_{1},\leq,T_{2})\}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}\quad\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\{\{(X,\geq,T_{1}),(X,\leq,T_{2})\}\cup C\cup C_{\Sigma}\}\sqcap\mathcal{S}\quad\forall C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}.\Sigma_{p}\cup\{(T_{1},T_{2})\},\emptyset\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{MS}}C^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}_{C^{\prime}}}{\Sigma_{p},C_{\Sigma}\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}\{(X,\geq,T_{1}),(X,\leq,X_{2})\}\cup C\rightsquigarrow\bigsqcup_{C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\mathcal{S}_{C^{\prime}}}\quad\textsc{right=({Sassum})}\frac{\forall X,T_{1},T_{2}.\nexists\{(X,\geq,T_{1}),(X,\leq,T_{2})\}\subseteq C}{\Sigma_{p},C_{\Sigma}\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}C\rightsquigarrow\{C\cup C_{\Sigma}\}}\quad\textsc{right=({Sdone})}\end{equation*}
{\small Where $\Sigma_{p},C_{\Sigma}\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{MS}}C\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}$ means that there
exists $C^{\prime}$ such that $\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{M}}C\rightsquigarrow C^{\prime}$ and
$\Sigma_{p},C_{\Sigma}\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}}C^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}$.}
\end{framed}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{9}}{Saturation rules}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 9}}{\small Saturation rules}}}
\end{figure}
\par After normalization, we have a set of constraint-sets where the same variable
might have several upper and lower bounds given by different constraints on that
variable.
After this step, we wish to obtain unique upper and lower bounds for the same
variable.
This is done in two phases. For each normalized constraint-set $C$ and following
the order on variables, we:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@Merge two constraints $(X,\leq,T_{1})$ and
$(X,\leq,T_{2})$ into $(X,\leq,T_{1}\wedge T_{2})$.
\par\itemize@item@Merge two constraints $(X,\geq,T_{1})$ and
$(X,\geq,T_{2})$ into $(X,\geq,T_{1}\vee T_{2})$.
\end{itemize}
Once the set $C$ has been transformed into $C^{\prime}$ where there are no such
constraints left, we saturate the set to verify that the lower bound is indeed a
subtype of the upper bound, once again following the order on variables.
From two constraints $(T_{1},\leq,X)$ and $(X,\leq,T_{2})$,
we normalize the constraint $(T_{1}\wedge\neg T_{2},\leq,\mathbb{0})$.
We obtain a set of constraint-sets $\mathcal{S}$.
We add the new resulting constraint-sets accordingly to the existing ones with
$\mathcal{S}\sqcap\{C^{\prime}\}$, then apply the step of merging and saturation again
on each constraint-sets in $\mathcal{S}\sqcap\{C^{\prime}\}$.
Termination of this step is assured as in the step of constraint
normalization by an additional argument $M$ to the merge function, saving
visited types.
\par Formally, this part is almost exactly the same as in the original algorithm in
\cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Section C.1.2)}.
One simply needs to replace occurrences of $\alpha$ and $t$ by the more general
meta-variables $X$ and $T$.
The rules are given in \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:mrules} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{fig:srules}.
Termination is proved thanks to the generalized definition of plinths
(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:plinth}).
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Harmonization}
\par Thanks to the last step, we now have a set of constraint-sets such that in all
constraint-sets, each variable in the domain of the constraint-set has at most
one upper bound and one lower bound.
Yet, for a row variable $\rho$ of the original type constraints, we can have
several constraints for the derived constructors $\rho\backslash{L}$, for instance
a constraint-set containing both
$(\rho\backslash{L_{1}},\leq,T_{1})$ and
$(\rho\backslash{L_{2}},\leq,T_{2})$,
with $L_{1}\cap\rdef(\rho)\neq L_{2}\cap\rdef(\rho)$.
There could also be occurrences of another construction $\rho\backslash{L_{3}}$ in
$T_{1}$ with a different $L_{3}$.
We want a unique decomposition of $\rho$ in each constraint-set, with
a unique set $L_{0}$ such that only $\rho\backslash{L}_{0}$
may appear in the domain of the constraint-set.
From this, we will build a solution such that
$\sigma(\rho)\simeq{(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{0}}}{r}$.
There can be occurrences of $\rho\backslash{L^{\prime}}$ not in the domain, or of
$\rho.\ell^{\prime}$, but substitution will be correctly defined since we take $L_{0}$ to
cover all such occurrences.
\par Formally, a harmonized constraint is defined as follow.
\begin{definition}Let $C\subseteq\mathcal{C}$ be a saturated constraint-set.
We say $C$ is \emph{harmonized} if for each type variable $\rho\in\dom(C)$,
there is a finite set of labels $L$ such that:
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$\forall(\rho\backslash{L_{i}},c,r_{i})\in C.\ L_{i}{\smallsetminus}\rdef(\rho)=L\smallsetminus\rdef(\rho)$;
\par\enumerate@item@$\forall\rho\backslash{L^{\prime}}\in\vars(C).\ L^{\prime}\subseteq L$;
\par\enumerate@item@$\forall\rho.\ell\in\vars(C).\ \ell\in L\cap\rdef(\rho)$.
\end{enumerate}\end{definition}
If a constraint-set is not harmonized, then it is of the shape
$C\cup\{\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\backslash{L}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L},c,r\}$, where $L_{0}\nsubseteq L$,
when we define
$L_{0}=\bigcup_{\rho.\ell\in\vars(C)\cup\vars(r)}\{\ell\}\cup\bigcup_{\rho\backslash{L^{\prime}}\in\vars(C)\cup\vars(r)}L^{\prime}$.
To harmonize the decomposition of the variable row,
we normalize the constraint-set
$\{(\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\rho.\ell)_{\ell\in L_{0}{\smallsetminus}L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\backslash{L_{0}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L},c,r)\}$.
We integrate the obtained set of constraint-sets $\mathcal{S}$ to the existing
constraints with $\{C\}\sqcap\mathcal{S}$ and apply merging and
harmonization recursively.
The rules are given in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:hrules}.
\par\begin{figure}\begin{framed}
\begin{equation*}\frac{\text{$C$ is harmonized}}{\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{H}}C\rightsquigarrow\{C\}}\quad\textsc{right=({Hdone})}\frac{L_{0}=\bigcup_{\rho.\ell\in\vars(C)\cup\vars(r)}\{\ell\}\cup\bigcup_{\rho\backslash{L^{\prime}}\in\vars(C)\cup\vars(r)}L^{\prime}L_{0}\nsubseteq L\quad\emptyset\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{N}}\{(\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\rho.\ell)_{\ell\in L_{0}{\smallsetminus}L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\backslash{L_{0}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L},c,r)\}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}\quad\forall C_{n}\in\{C\}\sqcap\mathcal{S}.(\emptyset,\emptyset\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{MS}}C_{n}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}_{n})\quad\forall C_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}\mathrel{\mathsf{and}}C_{m}\notin\Sigma.(\Sigma\cup C_{m}\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{H}}C_{m}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}_{n}^{m})}{\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{H}}C\cup\{(\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\backslash{L}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L},c,r)\}\rightsquigarrow\bigsqcup_{C_{n}\in\{C\}\sqcap\mathcal{S}}\bigsqcup_{C_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}}\mathcal{S}_{n}^{m}}\quad\textsc{right=({Harm})}\end{equation*}
\end{framed}\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{10}}{Harmonization rules}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 10}}{\small Harmonization rules}}}
\end{figure}
\par\begin{lemma}Let $C$ be a finite saturated constraint-set.
If $\emptyset\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{H}}C\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}$, then for all constraint-set $C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}$ and all substitution $\sigma$, we have $\sigma\Vdash C^{\prime}\implies\sigma\Vdash C$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof The proof is by induction on the derivation tree.
We prove the more general statement for all $\Sigma$.
The base case $\Sigma\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{H}}C\rightsquigarrow\{C\}$ is trivial.
In the inductive case, let
$C=C_{0}\cup\{(\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\backslash{L}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L},c,r)\}$.
By definition of $\sqcup$, there are $C_{n}\in\{C\}\sqcap\mathcal{S}$ and
$C_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}$ such that $C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}^{m}$.
Since $\sigma\Vdash C^{\prime}$, we have by induction hypothesis on
$\Sigma\cup C_{m}\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{H}}C_{m}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}_{n}^{m}$ that $\sigma\Vdash C_{m}$.
By definition of $\sqcup$ again, there is $C_{n}\in\{C_{0}\}\sqcap\mathcal{S}$
such that $C_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{n}$.
Since $\sigma\Vdash C_{m}$, we have by soundness of constraint merging on
$\emptyset,\emptyset\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{MS}}C_{n}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}_{n}$ that $\sigma\Vdash C_{n}$.
We have $C_{n}=C_{0}\cup C_{n}^{\prime}$ with $C_{n}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}$.
Since $\sigma\Vdash C_{n}$, we have $\sigma\Vdash C_{n}^{\prime}$ and by
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:soundness-normalization} $\sigma\Vdash\{(\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\rho.\ell)_{\ell\in L_{0}{\smallsetminus}L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\backslash{L_{0}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L},c,r)\}$.
Thus, $\sigma(\rho)\simeq\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L\cap\rdef(\rho)},(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{0}{\smallsetminus}L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}\rdef(\rho)}$.
Then, $(\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\rho.\ell)_{\ell\in L_{0}{\smallsetminus}L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\backslash{L_{0}}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L})\sigma=(\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\backslash{L}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L})\sigma=\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L_{0}{\smallsetminus}L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L}$.
Therefore, $\sigma\Vdash\{(\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\rho\backslash{L}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L},c,r)\}$.
We conclude $\sigma\Vdash C_{0}$ as well because $\sigma\Vdash C_{n}$
and $C_{0}\subseteq C_{n}$.
\qed\end@proof
\par\begin{lemma}Let $C$ be a finite saturated constraint-set.
The harmonization of $C$ terminates.
\end{lemma}
\@proof Let $B$ be the set of types, type fields and rows in $C$, finite since $C$ is
finite.
Let $\beth$ be a plinth such $B\subseteq\beth$.
When adding a set of constraint $C_{m}$ to $\Sigma$ during harmonization, every
constraint of $C_{m}$ belongs to $(\beth\times\{\leq,\geq\}\times\beth)$.
The proof is by induction on $(|(\beth\times\{\leq,\geq\},\times\beth)|-|\Sigma|,|C|)$ lexicographically ordered.
\begin{description}\description@item@[\rulename{Hdone}]Terminates immediatly.
\par\description@item@[\rulename{Harm}]Normalization, merging and saturation all terminate.
In the recursive step of harmonization that are applied, $|(\beth\times\{\leq,\geq\}\times\beth)|-|\Sigma|$ decreases.
\qed\end{description}
\end@proof
\par\begin{lemma}Let $C$ be a finite saturated constraint-set and $\emptyset\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{H}}C\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}$.
Then $\mathcal{S}$ is finite.
\end{lemma}
\@proof By induction on the derivation and by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:finiteness-normalization} and
finiteness of constraint merging.
\qed\end@proof
\par\begin{lemma}Let $C$ be a finite saturated constraint-set and $\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{H}}C\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}$.
Then for all constraint-set $C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}$, $C^{\prime}$ is harmonized.
\end{lemma}
\@proof Direct by induction on the derivation, finite by
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:termination-harmonization}.
\qed\end@proof
\par\begin{lemma}Let $C$ be a well-ordered saturated constraint-set and $\emptyset\vdash_{\!\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{H}}C\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{S}$.
Then for all harmonized constraint-set $C^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}$, $C^{\prime}$ is
well-ordered.
\end{lemma}
\@proof Consequence of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:wellordered-normalization} and conservation of
well-orderedness by merging and saturation.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{From Constraints to Equations}
\par Once normalization, merging and harmonization are done,
we have a set of well-ordered constraint-sets at hand, where all variables have unique
lower and upper bounds, and for each row variable $\rho$, there is at most a
unique occurrence of $\rho\backslash{L}$ (where $L$ can be empty if $\rho$ has not
been decomposed), such that also every occurrence of $\rho.\ell$ has $\ell\in L$.
We are now able to rewrite each constraint-set $C$ into an equivalent equation
system.
\par\begin{definition}An equation system $E$ is a set of equations of the form $X=T$
such that there exists at most one equation in $E$ for every variable
$X$, and $X$ and $T$ are of the same kind.
We define the domain of $E$, written $\dom(E)$, as the set
$\{X\mid\exists T.X=T\in E\}$.
\end{definition}
\par\begin{definition}Let $E$ be an equation system.
A solution to $E$ is a substitution $\sigma$ such that
$\forall(X=T)\in E.\sigma(X)\simeq T\sigma$ holds.
If $\sigma$ is a solution to $E$, we write $\sigma\Vdash E$.
\end{definition}
\par Given a constraint-set $C$, we will use the notation $(T_{1}\leq X\leq T_{2})\in C$ to indicate $\{(T_{1},\leq,X),(X,\leq,T_{2})\}\subseteq C$.
We assume that every variable and every term $\rho.\ell$, $\rho\backslash{L}$ in
$\dom(C)$ have an upper and a lower bound,
without loss of generality because a constraint with
bottom or top types can always be added if needed:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@For a type variable $\alpha$, we can add constraints
$(\mathbb{0},\leq,\alpha)$ or $(\alpha,\leq,\mathbb{1})$;
\par\itemize@item@For a field variable $\theta$ (or $\rho.\ell$), we can add constraints
$(\mathbb{0},\leq,\theta)$ or $(\theta,\leq,\mathbb{1}\lor\bot)$;
\par\itemize@item@For a row variable $\rho\backslash{L}$ (or $\rho$), we can add constraints
$(\mathbb{0},\leq,\rho\backslash{L})$ or
$(\rho\backslash{L},\leq,\boldsymbol{\langle}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}\textbf{..}\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}(\rdef(\rho)\cup L)})$.
\end{itemize}
\par We rewrite the set $C$ to a set of equations with a function $\solve(C)$,
where $\alpha^{\prime}$, $\theta^{\prime}$, $\theta_{\ell}$ and $\rho^{\prime}$ are fresh variables.
We write $(T_{1}\leq X\leq T_{2})$ for the constraints
$\{(T_{1},\leq,X),(X,\leq,T_{2})\}\subseteq C$.
\@@amsalign
where $\alpha^{\prime}$, $\theta^{\prime}$, and $\rho^{\prime}$ are fresh variables.
\par For type and field variables (not generated by the decomposition of a row
variable), we obtain an equation by means of the type connectives, where the
union entails a lower bound and the intersection an upper bound.
For a row variable $\rho$, there is $L$ and constraints
$(\tau_{\ell}^{1}\leq\rho.\ell\leq\tau_{\ell}^{2})\in C$ for all $\ell\in L$ and
$(r_{1}\leq\rho\backslash{L}\leq r_{2})$, with the potentially missing
constraints obtained with the default values.
Since we have decomposed $\rho$ into the labels in $L$ and a part of definition
space $\mathcal{L}\smallsetminus(\rdef(\rho)\cup L)$, we build an equation for $\rho$ by
concatenating the independent types for $\rho.\ell$ and $\rho\backslash{\ell}$
together.
\par To prove soundness of the transformation, we define the rank $n$ satisfaction
predicate $\Vdash_{n}$ for equation systems, which is similar to the one for
constraint-sets.
\begin{lemma}Let $C\subseteq\mathcal{C}$ be a well-ordered saturated constraint-set and
$E$ its transformed equation system.
Then for all substitutions $\sigma$, if $\sigma\Vdash E$,
then $\sigma\Vdash C$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof We write $O(C_{1})<O(C_{2})$ if $O(X_{1})<O(X_{2})$ for all $X_{1}\in\dom(C_{1})$ and all $X_{2}\in\dom(C_{2})$.
We prove a stronger statement:
\begin{description}\description@item@[(*)]For all $\sigma$, $n$ and $C_{\Sigma}\subseteq C$,
if $\sigma\Vdash_{n}E$, $\sigma\Vdash_{n}C_{\Sigma}$, $\sigma\Vdash_{n-1}C\smallsetminus C_{\Sigma}$
and $O(C\smallsetminus C_{\Sigma})<O(C_{\Sigma})$,
then $\sigma\Vdash_{n}C\smallsetminus C_{\Sigma}$.
\end{description}
Here $C_{\Sigma}$ denotes the set of constraints that have been checked.
The proof proceeds by induction on $|C\smallsetminus C_{\Sigma}|$, and is similar to
the proof of \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Lemma C.33)} for type variables only.
The base case $C\smallsetminus C_{\Sigma}=\emptyset$ is straightforward.
Let $C\smallsetminus C_{\Sigma}\neq\emptyset$ and let us consider the case of row
variables.
Take $\rho$ with the maximal order in $\dom(C\smallsetminus C_{\Sigma})$.
There are a set $L$ and corresponding equations
$\rho\backslash{L}=r=(r_{1}\wedge\rho^{\prime})\wedge r_{2}$ and
$(\rho.\ell=\tau_{\ell}=(\tau_{\ell}^{1}\vee\theta_{\ell})\wedge\tau_{\ell}^{2})_{\ell\in L}$.
As $\sigma\Vdash_{n}E$, we have
$\sigma(\rho)\simeq_{n}(\boldsymbol{\langle}\ell=\tau_{\ell}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{L_{\rho}}\sigma)$,
where $L_{\rho}=\mathcal{L}\smallsetminus\rdef(\rho)$.
Then, for all $\ell\in L$: $$(\rho.\ell)\sigma\wedge\neg\tau_{\ell}^{2}\sigma\simeq_{n}((\tau_{\ell}^{1}\vee\theta_{\ell})\wedge\tau_{\ell}^{2})\sigma\wedge\neg\tau_{\ell}^{2}\sigma\simeq_{n}\mathbb{0}$$ And similarly for $(\rho\backslash{L})\sigma\wedge\neg(\rho\backslash{L})\sigma$.
On the other hand, for all $\ell\in L$, we have: $$\tau_{\ell}^{1}\sigma\wedge\neg(\rho.\ell)\sigma\simeq_{n}\tau_{\ell}^{1}\wedge\neg((\tau_{\ell}^{1}\vee\theta_{\ell})\wedge\tau_{\ell}^{2})\sigma\simeq_{n}\tau_{\ell}^{1}\sigma\wedge\neg\tau_{\ell}^{2}\sigma$$ And similarly for $r_{1}\sigma\wedge\neg(\rho\backslash{L})\sigma$.
It remains to show that $\tau_{\ell}^{1}\sigma\leq_{n}\tau_{\ell}^{2}\sigma$ holds for
all $\ell\in L$ and that $r_{1}\sigma\leq_{n}r_{2}\sigma$ hold, that is
$\sigma\Vdash_{n}\{(\tau_{\ell}^{1}\leq\tau_{\ell}^{2})_{\ell\in L},(r_{1}\leq r_{2}\}$.
The rest of the proof goes as in \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Lemma C.33)}.
We use the fact that the order of $\rho.\ell$ and $\rho\backslash{L}$ is directly
superior to the order of $\rho$, and thus maximal in $\dom(C\smallsetminus C_{\Sigma})$.
\qed\end@proof
\par\begin{lemma}Let $C\subseteq\mathcal{C}$ be a saturated normalized constraint-set and
$E$ its transformed equation system.
Then for all substitution $\sigma$, if $\sigma\Vdash C$,
there exists $\sigma^{\prime}$ such that $\dom(\sigma^{\prime})\cup\dom(\sigma)=\emptyset$
and $\sigma\cup\sigma^{\prime}\Vdash E$.
\end{lemma}
\@proof Let $\sigma^{\prime}=\{\sigma(\alpha)/\alpha^{\prime}\mid\alpha\in\dom(C)\}\cup\{\sigma(\theta)/\theta^{\prime}\mid\theta\in\dom(C)\mathrel{\mathsf{and}}\theta\neq\rho.\ell\}\cup\{(\rho\backslash{L})\sigma/\rho^{\prime}\}\cup\{(\rho.\ell)\sigma/\theta_{\ell}^{\rho}\mid\rho.\ell\in\dom(C)\}$,
where $L$ is obtained as in the definition of $\solve$ either from $\rho\backslash{L^{\prime}}\in\dom(C)$ and $L=L^{\prime}\cap\rdef(\rho)$, or by $L=\{\ell\mid\rho.\ell\in\vars(C)\}$.
The case for type and field variables is as in \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Lemma C.34)}.
Let us consider an equation
$\rho=\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\rangle}^{\mathcal{L}{\smallsetminus}\rdef(\rho)}\in E$.
Correspondingly, there exist $(r_{1}\leq\rho\backslash{L}\leq r_{2})\in C$
and for all $\ell\in L$, there exist $(\tau_{\ell}^{1}\leq\rho.\ell\leq\tau_{\ell}^{2})\in C$ (without loss of generality, we suppose $C$ to be
saturated with default values).
As $\sigma\Vdash C$, then $r_{1}\sigma\leq(\rho\backslash{L})\sigma\leq r_{2}\sigma$, and for all $\ell\in L$, $\tau_{\ell}^{1}\sigma\leq(\rho.\ell)\sigma\leq r_{2}\sigma$, and the operations $(\rho\backslash{L})\sigma$ and $(\rho.\ell)\sigma$ are defined.
Thus,
\@@amsalign
The last line is justified by $\sigma$ being a solution to $C$, so being of
the shape $\sigma(\rho)=\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell}^{\prime})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\rangle}$.
\qed\end@proof
\par\begin{definition}Let $E$ be an equation system and $O$ an ordering on $\dom(E)$ and on the
labels occurring in $E$.
We say that $E$ is well-ordered if for all $X=T_{X}\in E$
and $X^{\prime}\in\tlv(T_{X})\cap\dom(E)$,
we have $O(X_{1})<O(X^{\prime})$.
\end{definition}
\par\begin{lemma}Let $C$ be a well-ordered saturated normalized constraint-set and $E$ its
transformed equation system.
Then $E$ is well-ordered.
\end{lemma}
\@proof We have $\dom(E)=\dom(C)\cap(\mathcal{V}_{t}\cup\mathcal{V}_{f})\cup\{\rho\mid\exists\ell.\rho.\ell\in\dom(C)\mathrel{\mathsf{or}}\exists L.\rho\backslash{L}\in\dom(C)\}$.
The case for type and field variables is as in \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Lemma C.36)},
but uses the fact that $(\tlv(T_{1})\cup\tlv(T_{2}))\cap\dom(E)=(\tlv(T_{1})\cup\tlv(T_{2}))\cap\dom(C)$.
Now, consider $\rho=r_{0}$ with $r_{0}=\boldsymbol{\langle}(\ell=\tau_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}\boldsymbol{\texttt{|}}r\boldsymbol{\rangle}$ obtained
from $(r_{1}\leq\rho\backslash{L}\leq r_{2})\in C$ with
$r=(r_{1}\wedge\rho^{\prime})\vee r_{2}$,
and for all $\ell\in L$ from $(\tau_{\ell}^{1}\leq\rho.\ell^{2})\in C$.
We have $\tlv(r_{0})=\tlv(r)$.
Since $C$ is well-ordered, for all $(\rho_{2}\in\tlv(r_{1})\cup\tlv(r_{2}))\cap\dom(C)$, $O(\rho)<O(\rho_{2})$.
Moreover, $\rho^{\prime}$ is a fresh row variable in $C$, that is $\rho^{\prime}\notin\dom(C)$.
And then $\rho^{\prime}\notin\dom(E)$.
Therefore, $\tlv(r)\cap\dom(E)=(\tlv(r_{1})\cup\tlv(r_{2}))\cap\dom(C)$ and the result follows.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{Solution of Equation Systems}
\par We have now obtained a set of equation-sets $E$, that we must each transform
into a substitution $\sigma$.
We do this in the same way as in \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, \lx@sectionsign 3.2.2)}, but with all kinds
of variables rather than just type variables.
In the set of equations, there is no construction $\rho.\ell$ or $\rho\backslash{L}$ anymore.
We define a function $\Unify(E)$ as $\Unify(\emptyset)=\{\}$, and otherwise:
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@Select in $E$ the equation $X=T$ for the smallest $X$
w.r.t. the order;
\par\enumerate@item@Let $E^{\prime}$ be the set of equations obtained by replacing in
$E{\smallsetminus}\{X=T\}$ every occurrence of $X$ by
$\mu X^{\prime}.(T\{X^{\prime}/X\})$ ($X^{\prime}$ fresh);
\par\enumerate@item@Let $\sigma=\Unify(E^{\prime})$ and return
$\{X=(\mu X^{\prime}.T\{X^{\prime}/X\})\sigma\}\cup\sigma$.
\end{enumerate}
The ordering on the variables guarantees the regularity of the obtained types.
For the elements $\sigma(X)=\mu X^{\prime}.T$ where $X^{\prime}\notin\vars(T)$, we can remove the introduced $\mu$-abstraction.
\par It is straightforward to extend the proofs and definitions.
\begin{definition}Let $E$ be an equation system.
A general solution to $E$ is a substitution $\sigma$ from $\dom(E)$ to
$\mathcal{T}_{\bot}\cup\mathcal{R}$ such that
$\forall X\in\dom(\sigma).\vars(\sigma(X))\cap\dom(\sigma)=\emptyset$ and
$\forall X=T\in E.\sigma(X)\simeq T\sigma$ holds.
\end{definition}
\par\begin{definition}Let $\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}$ be two substitutions.
We say $\sigma\simeq\sigma^{\prime}$ if and only if $\forall X.\sigma(X)\simeq\sigma^{\prime}(X)$.
\end{definition}
\par\begin{proposition}Let $E$ be a well-ordered equation system.
Let $\Unify(E)$ be the procedure described by \cite[citet]{\@@bibref{Authors Phrase1YearPhrase2}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{(}}{\@@citephrase{)}}} to build a
substitution.
\begin{description}\description@item@[Soundness]If $\sigma=\Unify(E)$, then $\sigma\Vdash E$.
\par\description@item@[Completeness]For all substitution $\sigma$, if $\sigma\Vdash E$,
then there exist $\sigma_{0}$ and $\sigma^{\prime}$ such that $\sigma_{0}=\Unify(E)$
and $\sigma\simeq\sigma^{\prime}\circ\sigma_{0}$.
\par\description@item@[Termination]The algorithm $\Unify(E)$ terminates.
\end{description}\end{proposition}
\par The last property we verified is well-formedness.
As in \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{})}, a type is well-formed if and only if the recursion
traverses a constructor, and this property is guaranteed thanks to the order on
variables.
\begin{proposition}If $\sigma=\Unify(E)$, then for all $X\in\dom(\sigma)$,
$\sigma(X)$ is well-formed.
\end{proposition}
\@proof Assume that there exists an ill-formed $\sigma(X)$.
That is, $\sigma(X)=\mu x.t$ where $x$ occurs at the top-level of $t$.
According to the algorithm $\Unify()$, there exists a sequence of equations
$(X=)X_{0}=T_{X_{0}},\dots,X_{n}=T_{X_{n}}$
such that $X_{i}$ is at top-level in $T_{X_{i-1}}$ and $X_{0}$
is at top-level in $T_{X_{n}}$ and where $i\in\{1,\dots,n\}$ and
$n\geq 0$.
We must necessarily have all the $X_{i}$ of the same kind.
Indeed, for type (resp. row) variables only types (resp. row) variables can
appear at top-level.
Now, if $X_{0}$ is a field variable, there can be a type variable
$X_{i}$ at top-level in the field-type $T_{i-1}$.
But then, $X_{0}$ cannot be at top-level in $X_{n}$ since $T_{i}$ is
a type, and field variables cannot appear at top-level in a type.
Since all $X$ and $T$ must be of the same kind and according to
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:wellordered-equations}, we have $O(X_{i-1})<O(X_{i})$
and $O(X_{n})<O(X_{0})$.
Therefore, we have $O(X_{0})<O(X_{1})<\dots<O(X_{n})<O(X_{0})$, which is impossible.
Thus the result follows.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par\@@numbered@section{subsection}{toc}{The Complete Algorithm}
\par The procedure $\mathbf{\mathrm{Sol}}_{\Delta}(C)$ to solve type tallying of a constraint-set $C$
proceeds as follows.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$C$ is normalized into a finite set $\mathcal{S}$ of well-ordered
normalized constraint-sets (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:normalization}).
\par\enumerate@item@Each constraint-set $C_{i}\in\mathcal{S}$ is merged and saturated
into a finite set $\mathcal{S}_{C_{i}}$ of well-ordered
constraint-sets.
Then, all these sets are collected into another set $\mathcal{S}^{\prime}$
({i.e.}, $\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\bigsqcup_{C_{i}\in\mathcal{S}}\mathcal{S}_{C_{i}}$) (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:merging}).
\par\enumerate@item@Each constraint-set $C_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}$ is
harmonized into a finite set $\mathcal{S}_{C_{i}^{\prime}}$ of well-ordered
harmonized
constraint-sets.
Then, all these sets are collected into another set $\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}$
({i.e.}, $\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}=\bigsqcup_{C_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}}\mathcal{S}_{C_{i}^{\prime}}$) (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:harmonization}).
This step is specific to row variables.
\par\enumerate@item@For each constraint-set $C_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}$, we
transform $C_{i}^{\prime\prime}$ into an equation system $E_{i}$ and then construct a general
solution $\sigma_{i}$ from $E_{i}$ (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:solving}).
\par\enumerate@item@Finally, we collect all the solutions $\sigma_{i}$, yielding a set
$\Theta$ of solutions to $C$ (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:solution}).
\end{enumerate}
In the original algorithm for type variables, failing at the step of normalization means
that there is no solution overall, even when increasing the cardinality of the
substitution sets sought by dove-tail order in the general algorithm
described in the beginning of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:tallying} (see \cite[citep]{(\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{castagna15}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, \lx@sectionsign 3.2.3)}).
Whether here a failure in the step of normalization or harmonization means the
absence of a solution overall is still an open question.
\par We write $\mathbf{\mathrm{Sol}}_{\Delta}(C)\rightsquigarrow\Theta$ if $\mathbf{\mathrm{Sol}}_{\Delta}(C)$ terminates with $\Theta$, and we
call $\Theta$ the solution of the type tallying problem for $C$.
\par See \ref{restatable:soundnesstallying}
\@proof Consequence of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:soundness-normalization},
soundness of merging, \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:soundness-harmonization},
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:soundness-solve} and soundness of $\Unify$ (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:unify}).
\qed\end@proof
\par See \ref{restatable:terminationtallying}
\@proof Consequence of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:termination-normalization}, termination of
merging, \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:termination-harmonization}, finiteness of the
constraint-sets and termination of $\Unify$ (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:unify}).
\qed\end@proof
\par See \ref{restatable:finitetallying}
\@proof The first item is a consequence of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:finiteness-normalization},
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:finiteness-harmonization} and finiteness of the constraints after
merging.
The second one is a consequence of \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:wellordered-normalization},
well-orderedness of merging, \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:wellordered-harmonization},
\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:wellordered-solving} and well-orderedness of $\Unify$
(\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{l:unify}).
\qed\end@proof

higher than arrows and records, and negation has the
